"Nick."

"I'll finish after dinner."

Grace held his eyes for a beat, then let it go. She turned to Dan. "The banister's loose again. The one at the top of the stairs."

"I'll look at it Saturday."

"It's been loose since November."

"Then another week won't kill it." Dan said this with half a smile, and Grace shook her head but didn't push. There was a language between them built from years of these small negotiations — what got fixed, what waited, what they pretended not to notice because noticing cost money they didn't have.

Grace stood to clear the plates. As she passed the doorway to the dining room, she stopped. Her hand went to the frame, her thumb finding the groove where the original molding met the casing — a seam you'd only notice if you knew to look for it, if you'd traced it a thousand times as a child, running your fingers along it on your way to a dining room that once held a mahogany table and eight matching chairs and a sideboard with crystal decanters that caught the afternoon light.

"What?" Dan said.

"Nothing." Grace pulled her hand away. "The paint's peeling."

She carried the plates to the sink and ran the water hot.

---

Nick finished his homework at nine — or close enough to call it finished. He brushed his teeth, said goodnight to his parents, and lay in bed with the lights off, listening.

Grace's footsteps in the hallway. Her bedroom door closing. The click of the lamp on her nightstand, and then quiet.

He waited.

Twenty minutes. Maybe thirty. Long enough for the house to decide everyone was asleep.

Then the sound came through the wall. Not from the house — from below it, from the garage, muffled by drywall and floor joists and the particular insulation of a secret kept so long it had become architecture. Guitar. Acoustic, played soft, the notes dampened like someone was cupping the sound with their palm. Nick couldn't make out the song — just the rhythm, steady and slow, a fingerpicking pattern that rose and fell like breathing. Sometimes the player paused, and Nick could almost hear the shift of fingers on the fretboard, the small squeak of callused skin on wound strings. Then the music would start again, patient and unhurried, a conversation between a man and an instrument that happened only when the house was dark.

Dan Johnson playing guitar alone in the garage after his wife went to bed. Nick had heard it for years — since he was nine or ten, since the first night he'd stayed up late enough and the house had gone quiet enough. He'd never mentioned it. He didn't know why. Some things you heard through walls were like prayers — not meant for you, not meant for anyone, and saying them out loud would break whatever made them true.

The music went on for a while. Nick lay still and listened, his sore fingertips pressed against the cool sheet, and the sound came up through the floor and the bedframe and into his chest like a second heartbeat. Outside, the March wind pushed against the windows. Inside, his father played a song that no one was supposed to hear, in a garage that smelled like sawdust and motor oil, in a house where the crown molding was buried under four coats of paint and a woman touched the doorframe every night like she was saying goodbye to a room that used to be hers.

Nick closed his eyes. The riff from "Walk This Way" moved through his fingers against the mattress — pull down, not up, right on the fret, feel that — and somewhere beneath him the guitar kept going, quiet and sure, the sound of a man who'd made his choices and lived with them and still, in the dark, when no one was listening, played.

---

CHAPTER 8: BEFORE THE TALENT SHOW

Three hours and forty-seven minutes left.

I'm sitting here in Studio B, the clock on the wall ticking past two a.m., and behind the glass my engineer — a college kid in a Bruins hoodie who thinks music starts with a laptop — is queued up on Fleetwood Mac's "Go Your Own Way." The opening guitar figure bleeds through the monitors at low volume, Lindsey Buckingham picking at something raw, and I realize I've been feeding you the end of the story without ever serving up the start. The Battle of the Bands in 1979 — that's the big finale, sure, but tonight we need to rewind the tape. Not the first garage jam, not even the friendship between Nick and Jackie, but the instant two best friends looked at a middle school talent show and thought, hell, why not give it a shot?

I take a sip of coffee — gone cold hours ago, but it's fuel either way — and I think about May 15, 1975. The day Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh stopped being two kids who played music in a garage and became a problem for every parent who thought the world came with a seating chart.

May 15, 1975. The annual Ephraim Curtis Middle School Talent Show. Open to all comers, one winner, and a gymnasium full of parents who'd spent the afternoon convincing themselves that their children were special without being too different, talented without being too unusual, competitive without being too ambitious.

Outside the booth windows, Boston glitters in the October darkness, but I'm seeing Sudbury in May, the kind of spring afternoon that makes thirteen-year-olds believe anything is possible.

I need you to understand something about middle school talent shows in suburban Massachusetts in 1975. They weren't about discovering new voices or celebrating anything raw. They were about maintaining the social order — proving that the kids who were supposed to win would win. I spent seven years at WBCN looking for kids who could win a Rumble at The Rat, and the talent show circuit was the opposite of that. The Rat rewarded danger. Talent shows rewarded safety.

I reach for the cassette on my desk, the one labeled "Demo — Nick/Jackie — Not Authorized — May 1979." But tonight I'm not playing that tape. Tonight I'm telling you about the day they decided to make it.

Nick and Jackie had been playing together for almost two years by then. Garage sessions on Dutton Road, acoustic guitars and a keyboard Jackie hauled over on his bicycle, two years of arguing about whether Mercury or Tyler understood something deeper about what a voice could do. They were tight, and they were good, and nobody outside that garage knew it.

The decision happened on a Tuesday afternoon in April. Nick was in the garage, working on a riff he'd caught on WBCN — Zeppelin's "The Ocean," that heavy descending lick he was trying to crack on his acoustic. Jackie sat on Dan's workbench, feet dangling, watching Nick's fingers on the frets.

"We should enter the talent show," Jackie said.

Nick looked up from his guitar. "What talent show?"

"The middle school one. May 15th. Mrs. Reed mentioned it in music class today."

Nick went back to his riff, but the idea was taking hold.

"Why would we do that?"

"Because we're good," Jackie said, and there was something in his voice that surprised both of them. Not bravado. Recognition. "Because we're really good, and nobody knows it except us."

I glance at the console — the old Neve 8068 with its rows of faders and the coffee ring I've been meaning to wipe off since 1986 — and let the moment settle.

That was the hinge. Not when they became friends, not the first garage jam, but when Jackie said "nobody knows it except us" and Nick realized he was right. Proof that nobody sees doesn't change anything. You have to carry it out and let people argue about it.

They spent three weeks preparing. Not practicing — preparing. Translating garage magic into something that would land in a gymnasium full of fluorescent lights, folding chairs, and parents who thought Freddie Mercury was a Roman god.

Their final choice was a mashup — Queen meets Aerosmith, classical training meets garage-band raw power. Jackie's descending Bohemian Rhapsody chords laid across the groove of Nick's Aerosmith riff, the same collision they'd stumbled into the previous October, but tighter now, arranged, with vocal harmonies they'd rehearsed until the garage walls rang. Risky as hell. Mashups demand that the audience understand both songs and recognize that what they're hearing isn't performance but creation. Middle school talent shows aren't built for that kind of risk.

An ambulance wails down Tremont. Behind the glass, my engineer looks up from the mixing board, peering at me the way you watch a pot that might boil over. I crack my knuckles and keep going.

The day of the talent show, Nick and Jackie arrived with their guitars and no expectations beyond proving something to themselves.

Karen Bowers was closing the show — blonde, privately coached, voice like a weapon built to spec. Singing "Tomorrow" from Annie, because of course she was.

The gymnasium was packed. Eight acts. Karen anchored the last slot — the traditional winner's position. Nick and Jackie drew sixth.

The first five acts went the way first five acts always go. A girl did "Feelings" on a recorder. Twin brothers performed a magic trick that ended when the rabbit escaped into the bleachers. A boy in a clip-on tie played "Für Elise" on a piano missing its D above middle C, and every time the melody hit that note the silence was louder than the music.

Then Nick and Jackie walked out.

Two kids. Two acoustic guitars. No clip-on ties, no sheet music, no safety net.

You know, Springsteen tells this story about his first time onstage — turns to the crowd and yells "Hi Mom!" because he didn't know what else to do with the terror. Nick and Jackie didn't have that kind of loose charm yet. They just stood there for a second under the fluorescent lights, two best friends from opposite sides of Hop Brook, and looked at each other. Nick nodded. Jackie counted them in — one, two, three, four — and they hit it.

The Bohemian Rhapsody chords rang out first, Jackie's fingers finding that cathedral sound on the acoustic. Then Nick came in underneath with the Aerosmith groove, that dirty, swinging riff, and the two lines locked the way they'd locked in the garage — Nick's guitar the engine, Jackie's the wingspan. The sound bounced off folding chairs, cinder-block walls, basketball hoops, and came back as something the gymnasium had never held before.

Nick sang in his rough, throaty voice — "sweet emotion" — and Jackie answered with "Is this the real life" in a clear tenor that cut through everything, and the two vocal lines braided and pulled apart and braided again. You could feel the room shift. Not the standing ovation kind. Something quieter and more dangerous. Parents looking at each other. Teachers along the wall no longer whispering. Even the kid whose rabbit was still loose under the bleachers forgot about the rabbit.

They played for four minutes. When they stopped, the silence lasted three full seconds — that airless pause before the gymnasium remembered it was supposed to react.

Then the applause came. Not polite. Not measured. The kind of noise that starts in the gut and doesn't ask permission.

I lean back in my chair and the springs creak. The Fleetwood Mac track has ended and the monitors hum soft static — that warm analog hiss that sounds like the space between songs.

Here's what the judges didn't know how to do with Nick and Jackie. They didn't fit a category. They weren't classical, weren't rock, weren't a novelty act. They were two kids who'd built a sound out of argument and love, and the rubric on the judges' clipboards didn't have a box for that.

Two kids from different sides of Hop Brook won. Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh, as a duo, on a mashup nobody had seen coming. Karen Bowers took second and went into the bathroom to cry and then came out and was gracious about it, the way girls like Karen Bowers had already learned to be gracious at thirteen. But every parent in that gymnasium knew what they had seen — a jolt off-schedule, off-script, a thing the Curtis Middle School Talent Show had not been built to hold.

What mattered wasn't the trophy. What mattered was the four minutes when two kids from different sides of Hop Brook proved that the most threatening thing you can do in a town built on categories is refuse to pick one.

Clock on the wall says I'm running out of night. Out there, Boston sprawls under neon and streetlamp while my voice drifts from this booth to whoever's still tuned in.

But we're not done. We haven't gotten to what happened after — the way the loss hit Nick and Jackie differently, the way it cracked something open the garage sessions had kept sealed. That's next. Stay with me.

---

CHAPTER 9: THE TALENT SHOW

May 15, 1975. Ephraim Curtis Middle School gymnasium. Seven-thirty PM.

The folding chairs had been arranged in rows by Mr. Petrocelli, the custodian, who'd waxed the basketball court that afternoon with the same lemon-industrial compound he used every Thursday, so the whole room smelled like floor polish and the particular sourness that happens when two hundred people sit close together under fluorescent lights on a warm spring evening. The lights hummed at a frequency you couldn't hear so much as feel behind your eyes — a sixty-cycle drone that flattened every color to the same institutional yellow-green. Programs had been photocopied on the school's Xerox machine, the ink still tacky, and parents fanned themselves with them because the gymnasium had no air conditioning and somebody had propped open the fire doors, letting in the May air that smelled like cut grass and warm asphalt and did nothing to help.

Parents sat in their good clothes. Kids fidgeted. Teachers patrolled the aisles like nervous ushers, shushing the sixth-graders who were already throwing wadded-up programs at each other.

Backstage was a storage room that smelled like rubber kickballs and chalk dust. Karen Bowers was in the corner doing vocal exercises, her voice climbing chromatic scales with the mechanical precision of a music box while her mother adjusted the collar of her navy dress for the third time. Karen would sing "Tomorrow" from Annie, the closing act, the slot everyone understood was reserved for the winner.

Nick and Jackie sat on a folding table near the emergency exit, their acoustics resting against their thighs. Nick's was the pawn-shop Fender with the crack in the pickguard. Jackie's was his birthday Gibson, the frets worn smooth in the first position from six months of daily work. They weren't warming up. They were just sitting, the way you sit when you've rehearsed something so many times your hands know it better than your brain does and all that's left is waiting.

"You ready?" Nick asked.

Jackie pressed his thumb across the low E string and released it. A quiet thud. "Yeah."

That was all they said.

In the audience, Dan Johnson sat with his work boots still on, concrete dust in the creases of his jeans. He'd come straight from the job site because Grace told him he was coming. Grace sat beside him in her blue church dress, her hands folded in her lap.

Six rows back, Dr. Richard Marsh sat with Eileen. He wore a gray suit, no tie. He'd come because Jackie asked him to, which was unusual enough that Eileen had noted it without comment. Beside him, Elizabeth was reading the program with the critical eye of someone who'd already found three spelling errors.

The first five acts were what they were. "Für Elise" on a piano tuned a quarter-tone flat. A boy whose card tricks kept slipping. Three girls whose harmony drifted like a radio signal from a distant station. A kid reaching for Jimmy Page and landing somewhere around a campfire in the Berkshires. The audience clapped after each act with the polite enthusiasm of people fulfilling an obligation.

Then Mr. Hennessey, the vice principal, read from an index card. "Next up — Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh."

They walked out carrying their guitars by the necks. No music stands. No sheet music. Two thirteen-year-old boys in jeans and t-shirts — Nick in a faded Aerosmith shirt with a hole near the collar, Jackie in a plain white tee his mother had ironed that afternoon. They sat in two chairs at center stage and adjusted their guitars in their laps. Nick tilted his chair back on two legs the way he always did, then let it drop forward.

They looked at each other. Nick raised his eyebrows a fraction of an inch. Jackie nodded.

Nick's right hand came down on the strings.

The opening riff of "Walk This Way" hit the gymnasium like a sentence in a language the room didn't know it spoke. Not the album version — you couldn't get that from a single acoustic. What Nick played was Joe Perry's riff stripped to its rhythmic bones, all muted downstrokes and hammer-ons, the strings slapping against the frets with a percussive crack that bounced off the cinderblock walls and the waxed floor and came back harder. He played it with his palm half-damping the bridge, so the notes came out short and punchy and mean, more rhythm than melody, the guitar turned into a drum that happened to have pitch.

Four bars in, Jackie entered.

Not with "Walk This Way." With the descending piano chords from "Bohemian Rhapsody," transposed for guitar, fingerpicked — each note placed separately, deliberately, the way you'd set glasses on a table. The chords dropped through the spaces between Nick's riff like water through a grate. Where Nick's guitar was all attack and percussion, Jackie's was sustain and air. The notes hung and opened the sound upward while Nick's riff drove it forward, and for a beat — maybe two — the audience didn't know what they were hearing. Two songs at once. Two worlds in the same room.

Then the two patterns locked.

It happened on the fifth bar. Nick's riff landed on the downbeat and Jackie's chord caught the upbeat, and the syncopation clicked into place like a latch finding its strike plate. The Aerosmith groove gave the Queen harmony a pulse. The Queen harmony gave the Aerosmith groove a cathedral ceiling. Nick was the engine. Jackie was the wingspan. And together the two acoustics produced a sound bigger and stranger than either song alone — a sound that had swagger and sorrow in equal measure, that walked and soared simultaneously, that made you want to move your body and hold your breath at the same time.

The audience stopped moving. Not the polite stillness of attention. The other kind — the kind where the woman three rows back stops fanning herself with her program and doesn't notice, where two hundred people in folding chairs on a waxed gymnasium floor become a single ear.

Nick sang first. The verses of "Walk This Way" in his rough, untrained voice — a voice that cracked on the high notes and didn't care, that sounded like a kid who'd learned to sing by singing along to records in a garage with the door closed. He leaned into the microphone and the words came out hot and a little too loud, the way Aerosmith was supposed to be too loud.

Then Jackie answered. Not the next verse. The "Is this the real life" passage from "Bohemian Rhapsody," his voice clear and placed, each note supported from the diaphragm the way his teacher had drilled into him, a choirboy's instrument carrying Freddie Mercury's question. His tone cut through the room the way a bell cuts through traffic — clean, high, impossible to ignore.

Nick's voice was gravel. Jackie's was glass. One was the street, the other the conservatory. But in the call and response they'd built over five months in Dan Johnson's garage, the gap became the music. Nick threw a line out raw and Jackie caught it and sent it back polished, and then Nick roughed it up again, and each exchange taught the room that these two voices needed each other the way a campfire needs wind — one to burn, one to breathe.

The middle section was where they'd spent the most rehearsal time and it showed. Jackie's guitar shifted into the arpeggiated bridge of "Bohemian Rhapsody" — the operatic section, all ascending figures and dramatic pauses — while Nick wove the "Walk This Way" riff underneath in a lower register, palm-muted to a whisper, just enough to keep the pulse alive. Their voices braided on a harmony they'd invented themselves, not Freddie's and not Tyler's but a third thing, a major third that split the difference between precision and abandon and lived in the crack between them.

Then the crash. Both guitars open-strummed, full volume, both voices rising on the final chorus — Nick's rasp and Jackie's clarity fused into a sound that rattled the folding chairs and buzzed in the fluorescent light casings and hit the back wall and kept going, through the propped-open fire doors, into the May evening, because sound doesn't ask permission and doesn't check the program to see if it's scheduled.

They ended together. A single chord, struck hard, left to ring.

The silence lasted three seconds. Long enough for Nick to glance at Jackie and Jackie to glance at Nick and for something to pass between them that did not need a word.

Grace Johnson pressed both hands to her mouth. Her eyes were wet.

Then the gymnasium broke open. Not applause — noise. Parents standing. Kids yelling. Mr. Hennessey clapping with his hands above his head, which no one had ever seen him do. Mrs. Stephens, the librarian who'd shushed them a thousand times, on her feet with her fist in the air like she was at a concert and not a middle school event in a room that smelled like floor wax.

Six rows back, Dr. Richard Marsh did not stand. He sat very still. His left hand had opened once in his lap, thumb and pinky spanning an octave the hand had not been asked to hold in a very long time, and then it had closed again. Elizabeth was standing on her chair, clapping, her program on the floor.

Karen Bowers sang "Tomorrow." Her voice was flawless. Every note arrived on time, technically irreproachable. The audience clapped when she finished — polite, appreciative, the way you clap for something you admire but that hasn't changed the shape of the room you're sitting in.

The judges deliberated for eleven minutes. Everyone already knew.

"First place — Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh."

They walked back onto the stage grinning, holding their guitars, and the applause hit them again and they stood in it. Dan Johnson was on his feet, work boots on the waxed floor, clapping in the slow hard way of a man who built things with those hands every day and knew solid construction when he heard it. Grace stood beside him, her mascara ruined, laughing and crying at the same time.

Someone had a Polaroid.

The photograph: two thirteen-year-old boys, arms around each other's shoulders, guitars at their sides. Nick's grin enormous, crooked, his Aerosmith shirt riding up. Jackie's smile quieter but no less total, the kind that uses the whole face. Behind them, the banner — EPHRAIM CURTIS MIDDLE SCHOOL ANNUAL TALENT SHOW 1975 — in hand-painted letters on butcher paper, folding chairs askew, programs on the floor.

Dan drove the Johnsons home. Windows down, the May air carrying the smell of lilac from someone's yard on Dutton Road. Nick sat in the back seat holding his guitar case and the first-place ribbon and he couldn't stop talking — the riff, the moment the harmony locked, the look on Karen Bowers' face. Grace reached into the back seat and squeezed his knee, once, and let go. Dan watched the road and said nothing, but in the rearview mirror his eyes were bright.

At the Marsh house, Jackie came through the door still holding the ribbon. Elizabeth said, "You were the best thing that's ever happened in that gym, including basketball," and went upstairs.

Dr. Marsh was in his study. Jackie stood in the doorway.

"Your harmonics were good," his father said quietly. "The voicing in the bridge. That was sophisticated work."

Jackie didn't move. His father had never commented on his rock arrangements. Not once.

"Goodnight, Jackie."

"Goodnight, Dad."

Jackie sat on his bed with the ribbon in his hand and the window open to the May night. From down the street, through the trees, the slow rhythmic tock-tock-tock of someone's sprinkler. He thought about the fifth bar when the riff and the chord locked. He thought about Nick's face when the silence broke. He thought about his father's left hand opening once in his lap and closing again.

He was thirteen years old and it was May and he'd just played the best four minutes of his life in front of everyone he knew and the night air smelled like lilac and cut grass and he was happy. Not careful-happy. Not measured-happy. Not happy-until-five-thirty. Happy the way a chord is happy when it finally resolves — completely, and without asking when it has to leave.

On Dutton Road, Nick lay on his bed with his hands behind his head, still hearing the sound. In the kitchen, Dan and Grace talked in low voices, and he could hear them laughing — Grace's high laugh and Dan's low one, two tones braiding the way two voices will when they've known each other long enough to harmonize without trying.

The Polaroid sat on Nick's desk, still tacky, the colors still deepening. Two boys. Two guitars. The gymnasium behind them already emptying, the folding chairs being stacked, the banner coming down.

But in the photograph, they're still up there. Still grinning. Still thirteen.

May 15, 1975. A Thursday.

---

CHAPTER 9A: MAXANNE'S DISCOVERY

Three hours and nineteen minutes.

The talent show story settles over the booth like the last note of a song nobody wants to follow. Two thirteen-year-olds in a gymnasium, harmonizing like they'd stumbled onto the frequency the rest of us spend our lives scanning for.

I take a pull of the Pappy. Let the burn settle.

"You want to know how I got here?" I say into the microphone. "Not the booth. Not WBCN. I mean *here* — the woman telling you about two kids whose dreams she helped kill. You want to understand how a person goes from rebel to executioner in six years? You've got to go back further than Sudbury. Further than 1979. You've got to go all the way back to a bar in Allston that smelled like spilled beer and ambition, and a twenty-three-year-old who was stupid enough to believe that talent and conviction were the same thing."

I spin the chair toward the album wall. Third row, left side. My hand finds it without looking — Aerosmith's debut, the blue wings on cream, January '73. The vinyl's in pristine condition because I never let anyone else touch it.

"1973. Twenty-three years old. About to find the real thing for the first time."

The memory takes me like a riptide.

---

Tuesday in January. The kind of cold that makes your teeth hurt and your breath hang in the air like tiny ghosts. I drove my Dodge Dart down Commonwealth Ave — heater worked when it felt like it, which was almost never — heading to a bar on Brighton Ave called the Brickyard because a sound engineer named Paulie DeLuca had cornered me at the station that afternoon and grabbed my arm.

"Maxanne, there's a band playing the Brickyard tonight. You need to hear them."

"What kind of band?"

"The singer moves like he's trying to shake demons off his skin. The guitar player looks like he'd kill you and not lose sleep over it. They sound like what you'd get if the Stones and the Yardbirds had a kid and raised it behind a dumpster."

Paulie had seen ten thousand bands and been impressed by maybe six. So I went.

The Brickyard sat between a laundromat and a sub shop, a narrow room with a ceiling so low the tall guys hunched, a stage the size of a dining room table, and a sound system that had been state-of-the-art around the Eisenhower administration. The floor stuck to your shoes. The bathroom door didn't lock. The neon Budweiser sign in the window buzzed like a dying insect. The room held maybe twenty people. Half of them teenagers with fake IDs and nowhere warmer to go. A woman at the bar read a paperback, unbothered by anything. The bartender — a thick-necked guy named Sal — wiped glasses with a rag dirtier than the glasses.

Then five guys walked onto that postage-stamp stage, and the air in the room changed.

The singer first. Twenty-four, rail-thin, lips that belonged on a different species, hair that looked like it had been in a fight with a light socket and lost. He wrapped his hands around the microphone stand like it was the only thing between him and the floor. Didn't say hello. Didn't introduce the band.

He looked at the guitar player — dark-haired, cheekbones sharp enough to cut glass, eyes that said he'd already seen things he wasn't going to share with you — and nodded.

The guitar player hit the opening riff.

I've spent my whole career trying not to sound like a tent-revival preacher, so I'll just say this: the sound that came off that stage was alive the way a downed power line is alive. Dangerous, electric, throwing sparks into the night and not caring who gets burned.

Steven Tyler sang like a man who'd swallowed fire and was coughing up something beautiful before it consumed him. Joe Perry played guitar like each note was a personal argument he intended to win. The bass player laid down grooves that producers would spend thousands trying to replicate. The drummer kept time like a heartbeat in a chest that was running out of reasons.

They played forty-five minutes. Four people left during the set — the woman with the book, a couple who'd been fighting since I walked in, some kid who probably had a curfew. The rest of us stood with our mouths open like we'd forgotten how jaws worked.

I had my portable Panasonic cassette recorder in my bag. I carried one everywhere — the day I talked my way into WBCN I swore I'd never let a sound I believed in go unrecorded. The tape quality was garbage. Tinny, distorted, the Brickyard's acoustics making everything sound like it was being played inside a coffee can. But underneath the noise you could hear it. The signal underneath the static that said *this is real*.

After the set I walked up to the singer. He leaned against the wall by the emergency exit, smoking a Camel, T-shirt soaked through.

"Maxanne Sartori. WBCN."

He looked at me like I'd told him I was from Neptune. "The radio station?"

"The one where I'm going to play your album tomorrow night."

He stared. Then he smiled — this enormous, disbelieving grin that transformed his face from dangerous to heartbreakingly young. "We have an album. Nobody plays it."

"I'm going to play it."

"You're going to play it."

"On WBCN. Tomorrow. Eleven o'clock. If I can find a copy."

He reached into a duffel bag and handed me a record like it was a relic. "Dream On," he said. "If you're only going to play one, play that."

I took the album and walked out into the January cold, and the air tasted different. Cleaner. Like the frequency had been adjusted and I was hearing the world properly for the first time in my life.

---

The next night I walked into WBCN at ten-fifteen. Charlie Morris, the station manager, was still in his office — unusual for Charlie, who believed the night shift ran itself or it didn't deserve to exist. He had his feet on the desk and a joint between his fingers and looked up at me with the tolerant suspicion of a man who'd built his station on the principle that rules were suggestions.

"You've got that look," he said.

"I found something."

"You always find something."

I put the Aerosmith album on his desk. "Track three."

He studied the cover. Studied me. Dropped the needle.

For four and a half minutes, neither of us breathed. When the piano came in, Charlie closed his eyes. When Steven Tyler's voice hit that open-throated scream, Charlie opened his eyes and looked at me like I'd just told him where they buried the treasure.

"Where'd you find this?"

"A bar in Allston. Tuesday night. Twenty people in the room."

Charlie picked up the album. Turned it over. Pointed at me with the hand holding the joint, ash dropping onto the desk. "This is what we DO, Maxanne. Find the real shit. Play it tonight. Eleven. Full volume."

Eleven o'clock, I dropped the needle on "Dream On" without announcing it, without context, without checking with the program director who'd left at six. Steven Tyler's voice pouring out of radios across greater Boston — into dorm rooms at BU and Northeastern, into apartments in Cambridge and Somerville, into cars on the Pike, into a city that didn't know yet what it was hearing.

The request line lit up before the song ended. Every light on the board, blinking like a switchboard plugged into something too powerful for its wiring. I played it again at midnight. Twice more before my shift ended at two. By Wednesday afternoon, kids walked into record stores asking for the album by name. By Friday, it had sold more copies in Boston than in the rest of the country combined.

That was discovery. A woman with a cassette recorder in a half-empty bar, hearing something true and having the nerve to share it.

---

Three months later, a kid walked into Studio C for his first radio interview. Twenty-three, skinny as a parking meter, leather jacket that had seen better decades, a bandana that looked like he'd stolen it from a gas station mechanic. He sat down across from the microphone and his hands shook — not nerves, exactly, but the vibration of a body that contains more energy than it knows what to do with.

"You're Bruce Springsteen," I said.

"That's what they tell me."

"From New Jersey."

"Asbury Park. Born to be anywhere else." He grinned. Wild. Unpracticed.

I leaned into the mic. "WBCN, 104.1. I'm sitting here with Bruce Springsteen, who just released his debut album, *Greetings from Asbury Park, N.J.* Bruce, welcome to Boston."

And this kid — unknown, unpolished, absolutely electric — leaned into the microphone and said, with the kind of pure unguarded joy that fame will burn out of you before you ever learn to protect it:

"This is my first time on the radio. My mom lives out in California. HI MOM!"

The control room cracked up. I cracked up. And somewhere out in California, a woman probably pressed her hand to her mouth and understood that her son was going to be all right.

That was 1973. The year I found Aerosmith in a bar in Allston and Springsteen walked in off the street with nothing but a guitar and his mother's love. The year WBCN was exactly what a radio station is supposed to be.

---

I open my eyes. 2009. Studio B. The ON AIR sign still pulsing its cardiac red.

The album wall stares back at me. Aerosmith. The Cars. Boston. J. Geils. All discovered within spitting distance of this booth.

"That was what we were," I say into the mic. My voice sounds old to me now. When did that happen? "We didn't market. We discovered. We didn't focus-group a goddamn thing. We listened. We put our ears to the ground and when the ground shook, we told people about it. And the ground shook often enough that we were right more than we were wrong, and the times we were right — the Aerosmith nights, the Springsteen mornings — those were the times that justified everything. Every bad call, every sleepless night, every screaming match with management about playlist freedom. Because we'd found the frequency. We knew what real sounded like."

I pick up the demo tape. Turn it in my hands. *Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979.*

"But that was 1973. Six years later I had changed. The woman who walked into a bar in Allston and recorded a band on a Panasonic cassette — she was still inside me somewhere. Buried under six years of learning how the business really worked. Six years of watching the frequency get jammed by consultants and demographic reports and suits who thought music was a product you could optimize rather than a wild animal you could only hope to set free."

I set the tape down. Gently.

"In 1973, I found Aerosmith and my first instinct was to share them with the world. In 1979, I found Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh and my first instinct was to figure out how to use them. I told myself I was still discovering. I told myself the Battle of the Bands was about finding talent. But here's what thirty years of insomnia have taught me: you can lie to management, you can lie to your audience, you can even lie to two seventeen-year-old kids who trust you because you're the woman who broke Aerosmith. The one person you can't lie to forever is the woman staring back at you from the dark window of a radio booth at midnight."

Outside, a bus pulls away from the curb on Boylston, its taillights red as the ON AIR sign.

"Six years. From dropping 'Dream On' unauthorized at full volume to designing a Battle of the Bands with corporate sponsors and demographic targets and a tie clause I buried in the fine print because I thought I could engineer reconciliation the way I'd once engineered discovery."

I lean back. The chair creaks like old leather does — tired, holding on.

"But we're getting ahead of ourselves. Before the Battle, before the rules I wrote in the dark, before the meeting where everything turned — first you need to hear what happened to Nick and Jackie after that talent show. How the golden moment tarnished."

I cue "Dream On." The album cut. The piano intro that still sounds like someone praying in a church they're not sure they believe in.

"Because you can't understand the war until you remember the peace. And the peace between those boys was the most real thing I'd encountered in four decades of rock and roll. What happened to it — what we let happen, what I helped happen — is the reason I'm sitting in this booth, burning the last of my borrowed time."

I press play. Steven Tyler's voice fills Studio B, fills the airwaves, fills whatever's left of a city that once believed a sound from a dive bar could change the world.

I close my eyes and let the music say what I can't.

**END CHAPTER 9A**


CHAPTER 10: CAMP ELBANOBSCOT

The bus smelled like diesel and bug spray and the vinyl seats were hot enough to brand the backs of your legs if you sat down wrong. Nick had the window. Jackie had the aisle. Their guitars rode between them, necks pointed toward the ceiling like they knew where they were going even if the boys didn't.

Camp Elbanobscot sat on forty acres of Sudbury conservation land between Route 20 and the reservoir, an outdoor camp that for some odd reason specialized in music. Not archery, not sailing, not the kind of camp where rich kids learned tennis and poor kids learned they couldn't afford to learn tennis. Music. Guitars and keyboards and voices carrying through white pines that had been dropping sap on everything since before the Revolution—your shoes, your sleeping bag, the fretboard of your guitar if you left it leaning against the wrong tree. The sap got into your fingertips and stayed there, tacky and sweet-smelling, so that for weeks after camp ended you'd catch a whiff of pine resin on your hands and feel the ghost of those two weeks rise up through your chest like a chord you'd forgotten you knew.

The cabins were screen-walled, which meant the mosquitoes had open invitations and the music had no walls. You could lie in your bunk at eleven and hear someone three cabins over working through a chord change, the notes drifting across the compound with the pine smell and the lake damp, tangling with the peepers and the occasional bang of a screen door. By morning the grass was silver with dew and your sneakers were soaked before you reached the dining hall, where the coffee was for counselors only but the smell of it mixed with woodsmoke from the breakfast fire pit and made you feel older than thirteen.

They arrived on a Sunday in late July. Nick and Jackie claimed bunks on the same wall of Cabin 7, guitars propped against the pine planks, and by dinnertime they'd mapped the place. The lake was a ten-minute walk through birch trees, down a path worn to bare dirt by twenty summers of campers. The music building was a converted barn with a piano that stayed almost in tune and acoustics that made everything sound better than it was. The practice meadow sat on a slope above the water, and if you played there at sunset the sound carried down across the lake and came back to you a half-second later, softened.

That first night, lying in their bunks while the cabin settled into the sounds of eight boys breathing and one boy quietly fingering chord shapes on an unplugged electric, Nick said, "This is different."

Jackie didn't ask what he meant. He knew. At Elbanobscot, nobody's last name meant anything. Nobody knew that Nick's house had a mortgage three months behind or that Jackie's father had opinions about rock and roll that could freeze a room. Here the only currency was whether you could play, and both of them were rich.

"Yeah," Jackie said. "It is."

The next morning they met Patty Lally.

She walked into the dining hall during breakfast and the room shifted. Not dramatically—nobody dropped a fork or stopped talking. But the air moved. She was thirteen, tall and lanky, with straight wheat-colored hair that hung past her shoulders. She was barefoot. Her feet were brown with dirt and she didn't seem to notice or care, and she carried a canvas bag slung across one shoulder with a songbook sticking out of it, pages flagged with torn strips of newspaper.

She smelled like something. Not perfume—the opposite of perfume. Something faintly animal and earthy that the other kids couldn't quite place, a smell that lived in her jacket and her hair and the leather strap of her bag. Pig farm. Cavicchio's, southeast Sudbury, where her family's property backed up against the hog operation and the smell got into everything—curtains, school notebooks, the seats of the car. Patty had grown up breathing it. She never apologized for it because she'd never been taught that the place you came from was something to apologize for.

She slid her tray onto the table across from Nick and Jackie and sat down like she'd been sitting there all summer.

"You two are in the intensive, right?" she said. Not a question, really. A confirmation.

Nick nodded. "Guitar. I'm Nick. This is Jackie."

"Piano and guitar," Jackie added.

"I know who you are." She bit into an apple, chewed, kept talking. Her hands on the apple were small and strong and the nails were cut straight across, and at the base of her thumb there was a faint permanent stain the color of tobacco, and a white scar crossed one knuckle like a crease ironed in; Nick noticed and didn't ask. "Talent show. 'Walk This Way' folded into 'Bohemian Rhapsody.' You made Karen Bowers cry in the bathroom afterward. That was the best thing I've ever seen at Curtis."

"You go to Curtis?" Nick said, and felt immediately stupid, because of course she did. She'd been in the hallways, in the cafeteria, at assemblies, a presence he'd walked past a hundred times without registering. But that was before. Before this summer. Before whatever was happening to his brain that made girls suddenly visible in a way they hadn't been six months ago.

"Curtis," Patty said. "Same as you. We had bio together last semester. I sat two rows behind you." She looked at Nick with a level gaze that had no accusation in it, just amusement. "You never turned around once."

Nick felt heat climb his neck. Jackie was very quiet beside him, holding his milk carton with both hands.

"I play piano," Patty said. "And I sing. And I hum constantly, which drives everyone insane." As if to prove it, she started humming something—a melody that moved in a way Nick couldn't quite track, rising and turning back on itself, restless and certain at the same time. He didn't recognize it. It sounded like it belonged to her, like she'd carried it out of a song the rest of the world hadn't written yet.

She stopped humming and looked at both of them. "So what are we working on?"

We. As if the three of them had already agreed on something.

---

Over the next five days they became the thing the other campers talked about. Not because they were the best musicians at Elbanobscot—there was a cellist from Newton who could make you cry and a drummer from Framingham who played like he had six arms—but because something happened when the three of them played together.

They found it on the third afternoon, in the practice meadow above the lake.

It had been ninety-two degrees at noon. The thunderheads had built up over the reservoir around three and spent themselves in a hard twenty-minute rain that knocked the pollen down and dropped the air ten degrees, and by four-thirty the sky was washed blue and the grass was steaming and everything smelled like wet pine and electricity.

Nick was noodling on his guitar, working through a progression he'd stolen from Joe Perry and bent into something his own fingers preferred. Jackie sat at the portable keyboard the camp kept under a tarp, adding chords underneath—not copying, not competing, but building a floor for Nick's line to stand on, the way he always did. They'd done this before, the two of them. It was good. It was always good.

Then Patty started singing.

She didn't ask what key they were in. She didn't wait for a verse or a chorus. She just opened her mouth and a voice came out that was lower than you'd expect from a girl that thin, with a grain in it like fine sandpaper, a texture that caught on the melody and held it. She sang without words at first—just vowel sounds, aahs and oohs that wove between Nick's guitar and Jackie's piano like a third rope braiding into a cord. Her voice found the spaces the instruments left open and filled them without crowding, and when she finally landed on a harmony it locked into place with a physical click, like a door latch falling home.

Nick stopped playing. Jackie stopped playing. The silence that followed had a different shape than the silence before—fuller, charged, like the air after lightning.

"Don't stop," Patty said. "Play it again."

They played it again. But before the instruments came back in, she took a breath and nodded at them both, and what they found on the second pass, when all three of them opened their mouths on the same held breath, was a plain C-major triad a cappella for one beat: Nick's root underneath, Jackie's G above, Patty's E clean and unshowy on top, and she laughed.

The laugh was short and surprised and belonged to no one. It belonged to the chord.

Then the guitar and the keyboard came back under it and this time all three of them leaned into the song—Nick driving the rhythm harder, Jackie pushing the chords into unexpected territory, Patty's voice climbing above both of them and then diving underneath, finding notes that shouldn't have worked but did because her ear was tuned to something the other two couldn't hear on their own. The sound carried down the slope and across the lake, and the lake sent it back, and for a few minutes the meadow held all three of them inside a single resonance that felt less like performing and more like breathing.

The sun was going down. The lake had turned the color of copper, and the tree line on the far shore was black against a sky that was moving from orange to violet in bands so distinct they looked painted. The light caught Patty's hair and turned it gold, and Nick felt something tighten behind his ribs that had nothing to do with music and everything to do with the way she tilted her head when she sang, eyes half-closed, like she was listening to a version of the song that existed somewhere ahead of them in time.

He looked at Jackie. Jackie was watching Patty too.

Not the way you watch a musician. The way you watch a person who has just changed the terms of your life without asking permission. Jackie's hands were still on the keys but he wasn't playing. He was just looking at her, and his face held an expression Nick had never seen on him before—open, unguarded, the careful Marsh composure cracked just enough to let something raw show through.

Nick looked away. He looked at the lake, at the sky, at his own hands on the guitar strings.

"Again," Patty said, and grinned.

They played until the mosquitoes drove them inside. They played in the music building after dinner, working through songs they all knew—Patty leading them into Fleetwood Mac, Nick pulling them toward Aerosmith, Jackie insisting on Queen, and the arguments becoming their own kind of music, a call-and-response that generated more heat than friction.

---

The night they found the chord, Nick couldn't sleep.

Cabin 7 had gone quiet by eleven the way it always did—eight other boys in their bunks, breath settling into the loose unison of camp fatigue, the pine-board walls ticking as the day's heat let go of the metal roof. Somewhere out past the reservoir a whip-poor-will was calling its own name on repeat, patient and mechanical, the way it does for two hours at a stretch until you stop noticing and then start noticing again. A bullfrog answered from the reedy end of the lake. Someone—Cabin 9 or Cabin 10, too far to tell—was playing a guitar under a blanket, the chords muffled and thumb-shaped, a kid trying to finger-pick "Blackbird" and getting most of it right. Outside the screen, fireflies worked the tall grass in slow, distracted pulses.

The boy two bunks down from Nick snored like a tractor. Not like a snore. Like a tractor—the low hitch and diesel catch of an engine trying to turn over in cold weather, once, twice, then the long roll.

"You awake?" Jackie's voice came down from the bunk across the aisle, pitched so it wouldn't carry over the tractor.

"Yeah."

A pause. Then Jackie said, "There's sheet music in our attic."

Nick rolled onto his side so he was facing the voice. "Yours?"

"My father's. Boxes of it. Piano." The word landed with the weight of a thing Jackie had not said out loud before. "I found it last year looking for skis. He doesn't know I saw."

"He played?"

"He played." Jackie was quiet a minute. "He doesn't anymore. The Baldwin in the den hasn't been tuned since before I was born."

Nick thought about that. About Dr. Marsh coming home from the hospital at seven and reading a journal in the wing chair and not looking at the piano that sat in the same room with him. About the sheet music in its boxes, upstairs, getting colder every winter.

"My dad's got a guitar in the garage," Nick said. "In his toolbox. I'm not supposed to know."

"You opened it?"

"No."

The tractor-boy snored. The whip-poor-will kept its count.

"I don't know what I'm going to do after high school," Nick said. It came out before he'd decided to say it, which was how most of the true things he'd ever said had come out. "I don't know if I can do anything."

"You can play."

"Yeah."

"That's something."

"Is it?"

Jackie didn't answer right away. The kid with the blanket guitar had moved on to something Nick didn't know—a slow descending line, almost a lullaby, the low E string buzzing a little where it needed a new set.

"It's what we've got," Jackie said finally.

Somewhere out beyond Cabin 7 the lake did whatever lakes do at midnight. The screen door of the counselors' cabin banged once and didn't bang again. Nick lay on his back and listened to eight other boys breathing and the whip-poor-will counting and his best friend not sleeping.

He thought about Patty's E on top of the triad. He thought about her hands.

He was asleep before he could think about anything else.

---

They played on the last night of camp, sitting on the dock with their feet in the black water, Patty humming that same restless melody while Nick picked a quiet line on his acoustic and Jackie added soft chords on a guitar he'd borrowed from the camp supply closet because the keyboard wouldn't survive the dew.

Three voices. Three instruments. The sound moving out across the water in the dark, mixing with the loon calls and the lap of small waves against the dock pilings, and if you were lying in your bunk in Cabin 4 or Cabin 9, half-asleep, you would have heard it and thought it was one voice, one instrument, one song.

The last morning, waiting for the bus, Patty wrote her phone number on the back of a camp schedule and tore it in half. She gave one half to Nick and one half to Jackie.

"You'll have to talk to each other to get the whole number," she said.

Nick laughed. Jackie laughed. The bus came. Patty climbed on first, bare feet on the rubber-matted steps, her canvas bag over her shoulder, humming.

Nick watched her go.

He climbed on the bus. Jackie climbed on behind him. They sat in the same seats they'd taken on the way up, guitars between them, and neither of them mentioned Patty for the entire ride home.

**END CHAPTER 10**


CHAPTER 11: THE FIRST FRACTURE

Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School. September 1976.

The cafeteria hit you before you saw it. Fryer grease and industrial tomato sauce and the sweet chemical fog of whatever they sprayed on the tables between periods — a smell that got into your clothes and stayed there, so your mother would sniff your jacket that evening and say you smelled like school, which was another way of saying you smelled like everyone and no one. The noise was a wall. Three hundred kids talking at once produced a sound that had no words in it, just a continuous roar like a river heard from a distance, punctuated by the crack of lunch trays hitting formica and the occasional shriek of a chair leg dragged across linoleum. The fluorescent lights buzzed at a frequency that lived behind your teeth.

Nick and Jackie walked in together that first Monday, carrying brown bags because the hot lunch line was forty minutes long and neither of them knew the system yet. They stood just inside the double doors, scanning the room the way you scan a lake before you swim — looking for the rocks under the surface, the places you can't see until you're already in.

The room had its own geography.

The Rampers owned the raised platform at the back — six tables on a section of floor eight inches higher than everything else, which was all it took. They sat with their textbooks stacked, spiral notebooks open, highlighters uncapped and organized by color. One girl had a day planner. It was September. She was already marking dates in November.

The Rats had the glass corridor, the breezeway between the cafeteria and the arts wing, where the afternoon light came through dirty windows and turned everything the color of an old photograph. Someone was always rolling a cigarette. Someone was always sketching in a composition notebook. A boy with safety pins through his jacket collar was reading a paperback with no cover.

The Regs filled the middle — the big undifferentiated mass of tables where you sat if you didn't sit anywhere in particular. Loud, safe, forgettable. The center of the room and the center of the bell curve and they knew it and that was fine.

The Rollers had the two tables nearest the door to the auto shop corridor, and you could smell them before you saw them — motor oil and Brakleen and the metallic sweetness of fresh-cut steel. Their hands were stained in the creases the way Nick's father's hands were stained, the kind of clean that soap can get to and the kind it can't. They ate fast. Lunch was what happened between engines.

Nick and Jackie didn't know any of this yet. They just knew the room was enormous and loud and that they needed to sit down somewhere before the standing itself became a statement.

"There," Jackie said, and pointed with his chin toward a half-empty table in the middle section, and they sat, and for the first two weeks that was the arrangement. Nick and Jackie at a Regs table near the water fountain, eating lunch, talking about the band they were going to start, about Patty's voice, about whether they could book a practice space at the community center on Route 20. The cafeteria roared around them and they were an island in it, the same way the garage had been an island, the same way the practice meadow at Elbanobscot had been an island — a pocket of air where the two of them made sense together and the outside categories didn't apply.

It lasted fourteen days.

On the Monday of the third week, Nick walked into the cafeteria and Jackie wasn't at their table. Nick scanned the room — the Regs section, the corridor, the raised platform — and found him. Jackie was sitting up on the Rampers' section, at the second table from the wall, next to a kid in a polo shirt who was showing him something in a textbook. Jackie had his notebook open. He was laughing.

The distance between their old table and the platform was maybe forty feet. Nick could have crossed it in ten seconds. He stood holding his brown bag and looked at the forty feet and felt something shift in his stomach, a small cold drop, like the moment a roller coaster crests the first hill and you realize gravity has been waiting for you.

He didn't cross the forty feet. He turned and walked to the Rollers' tables near the shop corridor, where Carl Marquis was eating a sandwich with hands that had black grease worked into the knuckles. Carl looked up. Nick sat down. Carl went back to his sandwich. That was the whole welcome. No questions, no audition. You sat or you didn't.

"You got shop second period?" Carl asked after a while.

"Third."

"Harrington?"

"Yeah."

Carl nodded. The conversation was over and it was enough.

Nick ate his lunch. The sandwich tasted like nothing. Across the room, on the platform, Jackie was still talking to the kid in the polo shirt — David Bellenghi, though Nick didn't know the name yet. Jackie's back was angled slightly away from the main floor. Not fully turned. Just angled. The way you angle away from a window when the light's too bright, without deciding to, without even knowing you're doing it.

Jackie didn't look for Nick. That was the thing. He didn't scan the room. He didn't check the table by the water fountain. He was up there and he was fine up there and it hadn't occurred to him that Nick might be looking for him the way a dog looks for the other dog it walked in with.

Or maybe it had occurred to him and he'd chosen not to look. Nick couldn't tell, and in a way that was worse — the not knowing whether it was careless or careful. Both options left him sitting at a table that smelled like motor oil, eating a sandwich he couldn't taste, forty feet from his best friend in a room so loud that even if he'd shouted Jackie's name it would have dissolved before it got there.

Across the cafeteria, near the milk coolers, a girl sat alone at the end of a Regs table. Wheat-colored hair, canvas bag, bare feet tucked under the bench even though the linoleum was cold. Patty Lally ate an apple and watched the room. She saw Nick at the Rollers' table. She saw Jackie on the platform. She saw the forty feet between them, and she saw what it meant, because Patty had grown up at the margin of things — pig-farm girl in a subdivision town — and margins gave you sight lines the center didn't have.

She didn't say anything. She didn't wave. She bit her apple and watched, and something behind her eyes was doing math — not the kind with numbers, the kind where you count the distance between two people who don't know they're moving apart and calculate how long before they notice.

That night, in the garage on Dutton Road, Jackie showed up with his guitar at six-thirty as if nothing had changed. Maybe for him nothing had. He tuned up, ran through a chord progression, waited for Nick to come in on top of it the way Nick always did.

Nick played. The music worked the way it always worked — his raw line and Jackie's architecture, the engine and the wingspan. They ran through three songs and the garage filled with sound and the sound was right.

But in the gaps between songs, in the silences where they used to talk, something was different. The silence had a texture now. It was the silence of two people not mentioning something, which is louder than the silence of two people with nothing to say.

"Good session," Jackie said when they finished, and he said it the way you say good session when you're confirming that the session was good and nothing else, when you're closing a door on everything the session didn't include — like why you were sitting on a platform eight inches above your best friend at lunch today, or why you didn't look for him, or why you laughed so easily with a kid whose name he doesn't know.

"Yeah," Nick said. "Good session."

Jackie packed up his guitar and rode his bike home through the September dark, up Dutton Road toward the north side where the lawns got wider and the porch lights were the kind that turned on automatically at dusk. Nick stood in the garage doorway and watched the red reflector on Jackie's rear fender shrink until it was just a point of light and then nothing.

He went inside. His mother was at the kitchen table, paying bills with the calculator that printed on the little paper tape, the sound of it filling the room — click, whirr, click — like a mechanical heartbeat counting down something. His father's work boots stood by the door, the leather cracked, dried plaster in the treads.

Nick went to his room. He picked up his guitar and played nothing — just held it, the body warm against his ribs, and thought about forty feet of linoleum and the sound of three hundred people between him and the kid who used to sit next to him.

It wasn't a fight. It wasn't even a disagreement. It was a Monday in September, a cafeteria, a brown bag lunch, and the discovery that forty feet can open under your friendship like a crack in a sidewalk — so thin you step over it without looking down, so thin you could tell yourself it was always there, so thin that you won't understand it was the beginning of anything until years later, when the crack has become a canyon and you're standing on one side trying to remember when the ground was whole.

Jackie probably didn't even notice.

Outside, September cooled toward October. The leaves on Dutton Road were just starting to turn, the green going pale at the edges, not yet the orange and red that would come but already not what they were — changing so slowly that if you watched them every day you'd swear nothing was different, and then one morning you'd look up and the whole street would be on fire and you'd wonder how you missed it.

---


CHAPTER 11A: THE FOUR QUADRANTS

April in Sudbury was all mud. Hop Brook ran brown under the Route 117 bridge, loud enough to hear from the Lincoln-Sudbury front walk, and the lawn in front of the school had gone to the color of a boot print that had filled with water and frozen and thawed a dozen times since March. The parking lot was a lattice of tire ruts. A kid came up the walk, stepped wrong, went in past the ankle of his sneaker, said a word his mother wouldn't like, pulled his foot out with a sucking sound, and kept walking. Nobody looked. April.

Seven-forty-five. First bell in four.

Carl Marquis's '66 Impala came in off Lincoln Road from the south, throwing gravel, the muffler louder than it had been yesterday. Nick was in the passenger seat, book bag between his feet, a quarter-eaten Hostess donut in his hand, his hair still wet from the shower he'd taken at six-thirty. The radio was on an AM station playing something neither good nor bad — noise to fill the seven miles from Marquis's driveway to the school lot. The speaker in the driver's door buzzed on the high notes. It had buzzed since Carl and his father put the dash back together in November. It was just the sound the car made now.

Nick got out. He slammed the door the way you slammed the door of a car like that — a second slam, because the first one never caught.

Up the walk, twenty yards away, a wood-paneled Buick Estate wagon pulled up to the curb. The wagon was always clean. Dr. Richard Marsh had Saturday-morning habits about wax and a chamois, and it showed. Jackie got out of the passenger side, closed the door with the small respectful click a kid closed a clean car with, and lifted his hand once to the driver's-side window without looking at his father's face. The Buick pulled away. Jackie stood on the curb a beat longer than he had to, adjusting the strap of his book bag, then moved up the walk.

His loafers were polished. The bottoms of his jeans were an inch dark with Sudbury mud because he had walked across the lawn yesterday to save thirty seconds. He noticed it now, the small pull of dried mud against the denim at every step.

Nick was fifteen yards to his left, coming in from the parking lot at a diagonal. Nick saw him. His eyes went to Jackie the way a dog's head turned when another dog walked by — before the thinking got there. Then Nick looked down at his own boots, at the donut in his hand, at the cigarette butt somebody had ground out in the gravel by his toe, and kept walking. His line and Jackie's line converged toward the same set of double doors and stopped converging eight feet before the doors, because Jackie went toward the right-hand door and Nick went toward the left, and they passed through at the same moment through doors hinged at different walls, and neither one looked at the other, and then they were inside and the hallway noise took them and they went in separate directions without a sentence between them.

The bell rang. In the window of the front office, a secretary picked up a rotary phone and began to dial.

---

Third period. Bay three.

The auto shop sat at the far end of the east wing — cinderblock walls painted the color of instant mashed potatoes, corrugated steel doors that rolled up on greasy chains, a concrete floor that sloped a half-inch toward a drain Mr. Harrington had been meaning to rod out for six years. Bay one was lecture. Bays two and three were alive. Bay two had a '68 Impala up on jack stands, hood yawning open, radiator pulled. Bay three had Nick's project and Carl's — a small-block Chevy 327 on an engine stand, heads off, the valve train laid out on cardboard in the order the parts had come out.

The shop smelled like Brakleen and hot metal and the gritty orange hand-soap in the gallon pump by the wash sink. A transistor radio sat on the bench by the compressor, tuned to WBCN, low enough that Harrington wouldn't make them shut it off. Big Mattress was long over. A woman's voice was on the air, for the last days it would be, though the boys in bay three didn't know that and wouldn't have made anything of it if they did.

Nick had a socket wrench and a head bolt that had decided it didn't want to come. He leaned into it. His wrists were oil-dark to the cuffs, and there was a smear of grease on his cheekbone from where he had scratched a mosquito bite an hour ago without thinking about his hands first. Carl was across the engine stand with a torque spec sheet in one hand and a cup of shop coffee in the other. The coffee was bad. Everybody said the coffee was bad. Everybody drank it.

"Break or lean on it," Carl said.

"I'm leaning on it."

"Lean harder. It's a bolt, not your grandmother."

Nick leaned. The bolt went a quarter turn and then another and then it broke free and came out in his hand clean, and he set it on the cardboard in its place in the line, and Carl made a small approving sound and went back to the torque sheet.

The door from the corridor opened and Anthony Rossi came through — Ant, to everyone — a junior who was not supposed to be in the shop during third period because he had study hall in the cafeteria. He had a folded green hall pass in his fingers and a face that said he had no intention of explaining himself to Harrington because Harrington had stopped asking. He came up the aisle, hands in the pockets of a Raytheon-blue work jacket that had been his uncle's before it was his, and stopped at the engine stand to look at the heads on the bench.

"You doing the valves today?"

"Reseating," Carl said.

"Harrington letting you grind em?"

"He said he'd do it. He's not doing it. So it's gonna be me doing it and him telling me I did it wrong."

Ant laughed — short, no teeth, the laugh of a kid who had been the youngest in a loud house and had learned to fit his amusement into other people's sentences. He owned, with his brother Frankie, a 1970 Nova that lived in the driveway of a split-level on Route 20 behind the sheet-metal shop where his father was foreman. The Nova had a rebuilt 350 and rust through the back quarter panels and a bench seat his mother had reupholstered herself in brown vinyl from a roll she'd found at a yard sale in Marlborough. He knew a carburetor the way a piano kid knew a scale. He was going to work at his uncle's garage on Boston Post Road when he graduated, and his family already knew it, and Ant had been fine with it since he was twelve.

"Friday night," Ant said. "We going or we not going?"

"Going where."

"Kevin said the trails. His brother's got that Yamaha. Ride til dark and then Store 24."

"I told my mother I'd be home by nine."

"Why."

"Cause I told her I would."

"You heard about Gary."

"Gary who."

"Gary Hagan. Broke his collarbone on the trails last weekend. Went over the handlebars on the big hill behind the transformer. His mother told him he wasn't gonna play JV baseball this spring, and he cried. Sixteen-year-old kid, crying at the kitchen table over JV baseball."

"Jesus," Nick said.

"Yeah. So — Friday night. Trails. But carefully."

"Carefully," Carl said, and made the word sound like the opposite of itself.

Harrington came out from his office at the end of bay one, coveralls oil-black at the knees and pockets the way they had been for thirty years, a cigarette tucked behind his ear he wasn't going to smoke until lunch. He gave Ant a look that was not a question so much as a timer. Ant pushed off the bench, folded the hall pass a little tighter, and started down the aisle.

"Nine o'clock," Ant called back.

"Nine o'clock," Nick said, and did not look up.

When the door closed, Carl tilted his head toward the engine. "Your mother's gonna let you out at nine."

"My mother's working the four-to-close at Friendly's. I'm letting myself out at nine."

Carl grinned. The grin stayed on the torque sheet and didn't find Nick's face.

The '66 Chevelle hood leaning against the wall gave back the radiator's clank as a faint metal shiver. Primer grey. A pencil mark on the fender where the paint guy had measured for the body line. The wall clock clicked up another minute, and on the radio the woman said something about a record Aerosmith had coming in the fall, and the shop went back to its own sound.

---

Glass Corridor. Fourth period. Lunch A.

Lenny Henderson was sixteen and had been smoking for fourteen months, still in the phase where he was proud of it and not yet in the phase where he was tired of it. He sat on the floor of the Glass Corridor with his back against the brick dado wall, legs out straight, a paperback copy of *The Stranger* open on his thigh. The paperback had no cover. It had come off on the T in February when he'd stuffed the book in his jacket pocket with a roll of dimes and sat on it for forty minutes, and now it was just pages and a bent spine, which he preferred.

The Corridor ran twenty yards between the cafeteria and the arts wing, dirty glass on the south side and brick on the north, and the two o'clock April sun came through at an angle that turned the smoke into a physical thing — a column of soft yellow light in the middle of the hallway, ash motes moving through it the way plankton moves through a shaft of undersea sun. An ashtray on a metal stand at the cafeteria end, chrome top, overfilled. The custodian emptied it Monday.

Lenny was rolling a cigarette one-handed because he could. Left hand holding the book, right hand with the paper between the first two knuckles, the Top tobacco tin balanced on his thigh, and he shook tobacco into the paper with a tilt that was almost elegant. He rolled, licked, sealed, tucked the cigarette behind his ear next to a pencil.

Two dado sections down, Maggie Doherty sat with her knees up and her head against the glass. Her leather jacket was cracked at the elbows, bought from a cousin in Dorchester last summer for twenty dollars. She was writing in a composition book with a ballpoint pen that kept skipping. Farther down, a junior named Walt was reading a paperback Dostoevsky in worse shape than Lenny's Camus. The three of them weren't talking. The point of the Corridor was you could be with other people without having to perform being with other people. The smoke did the work.

A chemistry teacher came through from the cafeteria end carrying a stack of lab reports, on her way to the arts wing. She walked past the ashtray, past Lenny, past Maggie, past Walt, without turning her head. Heels on waxed linoleum. She was gone through the far door in seven seconds. The ashes from her passing shifted in the yellow sunlight and resettled. Lenny took the cigarette from behind his ear and lit it with a Zippo that had been his grandfather's. He drew, held, let it out toward the glass. The glass fogged a square of itself and cleared.

Lenny Henderson lived on Goodman's Hill Road in a five-bedroom colonial with a flagstone walk and a tennis court behind the garage. His father was a dermatologist with an office in Newton and a second house on the Cape. The tennis court had been resurfaced in October. Lenny had not played on it since he was eleven, when he had gone one day without being told to and beaten his father in three sets, and his father had smiled and hugged him and then never asked him to play again.

Nobody in the Corridor knew any of this. Maggie had been to Lenny's house exactly once, the previous October, on a Friday when the family was in Chatham, and Lenny had taken her out the back slider to the court and they had stood on the baseline in the dark with their hands in their pockets and Maggie had said *is this yours* and Lenny had said *it's the house's* and they had gone back inside. That was as close as any Rat had come to the Henderson foyer. Lenny had done the math on what it would cost him in the Corridor if Goodman's Hill were known, and he had decided the Corridor was the first thing in his life he had chosen, and he was not going to let Goodman's Hill take it from him.

"What time is Hagan home," Maggie said without looking up.

"I heard four."

"His mom letting anyone see him."

"His mom is mad at the trails, at the Yamaha, at Kevin, at the transformer, and at the state of Massachusetts for building a dirt road behind her house."

Maggie laughed without moving. "Tell him to call me."

"He can't hold a phone, Mag. He broke his collarbone."

"He's got another arm."

Lenny smiled. He hadn't smiled in twenty minutes. "I'll tell him."

The fire alarm at the far end of the Corridor was behind a glass panel with a little red hammer attached to a chain. Someone had set it off in December and nobody had ever proved it. The kids in the Corridor knew who it had been. The kids in the Corridor were not going to say. Lenny let smoke out toward the glass square he had fogged before, and the smoke moved into the sunbeam, and Camus lay open on his thigh at a page he had stopped reading because the sentence had been good enough that he wanted to hold it for a minute.

---

Raised platform. Same period.

The Rampers' tables sat eight inches above the main floor on a section of poured concrete some architect in 1960 had thought would be used for public speaking. Six long tables. Folding chairs. A sight line of the whole cafeteria that nobody on the platform used except to check whether the hot lunch line was long.

Jackie sat at the second table from the wall with his brown bag open in front of him. Inside — his mother had packed it at six-fifteen — was a chicken salad sandwich on wheat with the crusts cut off, a peeled orange in a baggie segment by segment, two Pepperidge Farm Milanos in a napkin folded into a tight square. His mother was like that. His mother would be like that forever.

Kathy Bellenghi was three chairs down showing her day planner to a sophomore named Brooke, saying *the Junior Prom is literally six weeks away*, the third time in the lunch period she had used the word *literally* incorrectly and the fourth time today, and Jackie had started to count out of fondness more than irritation. Her highlighters were lined up on the formica in the order of the colors of the rainbow. She was filling a square on the sixth of May with pink. The prom.

Across from Jackie, Donnie Rossi was eating a meatball sub. Three sports this year — soccer, basketball, baseball — and the knuckle-scrape on his right hand from a headfirst slide on Feeley's mud Wednesday. The sub came from his uncle's deli off Concord Road, wrapped in white butcher paper saturated with sauce and sitting on an open section of the *Boston Globe* sports page so the sauce wouldn't stain the formica. Donnie did everything fast. His dress shirt had been ironed on Sunday night by a mother who woke at five-thirty six days a week to work the breakfast shift at the Friendly's on Route 20, and by Friday the shirt was a different object from the one that had come off the board — cuff-rolled, collar limp, a stripe of sauce at the second button.

"So, Doctor," Donnie said. He had called Jackie Doctor since the first week of September, and Jackie had stopped trying to make him stop. "What's the answer."

"The answer to what."

"Am I gonna ace the calc quiz sixth period."

"Did you study."

"I studied enough."

"Then you're gonna do fine."

"That's a non-answer. Give me an expert opinion."

Jackie set down his sandwich. "Expert opinion. You're gonna get a B-minus. You're gonna tell your mother you got a B-plus. She's gonna know you're lying but not make you take it back because she wants you to feel good about it."

"See," Donnie said to Kathy, who wasn't listening. "This is why I talk to the Doctor. He knows my mother."

"Everybody knows your mother, Donnie," Kathy said without looking up. "Your mother works the counter at Friendly's. I had pancakes from your mother's hand on Sunday morning."

"And were they good pancakes."

"They were fantastic pancakes."

"That's all I need to hear."

He went back to the sub. Jackie watched him a beat longer than he meant to. Donnie's right cuff had sauce on it now too. His mother would wash it tonight after the four-to-close, and it would be on the board by Monday morning, and Donnie would put it on with his eyes half-closed the way any kid put on a Monday shirt.

David Bellenghi was at Jackie's right, reading aloud from the Junior Prom ticket that had been mimeographed and handed out in homeroom, reading the part that said *semi-formal*, and laughing.

"Semi-formal means what, exactly."

"Means you can wear your father's tie," Donnie said.

"I don't own a tie."

"You can wear my father's tie. He's got three. One of them's paisley. You'll look like a lounge act but you'll be in regulation."

David laughed. Kathy did too, absently. Jackie did a small thing with his mouth that was the piano kid's version of a laugh.

Below the platform, at the milk coolers, a girl in mud-brown jeans and a red-checked flannel three sizes too big — a brother's flannel, cuffs rolled to the wrists, hem at the thighs — was lifting a half-pint of whole milk out of the case. A flute case was tucked under her elbow. Her hair was the color of wheat and uncombed at the back, and she was not looking at anyone. Patty Lally paid the cafeteria lady with a folded dollar and turned and walked toward the side door that went to the music practice rooms, and was gone before she could have been expected to register on anyone. Jackie's eye tracked her for two seconds, and then he was back to Donnie, and Donnie had said something that had made Kathy laugh, and the platform went on being the platform.

The bell would ring in four minutes. Kathy capped her pink marker.
