Donnie balled up his butcher paper with the sauce-stained *Globe* sports page inside it and carried the bundle to the trash can in a single walking motion, and on his way back he flicked the back of Jackie's neck with a forefinger as a hello and as a goodbye, and Jackie did not move.

---

Friendly's on Route 20. Eight-fifteen.

The grill was going. The Chef's Special — double burger with bacon and cheese — was laid out in a row, and Eddie the night cook was flipping them in the rhythm he had been flipping them in since 1968. The ice cream case ran the length of the counter, glass tops fogged at the edges, the mint chocolate chip gone bristled where the scoop had taken it down and the case had cycled through a defrost. The red Naugahyde booths ran along the window side, worn smooth at the corners where three thousand kids had slid in and out on their hips over the years. The place smelled like fryer oil and coffee forty minutes off the burner and the clean-cold smell of ice cream.

Grace had been on since four. Twenty minutes left before Peggy came in for close. Her polyester uniform was tan and brown with a white apron, and the apron pockets were full of the things a pocket collected over a shift — Sweet 'N Low packets, sugar packets, the little folded green guest checks with the carbon copies still attached, two pens, a rubber band, a matchbook from the Wayside Inn she had picked up off a table an hour ago. A pencil was behind her ear. The name tag over her left breast said GRACE in the same typeface they had used since Eisenhower.

At stool four, Dr. Kenneth Bellenghi was eating a bowl of chili and reading the op-ed page of the *Globe*. Dermatologist. He had been coming to this Friendly's most Fridays for eleven years because his wife did tennis round-robins at the Sudbury Country Club and the house was empty until nine. Coffee black. Grace poured without asking. He said *thanks Grace* without looking up. She said *you're welcome doc* without looking at him.

At stool five, one stool away, Mickey Rossi was eating a fried-clam plate. Foreman at the sheet-metal shop on Boston Post Road. Flannel shirt, canvas jacket, hands heavy and clean from the work sink. He was reading the *Herald* sports page because the *Globe* took the Red Sox too seriously. Coffee regular — two sugars and cream. Grace poured the refill without asking. Mickey said *thanks hon* without looking up. His wife Jeanne was working the four-to-close at the register on the other end of the counter, ringing out a family of five and handing the mother three paper place mats folded in thirds because the youngest kid had asked. Mickey and Jeanne did not look at each other at Friendly's. They had a system.

In booth two, Donna Koslowski — mother to a sixteen-year-old Rat who had learned to roll cigarettes from his Navy uncle — sat alone with a Fribble, her second of the week. Forty-one and recently on her own. Raytheon had let her off shift at four, lab-tech second. She had driven for twenty minutes without picking a direction and pulled in because the sign was lit. Grace brought her a fresh glass of ice water without being asked.

In booth four, the Carvajal family — mother, father, two kids under ten, a baby in a plastic carrier on the bench — was eating Happy Endings sundaes and arguing gently about who had gotten the extra cherry. The father worked at Raytheon on Hartwell. The kids had Fribble mustaches. Grace had brought them their check five minutes ago and would bring them a handful of the little red-wrapped mints with the coffee if the father ordered coffee, which he would.

Grace moved along the counter. Refilled Bellenghi. Refilled Rossi. Smiled at the Carvajal kids — the smile that had one specific watt for children and another for adults and a third for drunks coming in off the Post Road at eleven — and slid a clean glass of water in front of Donna Koslowski and took the empty and said *you let me know when you want another* and Donna said *Grace you are a saint* and Grace said *not yet* and kept moving.

At the end of the counter, below the register, she bent to pull a fresh stack of green guest checks out of the cardboard box on the shelf. Her knees made a small sound she ignored because she had been ignoring it since March. She came back up and set the stack in the apron pocket on top of the old stack. She had a pen in her hand already. She did not know she had picked it up.

Through the plate-glass window, across the lot, a station wagon pulled into a space under the lamp post and a family of four got out — father, mother, a boy and a girl, no one Grace knew — and walked toward the door in that slow family formation that meant they had already decided what they were ordering on the drive over. Grace watched them for the length of a breath, without thinking about them, without thinking at all. Then in the booth the Carvajal father lifted his hand for coffee and Grace was already moving toward the pot, and the pencil behind her ear stayed where it was, and the green checks in her apron pocket shifted against her hip as she walked.

**END CHAPTER 11A**


CHAPTER 12: NOVEMBER 14

Grace was up at five-thirty. She filled the coffee pot, set it on the burner, and stood at the counter in the kitchen light while the house was still dark around her. November dark. The kind that pressed against the windows like it wanted in.

She made Dan's lunch the way she made it every morning. Tuna on white bread, the can opened and drained and mixed with mayonnaise and a little celery salt, the way Dan's mother had made it for Dan's father in this same kitchen for forty years before Grace ever stood at this counter. She spread the tuna with a butter knife, her hands moving without thought — hands that were forty years old and looked fifty, the knuckles swollen from hot water and fryer grease, the skin across the backs cracked in fine white lines like the glaze on old china. She wrapped the sandwich in wax paper, folded the edges tight, wrapped it again in aluminum foil. An apple. Two oatmeal cookies from Sunday night. Brown paper bag. Thermos.

Dan came down at six-fifteen. Work boots, jeans, flannel shirt. He poured coffee, drank half of it standing at the counter, set the mug in the sink.

"Let him sleep," he said, meaning Nick, who was upstairs in a seventeen-year-old's coma after staying up past midnight working through a chord progression in the garage. "He's got calculus first period."

Grace nodded. She was wiping the counter where the tuna can had left a ring of oil.

Dan picked up the lunch bag and the thermos. He kissed her. His mouth tasted like coffee and his chin was rough because he never shaved before work, said it wasn't worth the blade. He smelled like sawdust. He always smelled like sawdust, even fresh from the shower — the smell had gotten into his skin years ago, part of him now, the way the fryer oil was part of her.

"Home by six," he said.

"Drive safe," she said.

He walked down the driveway. The truck's headlights came on. The engine turned over, caught, settled. Red taillights moved down Dutton Road and disappeared where the road bent toward Route 20.

She rinsed the tuna can and put it in the trash. She poured herself coffee and sat at the table and drank it while the sky went from black to gray. A Monday. Ordinary. That was the thing about it that would break her in half later — how ordinary it was, how much it resembled every other morning, how there was nothing in it to grab onto and say here, here is where I should have known.

---

60 State Street rose thirty-eight stories above the financial district. Dan had been on it since September, working steel. Sixty State, the crews called it — a tower going up between the Old State House and the harbor, a Skidmore job the same year Skidmore was finishing the Hancock down Copley way. Nineteen years in the ironworkers' union, never a lost-time injury, never a citation. He kept a 9/16ths socket wrench in his back pocket like other men kept a wallet.

At 10:17 AM, a crane cable snapped on the thirty-first floor.

The cable had been scheduled for replacement two weeks earlier. The replacement had been delayed. Cost savings. Somebody in an office somewhere had looked at a number on a spreadsheet and decided the cable could hold a little longer, the way somebody always does, the way the math always works until it doesn't.

Four steel I-beams dropped.

Dan was in the construction trailer below, checking blueprints. The tuna sandwich Grace had wrapped that morning was half-eaten on the desk beside him, the wax paper peeled back, the aluminum foil balled up next to his thermos.

The beams came through the roof of the trailer like it was made of paper.

---

The call came to Friendly's at 11:47 AM.

Grace was working the lunch rush. Tom Andrews, the manager, came out of the back office and stood at the edge of the dining room with a look on his face that made her hands stop moving.

She followed him to the office. The phone receiver was lying on the desk. She picked it up. Her hands — the same hands that had wrapped the sandwich six hours ago, that had spread tuna with a butter knife, that had folded wax paper with the unconscious precision of ten thousand mornings — went white around the plastic.

A man's voice. Frank Morrison, Boston Construction. An accident. The State Street tower. Your husband. I'm sorry.

Grace sat down in Tom's chair.

Tom drove her to the hospital. She sat in the passenger seat with her hands in her lap, still wearing her Friendly's uniform, the name tag pinned crooked over her left breast. She didn't cry. She looked out the window at Route 9 and the strip malls and the trees that were bare now, November-bare, every branch visible against the white sky like cracks in something.

---

At Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School, Nick was in calculus.

Mrs. Delano was at the board, chalk tapping in a rhythm that had nothing to do with music but that Nick's brain sorted into music anyway because that was what his brain did. The radiator clanked. A pencil rolled off a desk and hit the floor with a sound like a rim shot on a snare.

The classroom door opened.

Mrs. Patterson stood in the doorway. Low-heeled shoes the color of oatmeal. Gray cardigan buttoned to the neck. The look on her face that adults get when they've been told something terrible and are trying to carry it across a room without dropping it.

"Nick Johnson? Could you come with me, please?"

The hallway was empty and bright. Fluorescent tubes buzzing at their sixty-cycle frequency. The linoleum reflecting the light in a long pale stripe that ran the length of the corridor. Mrs. Patterson's oatmeal shoes clicked on the floor. Nick's sneakers made no sound. He followed her past the trophy case, past the gym doors, past the water fountain with its permanent puddle, and none of it seemed real — all of it too sharp, too detailed, the way things get when your body knows something your mind hasn't caught up to yet.

Grace was sitting on the brown couch in the principal's office. Still in her Friendly's uniform. Her face was a color Nick had never seen on a living person.

"Mom?"

"Sit down, Nick."

He sat.

"There was an accident at Daddy's job. A cable broke. Some beams fell." She was looking at a point somewhere past his left shoulder, somewhere behind the wall, outside the building, outside the town. "He's gone, Nick."

The words entered his ears and traveled to his brain and his brain converted sound to meaning, and the meaning was clear and simple and it broke something in his chest that he would spend the rest of his life trying to fix.

He didn't cry. Not then. He sat on the brown couch and looked at his mother's hands in her lap — the cracked knuckles, the fryer burns, the wedding ring that was now something else — and he felt the world tilt and resettle into a new configuration where Dan Johnson was not in it.

---

Closed casket. The doctor used the word trauma the way you'd use a term in a manual. Grace signed papers. Date of birth. Social security number. Next of kin.

Nick sat in a plastic chair in a hallway that smelled like floor cleaner and something under the floor cleaner that floor cleaner exists to cover up. He counted the ceiling tiles. Sixty-four. He counted them again. Sixty-four. This small consistency felt like the only thing in the world he could trust.

The construction company would pay fifty thousand dollars, the minimum required by law. The insurance company would fight the claim for eight months.

Tom Andrews drove them home. Grace went into the bedroom and closed the door. Nick stood in the kitchen where six hours ago his father had been alive, drinking coffee, saying let him sleep. The coffee mug was still in the sink. Nick picked it up. It was cold. He washed it and put it on the drying rack and stood there with the water running over his hands until the water went cold too.

---

The funeral was Saturday. St. Mary's. Closed casket, dark wood, brass handles. Union men from the construction site sat in the back pews in their one good suit, hands in their laps, faces set in the particular expression of working men who understand that the next body could be theirs.

Grace received every handshake with a stillness that people would later describe as strength but that was really just the absence of any other option. Nick stood beside her in Dan's old sport coat, the shoulders too wide, the sleeves past his wrists, and he shook every hand that was offered and held himself together the way a cracked glass holds water — carefully, and only for so long.

Jackie came with his parents. He stood at the back of the church after the service, and when Nick finally saw him, Jackie didn't say anything. He just stood there, and his face held the thing he couldn't say, and Nick nodded once, and that was it. Two boys standing six feet apart in a church that smelled like candle wax and chrysanthemums, and the distance between them already beginning to mean something different than it had a week ago.

---

Three days after the funeral, Nick went into the garage.

The door stuck the way it always stuck, the wood swollen with November damp, and he had to lean his shoulder into it the way Dan used to, and the motion of it — his body repeating his father's body — sent something through him that he didn't have a name for.

The garage smelled like sawdust. It would always smell like sawdust. Twenty years of Dan in here, the fine pale dust settled into every crack and joint of the concrete floor until the smell became the room itself. The tools hung on the pegboard where Dan had placed them, each one outlined in black marker so you'd know where it went — the system of a man who believed that order was a form of respect.

Nick's guitar sat in its case on the milk crate in the corner. He didn't touch it.

He looked at the workbench. Dan's red metal toolbox sat where it always sat, heavy and scarred, the paint chipped at the corners. Nick had never opened it. You didn't open another man's toolbox the same way you didn't read another man's mail.

He opened it.

The top tray held what you'd expect. Wrenches, screwdrivers, tape measure, the 9/16ths socket wrench. Nick lifted the tray and set it on the workbench.

Underneath, in the bottom of the box, he found what he did not expect.

Three guitar picks. Fender mediums, tortoiseshell, worn smooth at the edges from use. A stack of sheet music — not store-bought but handwritten on staff paper in Dan's careful block print, the notes drawn with the same precision he used on blueprints. "Stairway to Heaven." "Wish You Were Here." "Landslide." Chords marked in pencil, fingering notes in the margins — barre chord here, capo third fret, hammer-on to the suspended fourth.

Under the sheet music, a spiral notebook. The cover was soft with handling, the wire binding bent from being shoved into the toolbox a hundred times. Nick opened it.

Pages of chord progressions in Dan's handwriting. Not copied from anywhere. Original. Sequences that moved from major to minor and back in ways that surprised Nick's ear even reading them off the page — he could hear them, could feel where they wanted to go, and they were good. They were genuinely good. A page of lyrics, crossed out and rewritten and crossed out again:

    Sawdust hands and a borrowed name
    The house remembers what I became

Nick sat on the concrete floor with the notebook in his lap and the picks in his palm.

Dan had been a musician.

Not in public. Not in the garage when Nick was practicing, not anywhere his family could see. In the hours after Grace went to bed, in the early mornings before anyone was up, Dan had sat in this garage and played guitar and written songs and never told anyone. He'd hidden it the way some men hide drinking — not out of shame but out of the belief that the thing you love most is the thing you can least afford to need. He'd had a dream and he'd put it in a toolbox and closed the lid and gone to work.

The fingering was right. The progressions were sophisticated — not noodling but the work of someone who understood theory, who had studied, who had put in years of quiet practice that nobody knew about.

All those nights Nick had been in the garage learning guitar, Dan had been inside the house, listening through the wall, knowing everything Nick was discovering because he'd discovered it himself, alone, in secret, and chosen not to say. He'd taught Nick. He'd bought the guitar, built the practice space, shown him the basics. But he'd never said I play too. He'd never sat down beside his son and played.

Nick put the picks in his jacket pocket where they clicked together like small bones.

When Grace found him an hour later, he was holding the notebook open to the chord progressions. She looked at it and her face did something complicated — a recognition that moved through grief into something older.

"You knew," Nick said.

"He played after I went to sleep. I'd hear it through the floor." She leaned against the doorframe. "He was good, Nick. He was really good."

"Why didn't he—"

"Because we needed the money. Because he had a family. Because where he came from, music wasn't a job, it was a luxury, and luxuries were for other people." She wiped her eyes with the back of her hand. "He gave it to you instead. Everything he couldn't let himself have, he made sure you had it."

Grace reached over and closed the toolbox lid. The latch clicked shut with a sound that was quiet and final.

She couldn't open it again. The toolbox would sit on the workbench for years, and Grace would clean around it and never touch it, because inside it was the version of Dan she hadn't been able to save.

Nick would open it again. Many times. He would take out the notebook and study his father's chord progressions and learn them by heart, and when he played them on his own guitar the notes would sound like inheritance — not money, not property, not the things that pass from father to son in families that have things to pass. Something that couldn't be delayed for cost savings or dropped from thirty-one stories or sealed in a closed casket.

Music. The one thing Dan Johnson had that was entirely his own. And now it was Nick's.

---

# ACT II

---


CHAPTER 13: MAXANNE — WHEN FRIENDSHIP STARTED DYING

Three hours left.

The demo cassette is on the console at my right hand. Maxell, ninety-minute, label hand-printed in ballpoint: *Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979*. The plastic's warm. I keep picking it up and putting it down like I'm trying to decide if it's real.

The ON AIR sign blinks. Behind the glass, the kid they gave me for engineering — Bruins hoodie, laptop open, Red Bull sweating on the table — thinks music starts when somebody opens a DAW. He doesn't know he's working my last shift. They drew straws for tonight and I drew short. Everybody else gave their speeches this week. What you're hearing now is the one nobody wanted.

Two fingers of Heaven Hill in a paper cup. I've earned it.

On the turntable behind me, the station's farewell record is cued up and I haven't dropped the needle. Pink Floyd. *Shine On You Crazy Diamond.* The corporate playbook says that's how WBCN goes out. Graceful. Elegiac. The kind of ending you can put in a press release.

I reach for the needle. I don't touch it.

---

Thirty-nine years in rooms like this, and I still don't know what to say at a funeral.

So I'm going to tell you about two kids.

Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh. 1979. Sudbury, Massachusetts — a town with a soft-rock name and a geography like a knife. Route 20 splits it in half. North side: Tudors, horse fences, driveways long enough to teach a kid to drive. South side: ranches where the truck sits on blocks with the hood up and somebody's always halfway under it. Hop Brook runs between. There's a wooden bridge over that brook, and we'll get to the bridge in a minute. Not yet.

I met those boys through Maxell tape. Demo I wasn't supposed to have, the way demos come. I've discovered bands in worse ways. I broke Aerosmith out of a dive in Allston. I put The Cars on air when the labels still thought Ric Ocasek was a weirdo with cheekbones. Everybody at this station has a wall full of Christmas cards from people who went on to fill arenas. Mine's on the north wall. You can see it from here.

Nick and Jackie should be on that wall.

They're not.

---

November 14th, 1977.

Everything runs from that date, so I put it down first.

Dan Johnson died on a construction site off State Street. Crane cable snapped. Nineteen years of a man who built things with his hands, gone in the time it takes to breathe in. The Globe gave him one paragraph on page six, wedged between a fender-bender on Route 9 and a three-alarm in Framingham. They misspelled the last name.

That's what a working man's death is worth in print. A paragraph and a misspelled last name.

Nick was fifteen.

Grace Johnson went back to Friendly's the next morning. Not because she was tough. Because the mortgage doesn't give a damn how you feel, and when you don't have a cushion, grief is a luxury you can't afford. You carry your dead to work with you and you smile when the table asks for more coffee.

---

Nick stopped talking.

The guitar spoke for him. The Dutton Road garage became his whole vocabulary. Sawdust and motor oil, the concrete slab cold enough through his socks to make your teeth ache, Dan's tools still on the pegboards in the exact order Dan left them — Phillips-heads in descending size, the coffee can of sixteen-penny nails, a wrench set nobody had moved since November. Nick played till his fingers bled through the calluses and then he played some more. He wasn't practicing. He was bleeding off pressure the only way he knew.

I can see Jackie standing in that garage doorway. Clean jeans, one hand on the jamb, trying to find a sentence that won't make it worse.

I know what Nick said. Jackie told me six years later, in a booth at the Paradise, and he said it like a man reading off a charge sheet.

*I don't need your pity.*

Four words. That's how friendships end. Not in a fight. In a sentence you can't take back, because it was true enough to hurt.

---

Two hours and twelve minutes left.

You want a villain for this part of the story? I can't give you one. Jackie didn't do anything wrong. He showed up. He showed up with homework and with offers to just sit in the garage and not say anything. What Jackie couldn't know — his father was still at the kitchen table every morning — was that if Nick accepted help from the kid on the other side of Hop Brook, Nick was admitting the bridge existed. And the bridge was the thing he couldn't afford to see.

So Nick sent him home.

Then Jackie sent himself home. And the day after that, and the day after that.

By spring of '78 they were nodding at each other in the Lincoln-Sudbury hallway like guys who'd met once at a party. That school kept making musicians — four years later Mike Gordon would carry a bass case through the same corridor and nobody would know yet. The air at L-S was thick with kids who were going to be somebody. Nick and Jackie walked past each other in it like weather.

The cafeteria told on them. Nick sat with the kids whose fathers' names came up in the police log. Jackie sat with the kids whose fathers' names came up in the alumni magazine. Same room. Different zip code.

---

Here's the thing nobody warns you about this job.

Sometimes you hear the tape and you know. You know the way you know a storm is coming an hour before the sky cracks. I heard that Maxell in my kitchen on a Sunday afternoon in April of '79, and I stood up from the table with a coffee going cold in my hand, and I said out loud, to nobody in particular — *oh.*

I was looking for the next Neighborhoods. Upton boys, Rat regulars, the band WBCN's brand-new Rumble was going to put on the map that summer. I spent every weekend in Kenmore Square basements with beer on my boots, hunting for the next one. I had a type. Rough edges. Two voices that shouldn't work together but do.

I was looking for the next Neighborhoods, and they were on a cassette on my kitchen table.

Two kids. One in a garage that still smelled like his dead father. One in a bedroom lined with sheet music and a father who told him hands like his were for surgery. I put the tape down on the Formica and I thought, *I can save these boys.*

I have been wrong in this business before. I don't know that I've ever been wronger.

---

The thing about John Lennon is, he told us. Late in 1980, a few months before he went. About Bruce. *God help Bruce Springsteen when they decide he's no longer God.*

I've watched that prophecy come true in slow motion for thirty years. Not just Bruce. All of them. The machinery that lifts kids up is the same machinery that grinds them down, and the hand at the lever does not change.

Nick and Jackie never even got lifted. The machinery went to work on them before anyone outside that garage heard a note.

---

The Pink Floyd LP is still sitting there.

*Shine On You Crazy Diamond.* I could drop the needle right now and let Gilmour take us out. That's what the memo says. That's what the listeners expect. That's the ending somebody wrote on a clipboard in a glass tower downtown and forgot about ten minutes later.

I look at the record.

I look at the cassette.

I've been picking this tape up and putting it down for thirty years and the weight of it never changes. It's the weight of something that should have flown. Holding a bird's hollow bones and being told this thing used to fly.

One hour fifty-seven minutes left.

Plenty of time to get it wrong one last time. Plenty of time to get it right.

---

Before I tell you what I'm going to do with that tape, you need to hear the rest of the story.

You need to stand on a piece of wood across Hop Brook in October of 1978, late afternoon, oak leaves in the water, and watch two boys try to have a conversation.

That's where it really ended. Not in the hallway at Lincoln-Sudbury. Not in the cafeteria. On a wooden bridge with the brook running low under it — April you can't hear yourself think down there, but by October the water's a whisper, and every word lands.

Two kids went up onto that bridge.

I'll tell you in a minute what walked back down.

Stay with me.

**END CHAPTER 13**


CHAPTER 14: THE BRIDGE WHERE FRIENDSHIP DIED

The second Saturday in October, 1978. Eleven months since the trailer on Sixty State, near enough to a year that Nick had stopped counting and started rounding. The woods between Dutton Road and Cavicchio's back field were conservation land now, and the old stone walls ran through them waist-high, moss-headed, the farmers who'd stacked them a century gone and the sheep gone longer than that.

Nick led on the CR125. He'd rebuilt it over two summers out of scrap Dan had let him haul home from the dump — a '72 frame, a '74 engine set in one August Saturday with a jack and a two-by-four, which was how you did it if you were Dan and his son. The pull-start took three today before the plug cleared. The bark of the two-stroke came up through his wrist thin at idle, angry at half-throttle, the pre-mix sweet in the cold air. The grips had worn smooth to the shape of his hands. The handguards were Cavicchio-mud, spring-brown, because Nick didn't wash a bike for weather he'd just put back in the weather.

The trail was their runway. You rode it because you owned it, and you owned it because nobody else wanted it, and you didn't own much else.

Behind him: Stephen Murphy on a YZ80 with a bent kickstand, which meant Stephen parked it leaned against whatever tree was nearest. Behind Stephen: Mark Pianka, who'd grown four inches over the summer and whose jacket sleeves rode up past the wristbones, so his thumbs showed pale at the throttle when he came on the pipe. Nick didn't call them a rhythm section. He just knew what a rhythm section felt like at his back.

The trail climbed a low rise through the pines and opened on the bridge.

The bridge was older than either family's time in town. A CCC crew had put it in for a fire road in the thirties; some county man had patched the deck in the fifties with a sheet of diamond-plate steel where a plank had rotted through, bolted into the oak with carriage bolts gone orange at the heads. In spring the brook under it ran loud as a kitchen sink. In October the brook ran amber and thin, and you couldn't hear it except right under the bridge, where a flat slate rock split the current and made it say something.

Jackie was on the bridge.

Nick rolled the throttle off. The CR125 dropped to an idle and the idle filled the clearing. Jackie had both hands in the pockets of a Boston University windbreaker — his father's, Nick had known the jacket since they were twelve — and he was looking at the water like the water might answer something. He looked up when the engine came through the pines.

For a beat neither of them moved.

Nick's front tire was four feet from Jackie's loafers. Stephen and Mark had rolled up behind and let their engines idle down to the same nothing-sound. Three bikes coughing in a clearing and the brook under its rock.

"You need to move," Nick said.

"It's a walking bridge," Jackie said, not moving.

The almost happened then. The corners of both their mouths did a thing neither of them had asked them to do. Nick saw it on Jackie and Jackie saw it on Nick and for one half-count of the pipe they were thirteen again in a garage that smelled like sawdust, running a change that nobody in Sudbury could play but the two of them, together.

Then the footsteps came down the path.

---

Dr. Richard Marsh walked the conservation loop every Saturday in every month that wasn't January. He walked it in pressed khakis and rubber-soled Rockports and, today, the bottle-green half-zip he kept for mornings the windbreaker wasn't on its hook. His son had borrowed the windbreaker without asking. Richard had seen the absence on the hook and felt a small pleased thing and taken the half-zip down without comment.

He came up the path and saw his son on the bridge and three dirt bikes across from him, and his first thought was protective and clean. He came onto the plank deck. His eyes moved over the four boys the way they moved over an X-ray — pattern first, detail after. The lead boy he recognized. The Johnson boy. He had been in the house once, five years ago, holding a Coca-Cola in both hands the way a boy holds a Coca-Cola when he has not been in many houses like this one.

Richard's eyes moved down to the boy's left hand on the throttle grip.

The fingertips were callused in a line across the tops — not on the pads, on the very tops, the way a year of pressing steel strings into a fretboard too tall for a boy's hand shaped the skin and kept shaping it. Richard was not a guitarist. He had never owned one. But a musician's hand was a musician's hand; he had lived in conservatory practice rooms long enough to know the shape of sustained commitment even when it wore a different grain of callus than his own. The boy's thumb rode loose at the heel of the palm. The first knuckle carried a little white scar, not from labor. Something moved in Richard's chest that he had not invited and did not name. He looked away.

"Everything all right, Jackson?"

His son didn't answer right away.

"These boys bothering you, son?"

---

*These boys.*

Jackie felt the two words land the way a bruise lands — not at the surface, underneath. His mouth closed down on the thing that had almost happened, and his face put itself on the way he put it on at Mrs. Caldwell's Sunday recitals, the face his father thought was composure.

His lungs were tight with cold air. Pine sap had come off a low branch onto the khakis on his left thigh, a dark streak. His father's Old Spice came off the windbreaker in small warm waves when he moved — the smell of every morning he'd watched his father leave the house — and the smell now felt like a hand on his shoulder he had not asked for.

"No, Dad," Jackie said. "I was just telling them it's a walking bridge."

The almost-smile was gone. He had felt it go. Some muscle at the corner of his mouth that the body had kept ready for Nick for five years gave up its readiness, and the face was the face again.

He stepped aside.

He didn't decide to step aside. His legs did it before he did. Two measured steps to the right, the planks spongy under the loafers, his weight coming off the centerline of the bridge and onto the downstream rail. The body kept a small book of these measurements. Where to stand at recital. Where to stand at his father's cocktail parties. Where to stand on a bridge.

---

Nick heard *these boys* through the CR125 idle and heard it clean.

Something in his chest wanted to kill the engine and step off the bike and say *Jackie, it's us.* Something else in his chest wanted to put a fist into the Boston University windbreaker. The thing that won was neither. His right wrist tightened on the throttle half a turn, and the pipe bark jumped from nothing to something, and his jaw hinge tightened against itself, and the inside of his cheek cut against a molar and he tasted the small bright copper of his own blood.

He opened the throttle.

The front tire rolled onto the metal plate.

The plate rang.

One note, clean — the note a horseshoe makes dropped on a rail — the whole bridge saying it at once. Then the oak planks took the weight and groaned, the deep dry groan of old white-oak under a load it had not been asked to take, and under the planks the stringers shook, and from the underside of the stringers a fine red rain-rust sifted down into the amber water.

Stephen came next. The plate rang. The planks groaned. Mark came last. The plate rang a third time, flatter, because the plate was warm now.

Four bikes on a walking bridge. Three rings. Three groans. The stringers complaining in a register Nick felt up through the footpegs and into his shins.

He did not look back.

On the far bank the trail opened into the fire road, and the fire road ran down to Cavicchio's back field, and the corn there was cut to stubble and the crows were working the rows in that patient black way crows work a field in October. Nick rolled through the stubble edge and opened the throttle, and the engine bark got smaller in the woods behind him, and then there was no bark at all — just the small flat sound of Hop Brook under its rock, and the smell of hot two-stroke oil staying in the pines the way a thing stays after the thing that made it has gone.

---

Stephen peeled off at the Y for home. Mark peeled off at Dutton. Nick did not go home.

He circled back the long way — past the stone wall where the sheep had been and weren't, up the rise through the pines — and came down on the bridge from the south side, alone, and cut the engine.

The silence came back with the smell of hot oil.

Jackie was gone. Dr. Marsh was gone. The plate where Nick's tire had rung it was warm still — he could feel it through the sole of his boot when he set the foot down — and a little dust of rain-rust lay in a half-moon at the downstream edge of the plate where the stringers had shaken it loose.

He sat the bike and looked at his left hand on the grip.

The fingertips were callused in a line across the tops of the first three fingers. He had not thought about it in a long time. Nobody had ever looked at his hand the way the man in the half-zip had looked at it. He did not know what the look had meant.

In his jacket pocket, three Fender mediums — tortoiseshell, worn smooth at the edges — clicked against each other when he shifted his weight. He set his palm flat over the pocket to keep them quiet and rode home the long way, past the corn stubble, with the crows lifting and settling behind him in twos.

**END CHAPTER 14**


CHAPTER 15: THE POSTER

Monday morning, seven-thirty, and the music room at Lincoln-Sudbury still smelled of Friday's varnish — the janitor had refinished the risers over the weekend and the air hung sharp with turpentine. Cinder block walls the color of canned peas. Fluorescents with one tube stuttering. A faded Boston Symphony poster curling off its thumbtacks near the door. And beside it, something brand new.

The poster was a photocopy. You could tell by the fuzz on the letters — that soft Xerox black where the ink had spread a hair into the paper, so the edges of the words looked like they had been breathed on. Legal-sized, 8.5 by 14, and whoever had run the stack had run it hot; a faint gray bar ghosted down the left margin where the drum had smudged. The Lincoln-Sudbury crest sat slightly off-center in the upper corner, the shield canted maybe two degrees off true, as if the kid on the copier had been in a hurry. Four push-pin holes, one at each corner, punched straight through. The paper had a grain you could feel with a thumbnail.

BATTLE OF THE BANDS
May 15, 1979
Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School
Grand Prize: $500 — Professional Recording Session
Sponsored by WBCN — The Rock of Boston

The WBCN letters were set in that unmistakable seventies sans-serif — round, confident, the kind of typeface that belonged on a concert tee. Whoever had pinned the poster had pinned it at a height where tall kids had to stoop slightly to read and short kids had to look up. Nick Johnson came in through the west door at 7:31, on his way to first-period shop. He was reading it by 7:32. Jackie Marsh came in through the east door at 7:33, having left his sheet music in the practice room the night before, and saw Nick before he saw the poster. He read the poster anyway. They stood a foot apart at the bulletin board and their shoulders did not quite touch.

Five hundred dollars. Grace Johnson made two sixty-five an hour at the Friendly's on Route 20, plus tips on a good Sunday. A month of shifts would not close that number. Three months of groceries would. It was also the amount Jackie's father spent on a weekend in Chatham without remarking on it, which meant the figure read one way to the boy coming in through the west door and another way entirely to the boy coming in through the east — a Friday shift on one side, a receipt on the other.

From his desk, Bob Wentworth watched the two boys read. He did not stand. He kept the red pencil in his hand and kept it moving over the scale sheets in front of him, as if the check marks he was making were the reason he was looking up between them. He had seen students read a poster before. He had never seen two students read one the way Nick and Jackie were reading this one — as if the paper might, if looked at long enough, tell them what to do about each other.

"Gentlemen," he said. "I see you've noticed the announcement."

Neither boy moved.

"Band names due Friday. Eight slots."

Jackie spoke first, to the poster. "Queen."

Nick spoke to the poster. "Aerosmith."

Wentworth set the pencil down. "The competition is meant to celebrate —"

"It's about the music," Nick said.

"The music," Jackie said. "Not the story around it."

"Right."

"Just the sound."

"Just what you can do."

Nick turned his head a half-inch. Jackie turned his. For a beat the two of them stood with their faces angled toward each other without quite facing each other, the way two dogs stand before they decide whether they know one another. Wentworth opened the top drawer of his desk.

"Wait," he said. "Hold on a minute."

Under a stack of school-issue staff paper there was a manila envelope that had been in that drawer since September. In the envelope was a photograph. His fingers found it the way they used to find the mouthpiece of the trumpet in the dark. He slid it out and laid it on the blotter and flattened it with the heel of his hand.

Curtis Middle School Talent Show, May 1975. Two boys standing on a gymnasium stage that had been draped in crepe paper. Thirteen years old, one guitar each, matching too-big sport coats borrowed from fathers. Nick's hair a little longer then. Jackie's a little shorter. They had their free arms around each other's shoulders and they were grinning the kind of grin that gets you photographed — a grin that had not yet learned to be careful about anything.

"I was there," Wentworth said. "You boys played 'Walk This Way' into 'Bohemian Rhapsody' as a mashup that had no business working. It worked."

He turned the photograph toward them.

The music room clock, which lost forty seconds a day, ticked.

Nick's face did something — a small collapse at the jaw, a flicker above the eyes — that neither Wentworth nor Jackie could have put into a word, but Jackie saw it, and Jackie's hand came halfway out of his pocket and stopped and went back in. The fluorescent in the corner buzzed. Down the hall a locker slammed.

Then Nick's eyes came off the photograph and went back to the poster, and Jackie's followed a half-second behind.

"That was middle school," Nick said.

"We're different."

"Yeah."

The bell rang. Thirty seconds from a corridor of bodies.

"May the best band win," Nick said, because the other sentences did not have a way to be said.

"Count on it."

They left through the doors they had come in by. Wentworth sat with the photograph on the blotter in front of him, and did not put it away.

---

He did not put it away for forty minutes.

The first-period kids filed in past him and he took attendance by ear, counting the thumps of trumpet cases on the tile without looking up. The photograph lay where he had set it down, two boys with their arms around each other's shoulders, and beside it, because he had opened the drawer far enough, lay the other objects that had been in it for twenty years.

A dried-out reed in a cardboard slip, last damp in 1963.

A program, folded twice along old creases, from Jordan Hall, March 8, 1958: New England Conservatory Undergraduate Showcase, Robert A. Wentworth, trumpet, Hindemith Sonata, second movement. The paper had gone the color of weak tea at the folds.

A metronome that did not work.

He had not played the Hindemith since the year his second child was born. He had taken the music teacher's job at Lincoln-Sudbury the spring after that, and he had not unpacked the Hindemith from the bottom of the upright case in the hall closet, and he had not, in all the years since, explained to anyone — wife, children, department chair — what had gone out of him between the 8th of March, 1958 and the Tuesday afternoon in September of 1962 when he signed the W-2 for the Lincoln-Sudbury Regional School District. It was a gap a man could live in. He had lived in it.

He looked at the photograph of Nick and Jackie and felt the gap open a half-inch wider.

The call letters on the poster outside his office — WBCN — still carried Maxanne Sartori's signature even though Maxanne had been gone from the station two Aprils now, over at Elektra in A&R. She was the one who had played the Neighborhoods out of Upton when nobody else would. The lettering on the poster was the shape of her, even if her voice wasn't in it anymore. Wentworth had the thought and then put it down, the way he put most thoughts down, and closed the drawer without locking it.

---

By second period the poster was in the library. By third there was one in the cafeteria above the milk coolers, one on the glass of the gym door, one taped — against policy — to the inside of the smokers' corridor, where the light came through dirty windows the color of tobacco water and nobody took it down. By fourth, five copies had gone up school-wide, and the Xerox toner on all of them smelled faintly of ozone because the machine in the main office had been run thirty times in an hour.

The old four-way map of Lincoln-Sudbury — Rampers, Rats, Regs, Rollers — had been the only map anyone needed since before any of them started high school. By Monday lunch it had a new seam cutting diagonal across all four. A Ramper and a Roller could sit at the same table for the whole period arguing whether "Dream On" was a better song than "Somebody to Love," and the argument would be the only thing the table talked about.

By lunch someone had drawn a small careful skull in blue Bic next to the word Queen. By seventh period someone else had drawn a longer-fanged skull next to Aerosmith. By the end of the day the margin of the cafeteria poster carried, in black Sharpie, in the handwriting of somebody who had been to Kenmore Square, the words THE RAT, with an arrow pointing at nothing in particular.

The poster by the gym door was read fifty times before sixth period. You could tell by the thumbprints on the lower right corner where kids planted a finger while they leaned in to check the date a second time. The corners of the paper had already begun to soften.

In the hall outside the music room, Patty Lally stood with her books on her hip and read the poster the way she read everything, which was twice. Under her breath, without knowing she was doing it, she was humming a Fleetwood Mac song that had come out two years ago and had not left her.

Mr. Ogden, in sixth-period calculus, wrote the words harmonic and structure in the margin of a problem set and did not immediately remember why. Mrs. Williams in home ec caught herself humming "Dream On" while folding pie dough, stopped, embarrassed, and started again. The janitor, who had been at L-S longer than most of the teachers, fixed the cooling duct in the cafeteria ceiling and said nothing about anything, because he had seen this kind of weather come up before and knew it ran its course either way.

That night, Nick stayed in the garage on Dutton Road past midnight with the tube amp humming and his left hand cramping around the neck of the Les Paul copy and the opening of "Dream On" under his fingers for the fortieth time — the way you grind a thing down until it either gives up its secret or breaks your hand. The concrete was cold through his sneakers. The pegboard on the wall was his father's. He did not stop.

In the music room at L-S, at five-past-six that same evening, Jackie Marsh held the high note in "Somebody to Love" until his throat gave out, then drank from the tap over the utility sink and tried it again. The fluorescent stuttered. His voice, when it came back, was raw at the top. He went again.

**END CHAPTER 15**


CHAPTER 16: SENIOR YEAR — NICK'S WORLD

The L-S cafeteria smelled like fish sticks and canned tomato sauce and the plastic tang of trays still hot from the dishwasher. Friday menu. Above the steam tables a fluorescent ballast hummed at a frequency Nick's ear sorted, against his will, into a flat B-natural — the note it had hummed at every lunch for four years, the school's own pedal tone. He had his back to the cinder block wall and a tuna sandwich in wax paper Grace had made at five-forty that morning before the pickup left for Friendly's.

Stephen Murphy sat across from him with a chocolate milk. Mark Pianka drummed the edge of the formica with two fingers, unaware, the way he drummed everything. Three weeks since the Battle of the Bands poster had gone up, and the cafeteria had sorted itself without anyone moving a chair. Jackie Marsh's table was two rows over — the college-bound kids, the ones who said SAT the way other kids said Budweiser.

Jackie stood up.

The scrape of his chair on linoleum went through the room like a struck cymbal. A hush traveled outward in rings — first the kids who had been facing him, then the kids alongside, then the back benches where the Ramper girls sat, one of them mid-sentence with her hand lifted. Her hand stayed lifted. Stephen Murphy set his chocolate milk down very carefully, the way you set down a glass you've just learned was full of something breakable.

"'Somebody to Love,'" Jackie said.

He sang.

In the music room his voice had a ceiling. Here it didn't. The cinder block threw everything back at harder angles — a slap rather than a bloom — and Jackie rode the slap, Freddie Mercury through a kid trained since he was eight, operatic at the top, and here and there the trained edge frayed because he was seventeen and because you cannot really sing Queen at lunch without the frayed edge showing. His two bandmates fumbled into the harmonies a half-beat late. The fish-stick steam rose past him in the light. A lunch lady came to the kitchen doorway holding a pair of tongs and did not move again.

When Jackie hit the last high one he had to push, but he got there, and the note held against the block walls and came back at him from the other side. The clap that followed was scattered, dazed, not yet applause.

Nick set down his sandwich and stood up.

"'Dream On,'" he said.

No guitar. Nothing in his hands. His throat was dry. He swallowed and his ear found the note from the hum in the ballast above him and he stepped onto it like a man stepping onto a porch. Stephen Murphy came in under him, third below, rough. Mark Pianka flattened his hands on the formica and tapped the opening figure of the piano part, right hand arpeggio against the tabletop, fingers finding keys that were not there.

Nick sang. His voice was not trained. It went where he had dragged it — out past his father's funeral and back, past a toolbox he hadn't yet opened, past a hundred nights in a garage learning what his fingers could be made to do. On the long word — *dream* — the cafeteria held its breath. He went up for the top of it — *dream until your dreams come true* — and he pushed, and he got there, and the high note was thinner than Jackie's and rawer than Jackie's and nobody in that cafeteria mistook it for anything but what it was.

The silence after was not scattered. It was one silence.

Then the noise, all at once, the way a wave hits.

"Sit down." Mr. Wentworth stood at the end of the table, hands in the pockets of his corduroy jacket, the music teacher's face set in the expression of a man trying not to grin. "Both of you. Principal's office. Now."

---

Brad Sargent had been principal at L-S for twenty years and vice principal for eight before that. His office had a gray metal desk and a carpet the color of wet oatmeal that smelled like thirty years of industrial solvent laid over thirty years of whatever the solvent was laid over. On the desk sat a framed photograph of two kids in a canoe, a pen cup of Bic Clics, and a white ceramic coffee mug that read KEEFE TECH COUGARS — BASEBALL '63 in green letters faded at the rim where his thumb had gone a thousand times.

Nick sat in one chair. Jackie sat in the other. The chairs were molded plastic and exactly the same height.

"I have reports," Sargent said. He looked at a manila folder on his desk and did not open it. "Kids skipping class to rehearse. An argument in C-wing last Thursday. Mrs. Fiore says she can't get through homeroom announcements because of you." He tipped the mug an inch, set it back. "And now I hear singing in the cafeteria."

"We were —" Jackie began.

"I don't want to know what you were." Sargent leaned back. The chair creaked. A fluorescent tube in the ceiling ticked like a cheap watch. "I got a fire marshal who signed off on the auditorium for May 15th. I got WBCN calling my office. I got a superintendent who wants to know why his senior class is using the cafeteria as a rehearsal hall." He looked at Nick. He looked at Jackie. "You want me to cancel it?"

"No, sir," they said. Same word. Same moment.

Sargent almost smiled.

"Then off school property. You want to sing at each other, do it somewhere my secretary can't hear it. You have until May 15th. After that I don't care what you do." He reached for the mug. "Get out of my office."

---

In the hallway the lockers were pale green and their sneakers did not quite squeak on the waxed floor. They walked side by side because the hallway was narrow. Jackie's shoulder was an inch from Nick's shoulder and did not touch it.

"It's on," Nick said.

"Set list coming?"

"Yeah. Yours?"

"Yeah."

They reached the doors. Jackie pushed left. Nick pushed right.

Nick drove the wood-paneled wagon home down Route 20 past the Store 24 where Dennis was working the four-to-midnight, and lifted two fingers off the steering wheel as he passed the window. The sun was low over the trees. The fields behind the Cavicchio place were still brown. He turned up Dutton Road. The Johnson house sat where it always sat, the white paint going gray on the north side, the bay window in the dining room catching the last of the light.

---

The garage door stuck the way it always stuck. Nick leaned his shoulder into it.

Seventeen months. Dan had been dead seventeen months and the garage still smelled like sawdust and motor oil, the smell soaked so deep into the pegboard wood and the raw joists overhead that nothing short of a fire was going to lift it out.

The Aerosmith calendar from 1975 hung above the workbench where Dan had hung it when it was new. It was still turned to April — *Toys in the Attic* era, Joe Perry in a white scarf, Tyler leaning into a mic — the month Dan had turned it to four years ago and then left, because by May he'd bought a new one, and the May after that he'd stopped buying them, and the May after that he was gone. A 1975 April looking at a 1979 April through four years of furnace dust.

On the lid of a Maxwell House can sat a handful of loose hardware — nuts, washers, a bent finishing nail — and Dan's 9/16ths socket wrench, the one he'd carried in his back pocket at work every day for nineteen years. Nick had put it there himself the week after the funeral, because it was the only place he could think to put it.

The toolbox sat under the calendar.

Red. Scarred. Paint chipped at the corners. Latch shut. He had opened it once, the week of the funeral, and seen what was inside — the sheet music, the spiral notebook, the thing his father had never said out loud — and Grace had come in from the kitchen and closed the lid without speaking, and in seventeen months neither of them had touched it since. He had heard the music through the floor for half his life and he had never said he heard it, and Dan had never said he played it, and the closed red box held the proof of both silences. He did not reach for the latch. Not tonight.

He sat down on the milk crate and picked up the guitar.

"You eat?"

Grace was in the doorway. Still in her Friendly's uniform, the brown polyester that never let fryer oil all the way out of it, the little plastic name tag pinned crooked over her left breast — GRACE, and under it MANAGER, and a small gold foil star the company had added when they made it official in February. Her hands were red from dishwater up to the wrists.

"I had the tuna."

"That was lunch, Nick."

"I'll eat."

She came in. She did not sit. She leaned against the workbench, an inch from the toolbox, and her eyes went to it and then away — a glance so short he would not have caught it if he had not been watching for it all his life.

"Your father would've liked this," she said. "You going up against that Marsh kid."

"Yeah."

Her hand flattened on the workbench where Dan used to sit to unlace his boots at the end of a day. She looked past Nick, past the open garage door, past the driveway, toward the house — toward the bay window in the dining room, the one with the crown molding above it that nobody had painted over because nobody could reach. Her mouth opened on something and then closed on it. Whatever it was, it was older than him.

"He always said you had it," she said instead. "Not just the playing. The other thing."

"I know."

"Well." She pushed off the bench. "Come in by nine."

Her footsteps crossed the driveway and went up the kitchen steps and the screen door gave its two-note complaint.

Nick looked at the guitar in his lap. He played the opening of "Sweet Emotion." Thumb muting the low E, the slide up into the E-flat on the fourth string that Dan had shown him the summer he was fourteen, the week he'd blistered both thumbs and kept going. His fingers went without being asked.

The garage rang back. Concrete floor, bare joists, pegboard walls, no carpet anywhere — every note bounced off something hard and came back a half-breath later, doubled. The 9/16ths socket sat on the coffee-can lid. The red toolbox sat shut. The 1975 April looked down at the 1979 April from above the bench, and Joe Perry, caught forever in a white scarf, did not know yet what was going to happen to any of them.

Nick played it again. And again. The strings bit his fingertips. Outside, someone three houses down called a dog in for the night, and a truck on Route 20 downshifted for the light at Horse Pond Road, and the April dusk settled over Dutton Road the way it settled over every road in town — without asking, without promising anything.

He played until his left hand ached, and then he played another hour after that.

**END CHAPTER 16**


CHAPTER 17: JACKIE'S WORLD

The Howard Miller grandfather clock in the hall struck six-thirty and the Marshes sat. That was how it worked on Maple Street — the clock began to strike, and by the fourth tone the four of them were at the table with napkins across their laps. The tick kept going after the chime. Seventy-two to the minute. Andante.

The table was mahogany, the grain running the long way, a single dark whorl near the head like a water-stain from someone else's grief. The silver had belonged to Eileen's father — W.B.H. engraved at the shaft end of each knife, W. B. Hollister, a Beacon Hill man who had lived long enough to see his daughter marry a psychiatrist and long enough to let her have the forks. The money in this house had come from her side. Jackie had worked that out by eleven without anyone telling him.

Eileen brought the plates in on a tray. Chicken piccata. The capers sat in a thin gold sauce that caught the chandelier light and threw it back in small wet stars. The chicken pounded thin as stationery and laid in overlapping half-moons. Against the long wall, between the windows and the sideboard, stood the walnut upright Jackie had never seen his father touch. Its ivories had yellowed. Its music stand held an open hymnal nobody sang out of. Above it hung a Rothko reproduction — deep red over deep black, tasteful and just off-center in a way that pretended not to be. The brass switchplate by the door was polished Tuesdays.

"Jackson received his acceptance letter," Eileen said. She had said it twice already today — to Mrs. Caldwell on the phone, to Jackie in the kitchen. This was the performance. "Early admission. Juilliard. Full scholarship."

"Congratulations," Elizabeth said.

"Thank you," Jackie said.

Dr. Marsh set down his wine glass. The base touched the mahogany and made a small definite sound. "We're proud, Jackson." He cut a piece of the chicken with the same clean motion he'd used for twenty years. "I assume you'll keep with Mr. Holloway through graduation. No reason to let the hands go soft before fall."

"Of course."

"The Jordan Hall recital is April twentieth. The program coordinator telephoned on Thursday. They want the Nocturne in E-flat. Opus nine, number two. The one you took to Tanglewood."

Jackie put his fork down. The capers moved in their sauce.

April twentieth was three weeks out from the Battle of the Bands. Three weeks of Chopin in the cathedral acoustic of Jordan Hall, then a week and a half of Freddie Mercury's gospel choir stacked over A-flat. He could feel both pieces in different parts of his body. The Chopin was in the first and fourth fingers of his left hand. The Queen was in his throat.

"I can do both," Jackie said.

"Do both."

"The recital and the competition."

Dr. Marsh looked at him. Not unkindly. That was the thing — the look never went unkind. "The recital draws reviewers from the Globe, Jackson. Faculty from the Conservatory. That is a career. The other — "

"Is also real."

The words came out faster than he'd meant them. The word *real* sat in the air with the capers and the lemon and the tick of the clock in the hall.

Jackie watched his father's hand go to the wine glass.

Dr. Marsh's fingers closed around the stem. On the word he had not yet spoken but was about to, his first three fingertips curled in against the crystal in a shape the stem did not require — the shape of a hand laid across three strings and pressed. It lasted the breath before the word and was gone when the word came.

"Jackson. This is a high school competition. Sponsored by a rock station. It is beneath your level."

Jackie did not answer.

Eileen's smile did the thing it did, the tightening at the inside corners. "Sweetheart. Your father only means — "

"May I be excused?" Elizabeth said.

"Finish your beans," Eileen said.

Elizabeth ate three beans the way a person counts steps on a staircase she does not want to climb. Then she folded her napkin and left. Her door upstairs did not quite slam. The clock ticked.

"We'll discuss this later," Dr. Marsh said. "When everyone has had time."

"Yes, sir."

Jackie finished the chicken because he had been raised to finish the chicken. Under the table the first and fourth fingers of his left hand walked the Nocturne on his thigh through the denim, andante, seventy-two. His throat remembered the Queen chorus without being asked.

---

Upstairs, the hallway carpet deadened everything. His door closed behind him with the soft brass click of a door hung correctly.

The Henle edition of the Nocturne was open on the Steinway's music stand — E-flat major, Opus 9 No. 2, the *Andante* marking in the engraver's italic. On the shelf above the desk sat the Tanglewood trophy: a brass treble clef on walnut, *Young Artists — First Prize — Piano — 1977*. On the desk lay the Juilliard envelope. Cream laid paper. *The Juilliard School, 144 West 66th Street, New York, NY 10023*. Opened with a letter knife along the top edge, not torn.

Jackie sat on the bed. He did not go to the piano yet.

In the bottom desk drawer, under the old Curtis programs, lay a small Panasonic transistor the size of a deck of cards. The dial was taped at 104.1 with a strip of electrical tape so it would not wander off WBCN in the night. His father did not permit rock stations in the house. Jackie listened at a whisper after midnight with the radio under the pillow, and Maxanne's voice came through it bourbon-slow and went somewhere in his head and stayed.

A knock on the door. "It's me."

"Come in."

Elizabeth was thirteen and small for it. She wore her plaid uniform skirt and one sock to the knee and one sock rolled at the ankle, the asymmetry absolutely deliberate. She sat on the bed cross-legged. Her thumbnail found a loose thread in the chenille bedspread and began to work at it — the slow careful plucking their mother did at the pearls at her throat during the Globe crossword.

"You're going to do the Battle of the Bands anyway," she said.

"Yeah."

"Even though he'll lose his mind."

"Yeah."

Her thumbnail kept at the thread. "Is it because of Nick Johnson?"

Jackie's hand went still on the bedspread. The Juilliard envelope shifted half an inch under his elbow, paper whispering against paper. A small physical thing. He felt it without moving.

"It's complicated," he said.

"You guys used to be best friends."

"Yeah."

"What happened? I always wanted to know but I was afraid to ask."

Jackie looked past her at the window. Maple Street at dusk. The streetlights were coming on in sequence — the Caldwells', then the Petersons', then the Schiffs', each one striking a second behind the last, a small orderly arpeggio of sodium glow down a row of hedges clipped to the same height. Someone was pulling into a driveway in a Buick the color of a Buick.

"We grew up," Jackie said. "People change."

"That's what adults say when they don't want to explain things."

The corner of his mouth moved. A breath caught and came out as nothing.

"Yeah," he said. "It is."

Elizabeth pulled the loose thread another eighth of an inch. She did not look at him. The thread came free in her thumbnail and she rolled it into a small tight bead between finger and thumb.

"I heard you practicing last night," she said. "After they went to bed. That Queen song. The one."

"'Somebody to Love.'"

"You sounded different."

"Different how."

She thought about it. She thought about it a long time. The clock downstairs kept its andante. The sodium lamps on Maple Street came on in their order.

"Like you actually cared about the notes," she said, "instead of just hitting them right."

The silence held. Jackie did not fill it. Elizabeth did not fill it. The Juilliard envelope lay under his elbow with its Manhattan address pressed into the paper. The silence took up the space where the next thing should have gone, and then it took up a little more, and neither of them broke it.

"Don't tell Dad," Jackie said at last.

"Obviously."

She stayed a minute longer. Then she got up and went to the door with her one sock up and her one sock down, and she paused with her hand on the knob.

"Jackie. I hope you win the other one. Not because of Juilliard. Because last night you sounded like a person."

The door closed behind her.

---

Jackie sat where he was until the hallway carpet stopped conducting her footsteps. Then he went to the desk.

The Curtis talent-show photograph stood in a small silver frame next to the lamp. May 1975. Two thirteen-year-old boys on the auditorium stage — Jackie in a clip-on tie, Nick in a flannel shirt with the cuffs rolled — a plywood trophy held between them at chest height, their free arms around each other's shoulders. Nick's grin had a tooth missing. They had figured out, in that photograph, the only thing either of them had ever figured out.

He held it a long time. The desk lamp threw his own fifteen-year-old reflection faintly on the glass over the thirteen-year-old grin.

He opened the bottom desk drawer, laid the photograph face down on the stack of old programs, and closed the drawer.

The house was quiet. The piano was not playing itself.

Jackie crossed to the Steinway and sat and set the Henle edition square on the stand and put his hands on the keys. The fingers found the E-flat chord at the top of the page without being asked, the left hand low and open, the right lifting for the andante. His throat, on its own, remembered the Queen chorus and did not sing it.

He began to play the Chopin.

---

Downstairs, Eileen Marsh went into the small back bedroom she had not invited anyone into since 1966 and closed the door behind her.

The room had a single north window and a floor lamp with a cracked ceramic base she had meant to replace for eleven years. Along the far wall, a deep utility shelf held nine canvases turned to the wall, the backs of their stretcher bars the only thing a visitor could have seen if there had been visitors. The topmost canvas was dated in pencil on its stretcher, *9/23/65*, and she had carried it up from the basement the year she married Richard because she had not yet decided what to do with it and had never since decided. Beside the shelf stood her old easel, the oil stains on the bar the color of winter sunset under forty years of skin cells and dust. Her palette knife lay where she had put it down on the kitchen of a third-floor Huntington Avenue walk-up in the fall of 1959, a week before the acceptance letter from Richard's department chair changed her address for good.

She did not open a canvas. She did not touch the knife. She stood in the middle of the room with her arms loose at her sides and she looked at all of it the way a widow looks at her dead husband's coat closet — an inventory of a life she had agreed to put down, conducted in private so the agreement would hold.

Through the door and up the stairs, the Chopin came to her in fragments. The E-flat opening. The lift into the andante. A measure Jackie played twice. Then a third time. Then the return. The boy was good and the boy had been good for a long time now and she had taught him nothing — she had *given* him nothing she had kept for herself — and still when he played the first phrase of anything lyrical her right hand lifted involuntarily to her sternum as if to press a brush back against a canvas that was not there.

She was forty-six. She had been forty-six for four months. She had stopped painting at twenty-eight, formally — *set it down*, in her own phrasing, a phrase she had used with her mother and once with a professor and never since with anyone — and at twenty-nine she had given birth to Jackie. At thirty-nine to Elizabeth. Between those two children and the years around them she had run Richard's household the way another woman might have run a small institution: billing, scheduling, laundry, his summer research trips, his departmental wives' luncheons, the endless small repairs of a man who could publish on trauma but could not put a button back on a shirt without cutting himself. Richard was a loved man by his children and by his students, and he was a man who required, in the private way that did not appear in papers or in recommendations, almost constant tending. Medication schedules he would not set for himself. Appointment reminders. The two glasses of water before bed she set on his nightstand because if she did not he did not drink them and woke at three in the morning disoriented. The specific plate he preferred on Sundays. The way the pillow had to be on the bed. Eileen ran this small invisible country the way she had once painted — steadily, without fuss, with the same focus she had brought at twenty-one to a window in February with northern light. She had transferred the focus. She had kept the focus. She had only stopped producing the painting.

Upstairs the Chopin broke off on a phrase and resumed.

She crossed to the easel and stood in front of it. She laid her right hand flat on the wood of the bar where the oil stains were, and the hand remembered. It did the small unconscious thing a hand does when it has held a brush for five hundred hours — the thumb lifted, the first two fingertips curled, the pinky steadied against the heel of the palm — and for half a breath she was twenty-two, standing in a narrow Cambridge kitchen with a canvas on the sink counter and the afternoon light coming sideways across a still life she would never finish. The hand stayed there. It did not move.

From the study across the downstairs hall she heard the particular sound Richard made when he was trying to get up from his chair and the chair was not cooperating — the soft grunt, the scrape of the leg against the floorboard, the pause, the second attempt. He would call her in a moment. He did not like being heard calling her; the call would be quiet.

"Eileen?"

She did not answer immediately. The hand was still on the bar. She looked at the nine canvases turned to the wall and she looked at the palette knife and she looked at the easel her hand was flat against, and then she lifted the hand away with the small care a person uses laying down something that did not belong to her in the first place. She turned off the floor lamp. She left the room and closed the door behind her, and her footsteps across the hall were even and unhurried, the footsteps of a woman on her way to the study.

"I'm here," she said. "I'm coming."

**END CHAPTER 17**


CHAPTER 18: THE TOWN WEIGHS IN

By the last week of April the town had made up its mind without anyone calling a vote.

Grace Johnson felt it first at the Star Market on Route 20 with a ten-pound sack of Maine potatoes in the crook of her arm. The plastic netting was cutting a red line across the inside of her elbow and she was waiting her turn at register three. The cashier — Donna, the daughter of the older Donna who had worked this same register since Grace was a girl — rang the potatoes through without looking at them.

"Nick nervous?"

"He's fine."

"My Tommy says the whole school's picking sides."

"Tommy's a sophomore."

"Still."

She took her change in quarters because Donna was out of ones, and on the way to the door the pharmacist behind the raised counter of the Osco lifted two fingers and called across the shampoo aisle — *tell him good luck for us, Grace* — the way he might have said it at a wake. Grace lifted two fingers back. She did not break stride.

She put the potatoes in the back of the wagon and stood for a second with her hand on the tailgate, looking at the parking lot. A boy maybe nine years old was pushing a line of carts toward the corral. Two old men were talking by the ice machine. Nothing was happening. Everything was happening.

---

Tuesday was her one day a week off from Friendly's and she sat at the Sudbury Diner on Route 20 the way she had sat there every Tuesday for six years, in the back booth where the vinyl was split along the seam and the crack had been mended with a piece of black electrical tape. The Globe was open in front of her. The Red Sox had lost to the Yankees again.

The voices came over the top of the newsprint.

*— real talent versus noise —*

*— Marsh boy's had lessons since he was six —*

*— heart doesn't win anything —*

She did not look up. She turned the page. A Bradlees ad for lawn chairs.

"Grace."

Mary Murphy sat down across from her without asking. Mary's husband drove for the post office and had a brown Chevy Impala with a dented right-rear quarter panel where he'd backed it into the mailbox at the Fairbank Road turnaround in March and never gotten it fixed, and you could see the car from the diner window right now, the dent catching the late sun. Their son Stephen played bass in Nick's band.

"He holding up?"

"He's fine."

"Stephen says they're in the garage six hours a night."

"Something like that."

Mary leaned in. Her voice dropped half an inch. "Frank at the barber shop's got a pool going."

"On what."

"On Nick."

"Jesus, Mary."

"My Dennis put ten dollars down."

The waitress came by with the pot. The button over her apron pocket was a white circle with red letters — **WBCN 104.1 — THE ROCK OF BOSTON** — with the peace-sign logo underneath, slightly crooked where the pin had gone in a dozen times. She topped Grace's cup without asking, topped Mary's, moved on to the booth behind.

"The Weekly ran it again yesterday," Mary said. "Front page."

Grace had seen it. The photograph was Nick in the garage, three-quarters to the light from the open door, Dan's tools outlined in black marker on the pegboard behind him and the 1975 Aerosmith calendar hanging exactly where it had hung for four years. *BATTLE OF THE BANDS: TWO STUDENTS, TWO WORLDS, ONE STAGE.* Sponsored in part, the last paragraph said, by WBCN-FM. The reporter had called three times before Grace picked up. She had given him seven sentences.

"Dr. Marsh didn't like it," Mary said. "My neighbor works in the office. He called the school. Said they were turning a talent show into class warfare." Mary put quotes around *class warfare* with two fingers on each hand.

Grace sipped her coffee.

"There's a PTA meeting Thursday night. Emergency session. He's going to try to cancel it."

Grace set the cup down.

"Cancel it."

"Postpone until fall. For the kids' own good. You know the song."

"The Battle's in two weeks."

"I know."

Grace looked across the booth at Mary Murphy. Fifteen years they had lived three streets apart. Fifteen years of Little League and parent-teacher nights and hand-me-down snowsuits. Mary's house was the same house as Grace's house — the same square of chain-link, the same sagging gutter over the back door — paid for one month at a time.

"I work Thursday," Grace said.

"Get it off."

"I can't."

"Grace."

"I'll get it off."

---

Thursday the Lincoln-Sudbury library smelled the way it had smelled in 1953 — lemon oil the librarian rubbed into the oak shelves every morning before the doors opened, varnish gone amber on the long card catalog by the reference desk, paper and dust and the faint electric warmth of the fluorescent tubes overhead. Grace had checked out a copy of *The Yearling* from these shelves in the spring she turned fifteen. She had forgotten until she stepped through the door and the smell came up and found her.

She did not think about it for long. She did not have time.

The room was full. The good oak chairs filled first, in rows facing the librarian's desk; then the metal folding chairs along the walls, scraped out of the supply closet; then parents standing in the doorway. Someone's baby was fussing at the back. The PA system crackled once — a cough of static — and went quiet. Principal Sargent sat at the front with his hands flat on a legal pad, the expression of a man who had not asked for this.

Grace came straight from work. She stood along the side wall next to a radiator that was ticking. Brown polyester uniform. *GRACE — MANAGER* over her left breast. The smell of fryer oil was on her whether she wanted it to be or not.

Dr. Marsh sat in the front row with his wife. Blazer, gray slacks, the easy posture of a man who had sat on a dozen nonprofit boards and knew how long to wait before raising a hand. He raised it.

"Thank you, Brad." He used the first name the way a man uses a key he has been given. "I think we all want the same thing. And I think we all know that this — competition — has become something none of us signed up for. Students skipping class. Arguments in the hallway. A newspaper poll asking seventeen-year-olds to pick a side as if this were a selectman's race."

Around the room, heads moved. A woman two chairs over pressed her lips together and nodded once. A man in a Red Sox windbreaker folded his arms across his chest.

"What troubles me most," Dr. Marsh said, "is that we have let a talent show turn into a referendum. On what, I'll let each of you decide. I am asking this board to postpone the Battle of the Bands until the fall, when cooler heads —"

Grace said, "Excuse me."

Her voice was not loud. It carried because the room had gone still for it.

Principal Sargent turned his head. "Mrs. Johnson."

She walked down the center aisle between the oak chairs. She was conscious of her sneakers on the waxed floor and the fryer smell coming off her uniform and the fact that she had not had a sentence ready when she left her car. She stopped at the front. She did not go behind the little lectern Sargent had set up. She put her right hand on the back of the nearest oak chair and gripped it. Her knuckles went white around the wood.

Dr. Marsh, half-risen, sat back down. His right hand lifted and moved to his left breast pocket as if to take something out of it. The pocket was empty. His hand rested there a second and then came down to his knee.

"I'm Grace Johnson," Grace said. "Nick's mother. I work at Friendly's. I worked a double today."
