She stopped. The coffee she had drunk in the car an hour ago had gone cold in her mouth and she worked it down once before she went on.

"Dr. Marsh is right. This stopped being about a talent show. Our kids didn't do that. We did."

A chair creaked at the back.

"My son plays a twenty-five-dollar guitar in a garage his father built. His father is dead. He works Saturdays at a body shop for strings. Two weeks from now he gets to stand on a stage and play for five hundred dollars and a day in a studio. That's not nothing. That's the whole thing."

She looked at Dr. Marsh. She did not make a speech of it.

"You want to postpone it until fall. Fall is when Jackie goes to conservatory. Fall is when my son is fixing cars."

Someone in the metal folding chairs said, quiet, *that's right.*

Grace's hand was still on the oak chair. She could feel the grain under her thumb. The baby at the back had stopped fussing.

"Let them play," she said. "Win or lose. Let them play."

She did not walk back to the side wall. She walked down the aisle and out the double doors and into the parking lot. The sodium lights had come on. Her hands were shaking by the time she got the key into the door of the wagon, and she sat behind the wheel with both hands on it for a minute before she turned the ignition.

She drove home down Route 20 past the lit sign of the Star Market and the dark sign of the feed store, and the speedometer needle sat an even five miles over the limit the whole way, because that was the speed she drove.

---

Mary called at ten-forty.

"Forty-two to eleven."

"Alright."

"Grace. You should've seen his face."

"I'm glad."

"Brad called the vote five minutes after you walked out. Wasn't even close."

"Alright, Mary."

"That was the bravest —"

"I have to be there at five-forty."

A pause on the line.

"Get some sleep," Mary said.

"You too."

Grace set the receiver back in the cradle of the rotary phone on the kitchen wall, and the small internal click of the hanger-bar dropping into place was the last sound in the house. The kitchen clock ticked. Upstairs the garage amp was off. She stood with her hand still on the receiver for a second, then she turned off the kitchen light and went up the stairs in the dark the way she had gone up them ten thousand times, her right hand on the banister where the varnish had worn thin to the grain under three generations of Johnson hands and her own.

**END CHAPTER 18**


CHAPTER 18A: THE STATION MEETING

Two hours and forty-one minutes.

Grace Johnson just left a PTA meeting in her Friendly's uniform and told a room full of professionals what they didn't want to hear. Forty-two to eleven. Not even close. I told you that woman had steel in her spine.

I pour another inch of Pappy. Just an inch. I need to be sharp enough to finish this story and drunk enough to tell the truth.

"You know what the difference is between a rebel and a sellout?" I say into the microphone. "About one meeting. One conference room. One moment when you trade what you believe for what they'll approve. That's all it takes. You walk in pure and walk out with a company line, and it happens so smooth you don't feel the cut until you're bleeding all over someone else's floor."

I lean back and the chair protests, and I think: I know how it feels.

"The fall of '76. Two and a half years before the Battle of the Bands, six months before I walked out of WBCN for good, a Tuesday morning meeting I thought was going to be about fall programming. Let me tell you about the meeting that changed everything."

---

WBCN's management office sat on the third floor above the studios — a room that had once been somebody's idea of cool and had aged into somebody's idea of a health code violation. The carpet was the color of a bruise, stained with decades of spilled coffee and dropped cigarette ash. The walls held framed gold records from bands the station had broken, hung at angles that suggested whoever mounted them had been in a hurry or half in the bag. Aerosmith. J. Geils. Boston. The Cars. Each frame a trophy case for a war we'd already won and were about to surrender.

The conference table was a slab of pressboard that buckled in the middle, covered in ring stains from mugs that had been set down and picked up by ten years of people debating what rock and roll owed to its audience. I'd sat at this table when we decided to give Aerosmith their first heavy rotation. I'd sat here when we broke The Cars and the phones rang for three days straight. I'd argued at this table, cursed at this table, once threw a promotional ashtray at Tom Andrews across this table because he wanted to put a jingle between Led Zeppelin and Black Sabbath.

This table was where the real work happened. And now it was where the real work was going to die.

The meeting started at ten AM. Charlie Morris — station manager, joint-smoker, believer in the power of letting DJs run free — sat at the head, which told me nothing was wrong. But the man sitting to Charlie's right told me everything was wrong.

His name was Gerald Kowalski. New Program Director, hired six weeks earlier from a station in New York City. Late thirties, sharp jaw, hair parted with the mathematical precision of someone who'd never in his life played an instrument or felt the floor shake under a Marshall stack. He wore a suit in a building where nobody wore suits. He carried a leather portfolio the color of money. His shoes were Italian and polished to a shine that reflected the overhead fluorescents like a funhouse mirror.

I hated him on sight, which I understand isn't fair. But I've spent my life trusting my instincts about people the same way I trust them about music, and Gerald Kowalski's frequency was static. Pure, manufactured, corporate static. I would be gone from this building in six months. I did not know that yet. He did.

Tom Andrews sat across from me. Tom — who'd been at WBCN since '71, who'd broken J. Geils when Peter Wolf was still his overnight DJ roommate, who'd once stayed on the air for twenty-seven straight hours during a blizzard because he believed the city needed rock and roll more than it needed sleep. Tom caught my eye and gave me the smallest nod that said: I know. Hang on.

"Let's talk about the future," Kowalski said.

He opened his portfolio and spread papers across the buckled table like a general deploying troops. Charts. Graphs. Demographic breakdowns printed in the kind of typeface that makes everything look scientific and nothing feel human. I could see columns of numbers, age ranges, household income brackets, pie charts sliced into segments labeled 18-24, 25-34, Male, Female, College-Educated, Blue-Collar.

"WBCN's core audience is aging," he said. "Our 25-34 demo is up seven percent, but our 18-24 — your growth market, your future — is down twelve percent in the last eighteen months. The advertisers who matter — automotive, audio equipment, lifestyle brands — they're buying 18-34. That's the sweet spot. That's where the dollars live."

He tapped a chart like a teacher correcting a student.

"We need to grow the 18-34 demo, or we lose the automotive sponsors. Lose the automotive sponsors, we lose the revenue that keeps this station independent. Lose the independence—" He spread his hands and smiled the kind of smile that knows it's winning. "Well. You all know what's happening to independent radio."

We did. Clear Channel was swallowing FM stations like a python digesting rabbits. One by one, independent voices replaced by automated playlists generated by consultants in offices that had never been within a thousand miles of the bands they programmed. Boston had lost three stations in two years. We all knew the python was circling our building.

Charlie tapped his pen on the table. "Gerald has a proposal."

Kowalski pulled out a single sheet. Clean. Typed. The heading read: WBCN COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT INITIATIVE — BATTLE OF THE BANDS.

"Regional high school music competition," he said. "WBCN sponsors. We brand it. We market it to schools within a thirty-mile radius of Boston. Kids enter their bands, we provide judges from our DJ roster, winner gets a recording session and airtime exposure. We sell sponsorship packages — Coca-Cola, local car dealerships, Jordan's Furniture — and position WBCN as the station that discovers the next generation of rock talent."

He paused. Looked around the table with the confidence of a man who knows he's selling something people are going to buy.

"It's community engagement that drives 18-34. It's authentic — local kids, local music, local pride. It's sponsorable — brands want to be associated with youth and discovery. And it's repeatable — we build it as an annual franchise that generates revenue every spring."

The silence in the room lasted about four seconds. The time it takes to cross a line you can't uncross.

"Annual franchise," Tom Andrews said. Not a question. A repetition, the way you repeat a diagnosis you're not ready to accept.

"The data supports it." Kowalski tapped his charts. "Stations that run community engagement programming see a fourteen percent lift in target demos within two rating periods. WJLB in Detroit. KROQ in LA. It's proven."

I looked at Tom. Tom looked at me. And in that look was everything we both understood: this was the moment. This was where the road forked. This was where WBCN either stayed what it was — a station that found real music and played it at full volume because we believed in it — or became what everything else was becoming. A brand.

I opened my mouth. And what came out surprised me.

"What if we use it to actually find talent?"

The table turned to me.

"Not as a brand exercise," I said. "Not as a demographic play. As what WBCN does. Discovery. We go into these schools and we listen. We send DJs who know the difference between a kid who can play and a kid who can make you feel something. We don't look for the next marketable act — we look for the next real thing. The next Aerosmith playing a bar in Allston that nobody knows about yet."

Tom leaned forward. "Maxanne's right. If we do this — if we put our name on it — it has to be authentic. WBCN discovers talent. That's the brand, Gerald. That's what we do. If this turns into a glorified corporate talent show, we lose the one thing that makes us worth sponsoring in the first place."

Kowalski watched us the way a chess player watches an opponent move exactly where he wants them. "I agree completely," he said.

He agreed. That should have been my first warning. When the man in the suit agrees with the rebels, it means the rebels just walked into the cage and closed the door behind themselves.

"Discovery. Authenticity. That's exactly the narrative we sell to sponsors. 'WBCN — Where Legends Begin.' We find talent, we give them a platform, sponsors associate their brands with the discovery process. Everybody wins."

Charlie tapped his pen. "So we're doing this?"

"We're doing this," Kowalski said. "First Battle — spring of '79, let's say, gives us the school-year runway. Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School. I've already reached out to their music teacher, a Bob Wentworth, who's got connections to the local scene. He's enthusiastic. We start regional, prove the model, expand next year." He looked at me. "Maxanne, I want you to judge. You're the face of WBCN discovery. Your credibility makes this real."

My credibility. Like it was a product he could borrow.

"I'll judge," I said. "But I'm writing the rules."

Kowalski's smile didn't waver. "Of course. Tom, you co-draft with Maxanne. Get me something by Friday." He gathered his charts and his portfolio and his Italian shoes and stood. "One more thing. This needs to work. First time, every time. The sponsors are watching. Make it pay."

Make it pay.

He said it the way you'd tell a vending machine to deliver the soda after you've already put in the coins.

The door closed behind him and the room exhaled.

"Well," Charlie said, lighting a joint. "That's the future."

Tom and I sat in silence. The gold records on the wall stared down at us — bands we'd discovered when discovery meant something, when it wasn't a line item in a sponsorship deck.

"We can still make this real, right?" I said. Not to Tom. Not to Charlie. To the room itself, like the walls might answer.

Tom rubbed his eyes. "If we write the rules. If we control who judges. If we keep the sponsors at arm's length and the kids at the center."

"Then we write the rules," I said.

---

I wrote them that night.

Alone in my apartment in Brookline — a one-bedroom with a fire escape that rattled when trucks passed and a kitchen window that looked out on a brick wall close enough to touch. I sat at my kitchen table with a legal pad and a bottle of red wine and wrote the rules for WBCN's first Battle of the Bands like I was drafting a treaty between two countries that didn't know they were at war.

Entry requirements. Judging criteria — originality, emotional connection, technical ability, stage presence, with originality and emotional connection weighted double. Prize structure — five hundred dollars to the winner, and a recording session at WBCN's Studio C for the winner and any runner-up.

And then I got to Article 7, Section 3.

I poured more wine.

Article 7, Section 3: "In the event of a tie in the final round, all tied contestants shall share the studio recording session equally, collaborating on material of their choosing, to demonstrate the spirit of musical cooperation that WBCN's Battle of the Bands is designed to foster."

I read it back. The language was clean, legal, boring. The kind of clause nobody reads in a rules packet. Boilerplate. Standard contest legalese.

Except it wasn't.

I'd already heard the rumors. Two kids from Sudbury — one working-class, one wealthy, once best friends, now enemies — both entering the Battle. Nick Johnson, the guitar kid from Dutton Road with the dead father's garage and the sound that could pull tears out of people who didn't know they were sad. Jackie Marsh, the classically trained prodigy from Maple Street with the Queen belt buckle and the rock and roll heart trapped inside a Juilliard application.

I'd already visited both of them. Already heard what they could do separately. Already imagined — the way you imagine a song before you write it, hearing the ghost of the melody before a single note is played — what they could do together.

The tie clause wasn't boilerplate. It was an ambush. A carefully engineered piece of fine print designed to force two estranged friends into the same recording studio, because I'd convinced myself that if they just played together one more time, they'd remember. They'd hear the frequency they'd lost. They'd find their way back to the harmony that had made a middle school gymnasium stand on its feet four years earlier.

I was engineering reconciliation the way Kowalski engineered demographics. Using rules and structures to produce an outcome I'd already decided was right. Convinced that because my intentions were good — because I genuinely wanted those boys to make music together — the manipulation was justified. Noble, even.

I finished the wine. Slid the legal pad across the table and stared at Article 7, Section 3.

"It's just a contingency," I said to my empty kitchen. "Standard contest procedure. Ties happen."

Ties happen. Right. And lightning happens, and asteroids happen, and sometimes the thing you've been hoping for your entire career just falls into your lap. Except this wasn't going to fall. I was going to push it. I was going to score those boys close enough that the tie clause would trigger, and then I was going to put them in a room and lock the door and call it opportunity.

Because I believed — genuinely, desperately, with every molecule of the woman who'd walked into the Brickyard in 1973 and heard a sound that changed her life — that Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh belonged together. That their separation was a crime against music. That if the world wasn't going to fix this, Maxanne Sartori would.

I turned off the kitchen light. Went to bed. Slept the kind of sleep you only get when you've told yourself a lie you really need to believe.

---

2009. Studio B. My hands around the demo tape.

"That was the meeting," I say into the microphone. "Fall of '76. The day WBCN officially became a business instead of a movement. The day I watched a man with demographic charts convince a room full of rebels that rebellion could be monetized, and the rebels — the rebels meaning me — said yes because we thought we could outrun the cage."

I turn the tape in my hands. The label in my block lettering. Evidence.

"And that night, alone in my kitchen, I wrote the trap. Dressed it up as a contest rule. Told myself it was boilerplate when it was a loaded gun. I wrote Article 7, Section 3 knowing exactly what I was doing, and I lied to myself about it the way people always lie to themselves when they believe the ends justify the means."

The ON AIR light pulses. The station hums its late-night hum, the machinery of broadcast holding steady even as the clock eats what's left.

"The person who rigs the game because they're sure they know better — that person is the hardest to forgive. Because that person is convinced they love you."

I set the tape down.

"That was the moment I became what I'd fought. I just didn't know it yet. It would take two boys, a recording session, and thirty years of midnight silence for me to hear the frequency I'd jammed."

I cue the next track. "Somebody to Love." Queen. Freddie Mercury searching for what we all search for: someone who sees us as we are and doesn't look away.

"Thirty months from that meeting to the Battle of the Bands. Thirty months I spent convincing myself I'd written a fair piece of fine print. Tonight I'm going to tell you what I actually wrote, and why, and who paid for it."

I press play. Freddie's voice fills the booth — pleading, soaring, desperate for connection in a world that keeps handing him perfection when all he wants is something real.

"They don't know what's coming. Neither do I, not really. We all think we're making our own choices. We all think we're steering the ship."

The music climbs.

"We're not. The rules are already written. The cage is already built. And the lock — the lock clicks so quiet you don't hear it until you're already inside."

---

CHAPTER 19: GRACE LOSES HER GRACE

Grace Johnson carried the two pots the way she had carried them for twenty-three years: regular in her right hand, orange collar on the glass; decaf in her left, brown collar. Dr. Taub drank decaf. Had drunk decaf since 1956, the year she started and he was still new to his practice, thirty-two and just back from Korea with his wire-framed glasses and a cardigan his wife had knit him and a bedside manner he had not yet grown into. Grace went to his table with the brown-collared pot already tilted, because at Friendly's on Route 20 you did not ask Dr. Taub which coffee he wanted. You poured the decaf and you said good morning, Dr. Taub, and you did not stand any longer at his booth than the pour required.

Booth twelve was the booth by the window. The window looked out on the parking lot and the Route 20 traffic going east toward Wayland, and the Impala in the second row and Grace's wood-paneled '67 station wagon in the first. Above her, the fluorescent flickered the way it had flickered since Tom Andrews had taken over in '71 and failed to fix it. The ice cream case against the back wall hummed through its blue light. MINT CHOCOLATE CHIP, the little cardboard flag said in its Friendly's-green script, stuck into a tub gone frost-bristled at the edges. At booth seven a kid was working on a Jim Dandy with a long spoon. A booth down from him, two cops split a Fishamajig basket. The air was what the air at Friendly's always was — fryer oil laid over coffee laid over the bleach on the rag in her apron pocket.

Behind the counter Alma was working the ice-cream end. Every scoop the same motion — dip the scoop in the jar of hot water by the tub, shake once, cut the ice cream flat across the top, roll, plate. The scoop went back into the hot water with a small ringing clink against the glass, the sound the morning had every forty seconds, steady as a metronome. Over the pass-through Tom had put up the Saturday chalkboard: TUNA MELT $1.55 / FRIBBLE $0.85. The chalk had been rewritten enough times that the green board showed through in the loops of the Fs. Grace's apron pocket held a ticket book, two pens, a quarter-roll of Life Savers gone soft in the wrapper, and the wet rag. It rode against her hip. She did not notice it. She had stopped noticing it in 1961.

Dr. Taub had the Sudbury Weekly folded open to page three. The picture on page three was Nick, head down over the Les Paul copy in the music-room shot they had run with the WBCN story, the same shot that had been on WBCN's newsbrief last Thursday when the PTA fight went statewide for eleven seconds. Dr. Taub was a man of seventy now. The suit jacket hung over the back of the booth. Under it he wore the same kind of cardigan he had worn when she was twelve and his wife had been Ruth Lindstrom, in the L-S class of '57 two years behind her, and the two of them had sat at the same lunch table in a cafeteria that did not exist anymore. He did not remember that. He had never needed to.

The man across the booth from him was another doctor — younger, maybe fifty, a newer stethoscope-handler from the Emerson group — and he was asking Dr. Taub something about the paper in his hand, and Grace was at booth eleven with the orange-collared pot, topping the cup of an old man in a Wayland Country Club windbreaker who did not look up.

"Came in on the train from Boston in '56," Dr. Taub was saying, over her shoulder, not to her. "People were still talking then. Some of these families — you'd be surprised. That's all I'll say."

She did not turn her head. She poured the regular. She said thank you to the Country Club man, who had not said anything to thank her for, and she walked back to the counter, past the ice cream case and the chalkboard and the hot-water jar that rang once again as the scoop went in, and she set the orange-collared pot on the warmer and lifted the brown-collared one off its plate. On the shelf under the register, next to the box of straws, the frosted glass jar of Maxwell House instant sat where it had sat every day she had worked here, and the shape of the jar — squat, shouldered, a little amber through the frost — was the shape of a jar a man had once kept on the sideboard of a study in a big house on the other side of Hop Brook a long time ago, and for half a breath she was eight years old in a doorway watching him close the lid and label something and look up and smile before he remembered he was supposed to be working.

She took the decaf to booth twelve.

"Grace," Dr. Taub said. He did not look up from the paper. "I've been reading about this Battle."

"Yes, Dr. Taub." She poured. Brown collar. Steady.

"I worry about these boys. Yours. The Marsh boy." He touched the photograph with his index finger, the way he touched a chart. "The competition sets them up. Expectations. Talent alone — you know — it takes training. Connections. Most of these kids are going to be disappointed."

There was a small brown stain on the lapel of his cardigan he had not noticed. Coffee, or something coffee-adjacent, from a morning older than today. She noticed it.

"Disappointed," she said.

"I delivered that boy, Grace." He said it the way a man lays down a trump. "I delivered Nick in March of '62 at Emerson. I'm not against him. I'm *for* realism."

The pot in her left hand was half full. She did not set it down. She did not slam it. She held it.

"My son has been playing guitar since he was eight."

She said it the way she would have said, *the oven's on.* And she was looking, as she said it, past Dr. Taub's cardigan shoulder, through the window of booth twelve, and the late-morning sun on the parking lot caught the chrome handle of the station wagon in a flash she would not have let herself notice any other day — the handle of her third car, the wagon she'd bought used in '74 after Dan's truck needed a clutch, and for a single beat the light on the chrome was the light off a different door handle on a different car in a different driveway a long time ago, and she swallowed around it and did not let her voice shake.

"He practices every day in the garage his father built. He's won every talent show he's entered since he was nine. WBCN called my house. Not his. Mine. And you're drinking decaf in my section at eleven in the morning and telling me he's going to be disappointed."

Dr. Taub opened his mouth.

"Enjoy your coffee, Dr. Taub."

She moved to booth seven. Jim Dandy kid. "You need anything, honey?" He shook his head. She moved to the cops. Refilled them both. Regular. Orange collar. She did not look back at booth twelve. She did not need to. The whole dining room had gone a shade quieter, the way a pond goes quiet when something has fallen in it.

---

Tom Andrews was forty-eight and had been the Friendly's manager on Route 20 for eight of those years. His office was a cinder-block closet behind the walk-in, with a gray metal desk, a swivel chair with duct tape on the arm, a bank calendar from Middlesex Savings still turned to March. He had grown up on Horse Pond Road. He had poured concrete on the same crew as Dan Johnson for two summers in the early sixties, and in the summer of '66 he and Dan and three other guys had spent a Saturday on the Friendly's back roof with buckets of hot tar for cash-in-the-envelope off the then-manager, the same roof over his head right now. He had been at the funeral.

"Sit down, Grace."

"I'll stand, Tom."

"Sit down."

She sat.

He did not look at her while he said the sentences his job required him to say. Professionalism. Customer relations. The uniform meaning something. The word *uniform* was doing work in his mouth that the word was not built for. He finished. He set both hands flat on the desk.

"Fifteen more cents an hour."

"Thanks, Tom."

"Get back to work, Grace."

"Yes, sir."

She stood. She untied and retied the apron. Her name tag had come loose on the left pin; she straightened it. GRACE, in the raised plastic letters, and under it MANAGER, and the small gold foil star the company had sent in February. She went back out to the floor.

---

He came in at four-ten, through the glass door with the Friendly's-cone sticker on it, and he stood by the register with his L-S jacket on and his hands in his pockets and his weight on his right leg. Dan had stood like that. Waiting for Grace to finish a shift, Dan had always tilted the weight off the bad knee, and Nick had never once in his seventeen years been told to copy the stance, and there it was. Grace saw it from booth four, where she was wiping syrup off a formica edge, and went still for a half-second and then kept wiping.

He crossed to her.

"Mom."

"It's fine, Nick."

"I heard."

"You deserve a chance."

"You didn't have to do that."

"Yes, I did."

She folded the rag. She did not look at him right away. When she did, his face had the Friendly's sign in the window of it — the cursive neon red reflected on the lens of his reading glasses, which were her reading glasses, which he had grabbed off the counter when he came in, and he did not know he was wearing them. She almost said so. She didn't.

"Wait on the bench by the door," she said. "Two minutes."

He waited on the bench by the door. She took the apron off in the back. She hung it on the hook that had her name taped above it in Tom's block print. She clocked out. She walked past the ice cream case with its blue light and the mint chocolate chip and the Jim Dandy sign and out through the glass door, and Nick stood up off the bench and walked out beside her, and above them the Friendly's sign in its ice-cream-cone cursive threw red over the hood of the station wagon and red over the shoulders of her polyester and red, a softer red, over the side of Nick's face. Eight days to the Battle.

They did not speak in the parking lot. He opened the driver's side for her the way Dan used to. She got in. He walked around to the passenger side. For a moment, before he opened his door, he stood in the lot with his hand on the roof of the wagon and looked back at the diner where his mother had just, without once saying the word, defended him in front of the doctor who had delivered him and the town that had watched her serve coffee for twenty-three years.

Then he got in, and she started the engine, and they went home up Route 20 with the red sign fading in the rearview and the spring light going long over the Cavicchio fields.

---

Monday morning she stopped at the Store 24 on Route 20 for milk and a can of Bustelo. The fluorescents hummed over the magazine rack; the clerk was reading *People* with her chin on her hand. At the back cooler Grace lifted a gallon of Hood whole and turned with it, and on the other side of the snack aisle two women in windbreakers were talking over the top of the Fritos rack.

"— walked right up to him at booth twelve, Carol. Poured the coffee and let him *have* it. My Linda was at booth nine, heard the whole thing —"

"Grace Johnson?"

"Yes, Grace —"

"Well, good for her."

"I'm just saying. *Taub.* After all these years."

Grace stood with the gallon in her left hand and the can of Bustelo in the crook of her right arm. She did not turn her head. She walked to the register. The clerk put down the magazine. Two dollars and eleven cents, the clerk said, and Grace laid three singles on the counter and took her change in nickels and dimes and did not say thank you out loud, only nodded, and went out the door with the bell still ringing above it. The windbreakers were still talking when the door shut.

In the parking lot she set the milk on the passenger seat. The Bustelo she set on the floor where it would not roll. She sat for a second with her hand on the wheel. A Sudbury Weekly delivery van went by on 20, the kid behind the wheel flipping bundles onto the Store 24 stoop on his way around. The top bundle, as the van pulled away, showed the Monday front page folded once down the middle. She could not read the headline from where she sat and did not need to.

She started the engine. She drove home.

**END CHAPTER 19**


CHAPTER 20: TEACHERS WHO FANNED THE FLAMES

Tuesday afternoon, May 1979, ten days before the Battle. Bob Wentworth had the jazz band in a horseshoe — seventeen kids on folding chairs in the cinder-block room off the east wing, ballast buzz in the fluorescents, dust motes turning in the late light that came slantwise through the one high window. The posters on the wall were a small argument he had been having with himself since 1962. Ellington at the Blue Note. Miles in shadow, the Harmon mute at his lips. Bernstein with his baton down. A tasteful cross-section, or that was what he had told his wife when he tacked them up. A man's divided loyalties in frames.

The Yamaha upright stood against the long wall, lid open, middle C pitted where ten years of middle-school hands had found it first. Nick's Marshall practice amp sat on a chair by the piano, not plugged in yet, the cord coiled on top with the precision of a boy who owned one cord. Music stands at seventeen different heights. A broken maraca on the shelf above the tuba case, the handle snapped off at the bead, which Wentworth kept for reasons he could not articulate. The room smelled of old valve oil and floor wax.

He had the photograph in his drawer. He had been thinking about it all period.

"Put the scales away," he said. He set a stapled two-page chart on each of their music stands. "Brubeck. 'Take Five.' Nick, Jackie — just you two. The rest of you listen."

Seventeen heads came up.

Nick was by the amp, not looking at the piano. Jackie was already on the bench, not looking at the amp. They had not spoken, Wentworth knew, since October. Two Aprils of careful air between them in a building with two hundred and forty feet of corridor.

"Mr. Wentworth," Nick said. He had not picked up the guitar. "I don't think —"

"Five-four," Wentworth said. "One, two, three, four, five. The off-balance is the point. You lean into it. Just see."

Jackie put his hands on the keys. Nick, because there was nowhere else for his hands to go, plugged in.

---

The left hand came first. D, E-flat, F — ostinato, walking — the Brubeck bass figure the way the record had it, Jackie's fingers finding the shape clean on the first pass because classical training had given him the 5/4 the way it had given him everything, for free. The ostinato settled into the room like a heartbeat that had decided to come in a pattern nobody's body expected. One, two, three, four, five. One, two, three, four, five.

Nick listened for four bars. He found the crack between the four and the five and he put a bent blue note in it. Low on the neck. Not loud. The string was slightly flat and he let it be flat. Another bar of listening, and then he threaded the second phrase, longer, letting the time signature lean him sideways and catching himself on a chord, and the chord was not a Brubeck chord, it was an Aerosmith chord, and it sat down in Jackie's ostinato like it had been waiting there.

Jackie's right hand came in above it. Not flourish — restraint. A single high note held, then a run, then held again. He was leaving Nick room.

Nick stepped into the room Jackie left him.

For thirty seconds it was a working thing. The off-balance found its center the way a bicycle finds its center, not by stopping the wobble but by leaning into it. In the horseshoe one kid — Brian Castellano, second chair trumpet — stopped chewing his gum and his jaw hung half-open and he did not remember to close it. In the corner, on flute, Patty Lally from Sudbury Southeast had been humming something of her own under her breath the whole period, a Fleetwood Mac melody she didn't know she was humming, and the hum was going now, quiet, underneath the 5/4 like a third voice the room had not been asked to provide. She lowered the flute to her lap. She did not lift it again.

Wentworth did not move. He was not sure he was breathing. The ballast buzz was the only thing in the building that did not know what was happening in the room.

---

The door whooshed on its hydraulic hinge.

Don Gould came in the way Don Gould came in — Northeastern coffee mug in the left hand, jeans, sleeves rolled to the elbow, wool tie loose at the collar, the AP American History grin he wore on days he thought he was going to teach somebody something. He stopped in the doorway long enough to register the music, which was still happening, the ostinato walking under him, and then he smiled wider.

Patty Lally's hum stopped.

"You know what this is?" Gould said, loud, over the music that was still going. "This is the Lincoln-Douglas debates, right here."

Nick's hand came off the strings.

Jackie's ostinato carried one more bar on its own, alone, the D-E-flat-F figure with nothing on top of it, and then Jackie's left hand stopped too, the last note hanging and dying into the ballast buzz.

---

Nick set his pick down on the amp. He set it down carefully, because if he had not set it down carefully he would have thrown it.

"We were playing music," he said.

Gould was still smiling. "Right, but — the tension, Nick, the tension between —"

"Between the kids who matter and the kids who don't." Nick stood. The strap caught on the music stand; the stand went over; the Brubeck chart went under a chair. He didn't pick it up. "Between the kids whose parents donate the new risers and the kids whose parents die in a trailer the company blames on the guy who was inside it."

Gould's smile did a thing. It did not go away. It got confused at the edges.

"You want a lesson?" Nick was unplugging the cord now. His hands knew the cord. His hands did not need his face to do it. "Fine. Here's your lesson. Jackie's going to Juilliard or Yale or wherever. I'm going to work construction like my dad. Maybe die quick like him too. That American enough?"

In the horseshoe, Patty Lally looked down at the silver keys of the flute in her lap. Her thumb moved on the G key and stopped. Something in her face went somewhere it had not been before.

"Nick." Jackie's voice was small. He had not stood up. His hands had come off the keyboard and were flat on his thighs.

"What."

"Don't."

"Don't what. Don't say it in front of people? Your dad said it in front of people. *These boys don't understand boundaries.* On the bridge. In front of my friends. In front of me."

Jackie's hands came off his thighs and went back to the keys and did not press anything. The fluorescent buzzed. One of the tubes up near the door was going; it ticked twice, a small electric tick, and then buzzed again.

"I was trying to avoid this." Jackie's voice had gone quieter, not louder. The precision was starting to crack at the edges the way it did when he was tired. "I thought maybe we could get through one week without —"

"Without what. Without you stepping aside like a good little prince on a wooden bridge. Without your Queen covers being you slumming with real people."

"Slumming." Jackie said the word like it was a small heavy object somebody had handed him. He did not look up. His breath came in once and did not come out evenly.

He stood. The piano bench scraped on the tile, the short ugly sound of oak on waxed linoleum, and the sound was loud because the room had gone so quiet around it. He shut the fallboard. He did not slam it. He set it down the way his mother set a china cup down.

"See you Friday," Jackie said.

That was all. He walked out. The door went on its hinge and the hinge whooshed and closed on the soft seal.

Nick was still holding the cord.

---

Wentworth got them out of the room. He did not remember, afterward, what he said to do it — some sentence with the word *dismissed* in it, probably — but the seventeen chairs emptied, folders under arms, and the last kid out was Patty Lally, who paused at the door with the flute case under her elbow and looked back once at the piano bench and then was gone.

The door closed on its seal.

The ballast buzz was still the only sound.

Wentworth walked past the Yamaha, past Nick's amp with its coiled cord, past the Brubeck chart under the chair, and went around behind the piano to the stand where the trumpet had been since September, in its black case, and had not been out of its case in fourteen months. He opened the case. The felt lining had gone bald at the bell end. The valve oil bottle on the inside of the lid was three-quarters down, where he had left it.

He took the horn out. He set the mouthpiece in. The mouthpiece was cold. He wet his lips. He raised the horn. The ring on the bell said *Bach Stradivarius 37* in the same small script it had said it in at Jordan Hall in March of 1958.

He blew one note.

It was not a good note. It was an F, and the F sagged under his breath because the embouchure had gone and the lip had gone and the air had gone, and the note came out of the bell and went nowhere. Nobody heard it. The room ate it.

He set the horn back in the case. He closed the case.

He sat down on the piano bench Jackie had left.

**END CHAPTER 20**


CHAPTER 21: NICK'S REHEARSAL

The garage bay door stood up to the chest-high stop, lifted halfway for the air — the fourteenth of May in Sudbury, sixty-five degrees if it was a degree, the kind of spring night that felt warm only because April had set the bar so low. Crickets had started in the grass by the oil stain. Behind the Cavicchio fence a spring peeper was going in the wet low place, one note at a time, not fast. A Chevy with a bad belt whined down Dutton Road and faded toward the Horse Pond light.

Inside the garage the air was forty watts worse than outside. One bare bulb on a length of exposed Romex swung a finger's width when a kid walked under it. The Marshall practice amp on the milk crate was warm enough to put a palm on. The guitar case was open on the concrete, cables heaped in it like rope in a dinghy.

Nick stood in the middle of it with the twenty-five-dollar acoustic he'd walked out of a pawn shop in Framingham with the summer he was fourteen — four springs ago, Dan still around for one more year, the price Dan had paid because he'd said *don't tell your mother* and then told her at dinner that night himself. The guitar had a crack in the upper bout Nick had packed with wood glue the summer he was fifteen. His fingertips knew every raised grain on the neck.

Stephen Murphy had his Univox Hi-Flyer plugged into a little Kustom 2x12 borrowed off his cousin in Hudson. Univox was the cheap copy of the Mosrite the real players in Boston clubs used, and Stephen had saved for it out of a paper route and three-fifty an hour at the Sunoco on 126. It was the color of a Ford brake light. He'd tuned it four times already.

David Cassel sat on a crate behind a Ludwig four-piece his uncle had handed down, one cracked crash rivetted back together with a brass washer his father had drilled out on the basement bench. Mark Pianka had a Rhodes on loan from Mr. Wentworth — a Stage Piano out of the music-room closet that buzzed on the B below middle C if you leaned on it. Mark had figured out not to lean on it, mostly.

"One more time," Nick said.

"We've done it four times," David said.

"One more."

They ran "Dream On." Stephen's bass locked in under Mark's keyboard figure. David's snare hit was quieter tonight than it would be tomorrow — the neighbors closer at a milk crate's remove than they'd be at the back of an auditorium. Nick sang.

When they finished, Stephen laid the Univox flat on his thigh.

"Nick."

"Yeah."

"Whatever happens tomorrow. We're with you. Not because of your mom at Friendly's. You're the reason any of us started playing."

Mark was wiping Rhodes-sweat off the F key. "Freshman year you were the only one who didn't laugh when I came in with my grandmother's Magnus chord organ. You said *can you play it.*"

David twirled a stick between his fingers and watched it. "You taught me the grip. Three lunches in a row, the music room."

"So tomorrow," Stephen said, "we play. Screw the rest."

Nick nodded once. He did not trust his voice yet.

"One more," he said, when he did. "Then go."

They ran it one more time. Then the Kustom cut and the Rhodes cut, and David laid the sticks crossed on the snare head the way a carpenter lays a level down, and they were packing up.

At the curb Stephen's father's '74 Nova sat idling with the headlights aimed at the garage. Mark's brother was five minutes out. David lived a quarter mile down Dutton and was walking. The three of them went out under the half-raised bay door with their cases and their cords, and at the end of the driveway Stephen said *Nick,* once, and Nick lifted two fingers off the neck of the acoustic, and Stephen got in the Nova and the Nova pulled away down Dutton and the headlights took the last of themselves with them.

The crickets came back up.

---

Grace stood on the back step in her Friendly's uniform with her hand on the storm-door handle and did not open it.

The laundry line ran from the porch post to the maple in the side yard and the line was empty — she had brought the sheets in at six the way she had brought them in every Thursday for twenty-three years. Across Dutton Road on the Connolly porch the orange cat sat on the railing with its tail hanging over the mailbox side. Up by the mill the Chevy with the bad belt passed again, coming back. The same one. The belt did the same thing.

From the open garage she could hear him finishing out. The kids were gone. It was just the one guitar now.

She was afraid of what he would do if he lost. She was afraid of what he would do if he won. Both fears were the same fear, and both fears had her hand on the chrome storm-door handle of the house Dan had grown up in — the house she had walked into as a bride twenty-three years ago carrying a casserole and a suitcase, and had never walked out of since. She did not open the door. She stood with her hand on the handle until the chrome warmed. Then she took her hand off and went in.

The storm door shut behind her on its own hinge.

---

In the garage Nick stood alone in the forty-watt yellow.

His left hand hurt. The pads of his fingers had gone past sting to something that was not quite numb. The high E bit him a little every time he landed on it, and he landed on it a lot, because "Dream On" started there and the bite got worse as the night went. His grip on the neck tightened and he made himself open the hand twice and close it.

He bent and picked up a pick off the concrete — Fender medium, the logo worn white down one side where his thumb had been. He turned it over. The other side still had the tortoise pattern readable. He set it on the Marshall next to the one already there and stood up.

The toolbox sat where it had sat for eighteen months, under the 1975 April calendar, red paint chipped at the corners, latch shut. On the Maxwell House lid beside it, the 9/16ths socket. Nick glanced at the latch once. Still shut. He did not go near it.

He sat on the milk crate. He put the acoustic across his lap. He worked his left hand open and shut twice, the way Dan had taught him to do when the tendon got tight — *shake it out, Nicky, don't fight it, shake it out —* and then he laid his fingers on the neck and played the piano intro to "Dream On" on six strings of gut and wire.

On the record there was a piano on the intro — Tyler himself, one finger at a time, the way Nick had read it in Circus Magazine two summers ago on the floor of the garage. Nick played the piano part on the twenty-five-dollar acoustic because there was no piano in the garage and because it was the only way he could play it alone. His right thumb picked the melody low on the neck. His left hand fretted slow. When he came to the bend at the end of the first phrase his fingertip bit and he breathed in through his nose and did not stop.

Outside, the crickets had gone quiet.

He did not notice at first. He was in the third phrase before he noticed. The peeper had stopped too. The Chevy with the belt was gone up the road. The night past the half-opened bay was holding still around what was coming out of the garage.

*You got the gift, Nicky.*

The Battle was eighteen hours away.

He played the passage through, alone, the acoustic bright and thin against the pegboard and the bare joists and the concrete, the way Dan had bent over him one Sunday afternoon in the summer he was fourteen and guided his hand on the neck until the fingering came right. When he stopped the crickets started back, first one, then two, then the whole yard, and the peeper came in behind the Cavicchio fence on its slow cadence, and the bare sixty-watt bulb on the Romex swung the width of a finger because a moth had hit it.

Nick set the guitar down in the case on the tangle of cables. He closed the lid. He did not latch it. He reached up and pulled the bay door down to the concrete with both hands, slow, the way Dan had taught him, easing the springs the last six inches so the counterweight did not slam.

On his way to the side door he did not look at the toolbox.

**END CHAPTER 21**


CHAPTER 22: JACKIE'S REHEARSAL

May 14, 1979. Early evening. Eighteen hours to the Battle and Jackie Marsh was alone in the basement on Maple Street, running the intro of "Bohemian Rhapsody" in B-flat because his left hand had asked for it the second he sat down.

The room had been finished the summer he turned fourteen. Foam panels on the walls, the two-inch studio kind — white once, now going yellow at the glued edges the way a paperback goes yellow on a south-facing shelf. A Shure SM58 stood on a boom stand at the center of the floor, black grille, the XLR running in a clean loop to the four-track against the wall — a Tascam, four little red LED indicators glowing dim as pilot lights in a kitchen at night. Against the long wall, the upright. A 1920s Baldwin, its walnut case gone plum-dark at the corners, its ivory top keys chipped in two places from a thumbnail that had been Eileen's mother's in 1931 in a parlor on Beacon Hill. The Steinway lived upstairs in the good light. The Baldwin lived down here with the amps. That had been decided early.

Jackie played the opening six bars and let them stop themselves. His right thumb rested on the D. The ballast in the overhead fluorescent made its small even buzz and the foam drank it.

He had heard about Grace Johnson's speech at Friendly's. Everyone in Sudbury had heard about it by Wednesday. The knowledge sat in his chest the way a swallowed pill sits before it starts to go down.

---

They came in at six-thirty the way they always came in, Tuesday-Thursday-Saturday, the Crown Jewels coming down the side stairs in the order of their cars.

Ace Callahan first, because Ace always came first — the Premier kit was already set up down here, red-sparkle shells, the kick drum head with the black CROWN JEWELS decal he'd rubbed on with a pencil and a sheet of Letraset. He sat and ran a double-stroke roll on the snare without a word and the foam ate most of it. Max Wesslen came in behind him with the Fender Precision — black body, rosewood neck, the bass his father had bought him at E. U. Wurlitzer in the Back Bay for his sixteenth birthday — and plugged into the Ampeg cabinet and played four notes of the Queen bassline to warm the hand.

Elizabeth came last, because she lived upstairs and there was nothing to carry but the case. Thirteen years old. Violin under her arm in its student Gewa case. She sat on the arm of the chair Dr. Marsh sat in and opened the case on her lap and took out the chamois and wiped the rosin dust off the belly of the violin in small slow circles. On the left side of her neck, above the collar of her cardigan, a small pink callus had been forming for two months now where the chinrest lived. She was not aware of it, which was the point. She had cleared the bar so young that nobody had thought to put a bar there. She got grandfathered into freedom before she knew there was a cage.

"From the top," Jackie said.

They ran the Chopin étude first — Opus 10, No. 3, the one called "Tristesse," the one anyone's mother recognized — arranged for four pieces, Jackie's arrangement, the melody in the violin, the harmony voicings on the Baldwin, Max walking the root under Ace's brushes. Jackie had written out every chart in his own hand, the ink neat as a ledger. It came out of the four of them the way a good clock comes out of its case. Nothing missing.

Then "Somebody to Love" in A-flat major — the Mercury gospel piled up in stacked thirds, Elizabeth doubling the top line with the bow, Max and Ace holding the pocket. Then "Bohemian Rhapsody," the whole six-minute architecture, B-flat opening, the operatic middle stacked, the hard-rock vamp, the coda. The harmonies locked. Every chord where it went. The kind of rehearsal a band videotapes and sends to a label.

Dr. Marsh came down during the last verse.

He came down with the leather notebook under his left arm, the one he used for patients and lectures and his children. He did not sit right away. He walked across the carpet behind Jackie, behind Ace's kit, and stood at the Baldwin for a breath — and as the last chord rang Jackie, whose eyes were on the keyboard, did not see his father's right hand come out and settle, and did not see the thumb touch middle C. Not press. Touch. Half a beat. The key did not sound. The thumb came off. His father's hand closed, went to the spine of the notebook, steadied it. Then Dr. Marsh crossed to the chair by the wall and sat, and opened the notebook on his knee.

Jackie played the last resolving chord and lifted his hands.

"The Chopin was excellent," his father said. He was smiling the small careful smile. "Elizabeth, your intonation in the second phrase was exactly right."

Elizabeth said thank you and put the chamois back in its corner of the case.

Dr. Marsh stood. He walked to the Baldwin and rested his hand on Jackie's shoulder, the weight of it careful as a stethoscope.

"The Queen, Jackson. When you play it like that — controlled. Precise. Every note exactly where it belongs — " He paused. He was choosing his word. He found it. "You remind me of Glenn Gould. That kind of technical mastery. Mercury is a talent, but he is undisciplined. You've taken his chaos and made it civilized."

He squeezed the shoulder once. The notebook went back under his arm.

"Keep this up," he said, "and tomorrow will take care of itself."

The side stairs took him back up. The door at the top closed on its soft seal.

Civilized. Jackie sat with the word on him.

---

Ace packed first, because Ace always packed first, sticks into the stick bag, cymbals into the gig bag with a folded bath towel between them. Max coiled his cord over-under and slid the Precision into its case. Goodbyes at the side door. Feet going up the driveway in sneakers and a Chuck Taylor, a car door, a second car door, an engine, another engine. Quiet.

Elizabeth was still on the chair arm.

"You okay."

"Fine."

"Jack."

He looked at her. She had the chamois folded into its small square now and was closing the case in the way she closed books — not snapping the latch, pressing it shut.

"You know he stopped asking me to practice last year," she said. "I'd already done the job. He lets me alone." She looked at the Baldwin, not at him. "But you keep trying to make him hear something. And he doesn't hear music, Jack. He counts notes."

Jackie did not answer.

"Go to bed," she said. "Tomorrow's long."

She took the violin upstairs. The door at the top closed again.

---

He sat on the bench another minute with the fluorescent buzzing and the four little red Tascam LEDs glowing at the wall and the foam doing its patient work on every sound.

Then he played "Bohemian Rhapsody" for himself.

Not the arrangement. The record. He started at the operatic middle section where the voices stack, and he sang the *Galileo, Galileo, Galileo figaro* in his head because you could not sing it in a Marsh basement, and his left hand made the big jumping accompaniment that wasn't in any of the charts, and his right hand did the Mercury flourishes the charts had taken out, and for three minutes he was not an early-admission to The Juilliard School, 144 West 66th Street, New York. The ballast buzzed. The foam drank. The Tascam's LEDs held their small steady red.

Then the song ended.

Classical earned his father's approval. Rock earned his breath. He couldn't have both.

---

He gently lowered the piano lid. The soft wooden click went across the foam and was absorbed before it finished. He killed the basement lights at the switch by the stairs.

He went up.

He passed the study door, open a hand's width — his father at the desk inside, textbooks fanned out in the green pool of the banker's lamp, a pencil in his hand, checking one book against another for tomorrow's lecture. He passed the living room. His mother in the wing chair under the floor lamp, a novel on her lap, her eyes on the page and not on the page. He passed Elizabeth's door, closed, the thin line of her bedside light under it.

His own door closed behind him with the quiet click of a door hung right.

On the desk, the Juilliard envelope lay where it had lain for three weeks, cream laid paper, the letter knife slit along the top. Next to it, face down on the stack of old Curtis programs where he had put it in March, the small silver frame held the photograph he could not look at tonight.

He did not turn the photograph over.

He did not open the envelope again.

He took off his shoes, set them at the foot of the bed, and got under the covers in his clothes.

**END CHAPTER 22**


CHAPTER 23: MAXANNE — THE EVE OF BATTLE

Three hours into the broadcast. One hour left.

The Heaven Hill paper cup has gone soft at the rim. I refill it. My hands on the console are steady now. Guilt doesn't shake you forever. It just sits down beside you and waits for the cue.

The ON AIR sign holds its red like an open wound. Behind the glass, the kid finally put the phone down. Quit scrolling about an hour ago. He's watching me the way you watch an old drunk at a bar who's about to say something true.

The Neve 8068 under my fingers was old when I left this station the first time. Faders worn smooth at the tips from a thousand hands. Coffee ring from '86 still there, exactly where I left it. Studio B still smells like the forty years of cigarette smoke nobody bothered to shampoo out of the walls, even after the state banned it in 2003. Some of that smoke is mine. Pall Malls, '73 to '76. I quit the year I quit WBCN. One vice at a time.

On the wall above the console, the old NAB cart rack — steel frame, thirty slots — is empty. Just the dust shadows where the brown-and-cream carts used to sit. Little gray rectangles. Ghosts of rotation. In '75 I could put my hand on any one of those carts in the dark and know the song by the weight of the tape. Now it's a laptop and a playlist somebody in Texas printed out. The kid runs the laptop. The kid does what the laptop tells him. Nobody in this building has their hand on a song anymore.

The Pink Floyd LP is still cued on the turntable, needle up. Waiting. To my right, the demo cassette: *Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979*. I picked it up an hour ago and I haven't put it down.

---

The Battle of the Bands was my idea.

I want that on the record before we go any further. Not Tom Andrews. Tom ran it. Tom executed. The seed was mine, planted in a pitch meeting in the fall of '76 — regional high school contest, WBCN sponsorship, a recording session for the winners. I sold it to management the way you sell anything to management: find the next Aerosmith before the next Aerosmith needs us. Drive the fifteen-to-twenty-fives. Attract the sponsors who wanted the kids.

Six months later, in April of '77, I left.

Elektra had offered me A&R. More money. More reach. I'd spent seven years in this building breaking bands — Aerosmith out of a dive in Allston, the Cars when every label thought Ric Ocasek was a weirdo with cheekbones. I told myself A&R was the next floor up. Same work, better budget. I was still going to find the next Neighborhoods. I'd just find them with a checkbook in my hand.

The Battle went off without me. Spring of '79. Tom called and asked if I'd sit the judges' panel. Elektra said yes before I did. Scout the room, sign somebody. The industry doesn't care which end of the microphone you hold. It only cares that you keep the line moving.

So I came back.

Not as WBCN. As Elektra. In a blazer I wouldn't have owned two years before.

---

May 15, 1979. Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School auditorium. Four hundred and forty-three seats, every one of them filled, every one of them already decided. I sat on the judges' panel between two people whose names I have let myself forget, clipboard on my knees, pencil in my hand.

One concrete image I've kept for thirty years.

Nick Johnson's hands, before Nick played a note. Thumb flat-picking the low E to tune. Knuckles still raw from a week of rehearsal with no stopping. *Dan Johnson's hands*, I thought. *Nick has his father's hands.*

Across the stage, Jackie Marsh tuning too. Jackie's hands were clean. Long fingers. Surgeon's hands the way the father wanted them. The kind of hands that know what a metronome is.

They did not look at each other. Not during tune. Not during the set. Not once.

Grace was in the third row. Still in the polyester. Name tag *Grace* pinned on the apron she hadn't bothered to take off because she'd come straight from a double at Friendly's. The seat next to her was empty. Dan should have been in it. Everybody in the room who knew pretended not to see the empty seat. I had never met Grace. Never have. I knew her from two rows up the way you know weather.

I wrote the contest rules myself. Two and a half years before the event, at my kitchen table in Brookline the night of Kowalski's meeting, I drafted the tie-breaker clause. Small-print paragraph most panels never read. *In the event of equal scores, the panel may elect to declare co-winners.* I told myself I was being fair. I told myself I was hedging for quality.

What I was doing was leaving a door open.

The clipboard on my knees that night had a scoring rubric the other two judges treated like a church. Technical execution. Arrangement. Stage presence. Originality. Little boxes. Little numbers. Neither side was going to win cleanly on that rubric, and some part of me already knew it. Some part of me had already rigged the ending and was waiting for the other two to catch up.

That's the thing nobody warns you about this job. Sometimes you set a trap months before you meet the people who will walk into it.

---

I didn't create their division.

Hop Brook did that. The cafeteria did that. Dan Johnson's crane cable in November of '77 did that. Dr. Marsh did that, at his kitchen table, in sentences about surgery and sensible futures. Class did that. America did that.

But I took a slow, private death and I put a ticket price on it. I saw two boys drifting and said, let's bring them back together onstage. For content. For sponsors. For a photograph in the Globe.

I didn't kill their friendship. But I gave class warfare a microphone. I sold tickets to watch innocence die. I turned tragedy into entertainment.

And then I called it opportunity.

---

Eleven forty-seven. Thirteen minutes.

Freddie Mercury's voice comes through the monitors from somewhere deeper than analog. He's been gone eighteen years. Thirty years of medicine too late.

I used to tell myself I left this station before I had to compromise. That leaving in '77 was the clean choice. A&R was just a different angle on the same work. I was still looking for the next Neighborhoods. I still had the ear.

Elektra was a cage with better carpet.

I signed the safe ones. I passed on the dangerous ones. I learned to say *demographic* with a straight face. I helped turn the kind of band I used to champion into the kind of band I used to warn kids about. The rebel DJ who wouldn't shut up became a woman in a blazer who filled out quarterlies and measured risk and told herself this was still music.

I went quiet. For thirty-two years.

The station brought me back tonight because nobody else wanted it. I drew short straw. Walked in at nine o'clock to play eulogy for my own life. On paper I'm the guest of honor. In the actual room I'm a mirror.

Four decades of rock radio. Forty-one years, give or take. The brochure says *fifty-six* because somebody in corporate couldn't count and nobody stopped them. Monday morning, 98.5 starts playing contemporary Christian out of this same transmitter. Jesus on the frequency we used to give Patti Smith. I don't know if that's irony or just America.

---

The demo cassette is warm in my palm. *un-Authorized.* Still unplayed. I haven't put it down since the ON AIR light came on.

The Pink Floyd LP waits. *Shine On You Crazy Diamond.* The memo says that's how WBCN goes out. Graceful. Elegiac. Ending by committee.

The clock gives me twelve minutes now.

Before I choose, there are two rooms you need to see. The night before the Battle. May 14, 1979.

Dutton Road first. Nick's garage. His dead father's tools still on the pegboard in the exact order Dan left them — Phillips-heads in descending size, the coffee can of sixteen-penny nails, the wrench set nobody had moved since November of '77. Overhead bulb. Concrete cold through a pair of gym socks. An acoustic guitar worn raw at the twelfth fret because he won't stop playing it even when his fingers leave prints of blood on the strings.

Then across the brook, under the Marsh house. A finished basement. Carpet. Acoustic panels on the walls the color of a manila folder. A Fender Rhodes on a stand. Sheet music in a binder with tabs. A boy whose father is paying for the next fifteen years of his life in advance and expects a receipt.

Two kids. Same hour. Four miles of road and one wooden bridge between them.

Neither of them knowing they'd already lost.

My hands are steady. The engineer behind the glass isn't blinking. I thumb the bourbon cup empty in one swallow, set it down next to the demo tape, and I lean into the mic.

Stay with me.

**END CHAPTER 23**


CHAPTER 23A: PATTY'S DECISION

---

The rooster didn't crow at the Lally place. The pigs handled that — two hundred and sixty Yorkshires grunting their way to consciousness at five-fifteen every morning, a sound like the earth clearing its throat.

Patty Lally pulled on her boots at the back door and stepped into April air thick with the smell of hog waste and wet hay. The southeast corner of Sudbury didn't wake up pretty. It woke up honest.

She'd done morning feed since she was twelve. Five-gallon buckets of grain mixed with kitchen scraps, distributed across eight pens while her father checked water lines and her mother tallied receipts at the kitchen table. The pigs pressed against the gates when they saw her coming — not affection, just hunger wearing a familiar face. Patty didn't mind. There was something clean about a relationship that never pretended to be more than it was.

The Cavicchio farm sprawled three fields east, and when the wind came from that direction the combined stench of two pig operations could stop traffic on Landham Road. People on the west side of Hop Brook called it the smell of Southeast. They meant it as an insult. Patty wore it like a badge.

By seven-forty she'd showered twice, braided her hair still damp, and caught the bus at the corner of Morse and Landham. The shower never fully killed the smell. Soap could mask pig but not erase it. She'd learned this in seventh grade when Cheryl Mancini wrinkled her nose in homeroom and said, loud enough for everyone, "Something smells like bacon." The class laughed. Patty didn't. She looked at Cheryl until the laughter died, and said, "Bacon pays our mortgage." Nobody brought it up again.

Lincoln-Sudbury in April smelled like chalk dust and floor wax and the low-grade panic of seniors counting down to graduation. Patty walked the east hallway toward first period, past lockers tagged with band names and Aerosmith vs. Queen graffiti that the janitor scrubbed off every Friday and that reappeared every Monday, the argument as permanent as the cinder blocks underneath.

She stopped at Mr. Wentworth's music room to drop off a permission slip. The room occupied a corner of the building where acoustics went to die — cinder block walls painted institution green, fluorescent lights buzzing overhead, instrument lockers that smelled like valve oil and teenage spit. But on the corkboard between a yellowed Boston Symphony schedule and a jazz band sign-up sheet that nobody had signed, someone had pinned something new.

Block letters on goldenrod paper.

BATTLE OF THE BANDS
MAY 15, 1979
LINCOLN-SUDBURY REGIONAL HIGH SCHOOL
GRAND PRIZE: $500 AND PROFESSIONAL RECORDING SESSION
SPONSORED BY WBCN "THE ROCK OF BOSTON"

Patty read it twice. Not the prize money — five hundred dollars wouldn't change what she already knew about her music. She read the date. Six weeks away. Six weeks to decide whether she wanted to walk into a war zone carrying a guitar instead of a weapon.

---

She'd been watching them for months.

The cafeteria at Lincoln-Sudbury split like a cell dividing — Nick Johnson's table near the windows where the Rollers and working-class kids ate fast and loud, Jackie Marsh's table by the doors where Rampers debated college applications over sandwiches their mothers had wrapped in wax paper. Between them, thirty feet of linoleum they did not cross.

Patty sat with Kristy Wesslen and Robin Bosshard at a table near the milk coolers — neutral ground, neither camp, claimed by nobody. From there she could see both boys without turning her head. Nick hunched over a tray, jaw working, not talking much, eating the way a man fuels machinery. Jackie sat with posture like a conductor waiting for the downbeat, every gesture measured, every word selected before it left his mouth.

Two boys performing different versions of who they thought they had to be.

The strange thing — the thing only Patty seemed to notice — was the way they never looked at each other. Not avoiding. Avoiding would require acknowledgment. This was something worse. They'd erased each other from the room. Nick's eyes swept the cafeteria and skipped Jackie's table like a scratch on a record, the needle jumping over the part it couldn't play. Jackie's gaze moved through the space where Nick sat as if that corner of the cafeteria held nothing but empty chairs.

Patty saw it because she remembered when they couldn't stop looking at each other.

---

Camp Elbanobscot. Summer of 1975. A music camp tucked into thirty acres of pine and scrub oak off Route 117, where Sudbury's conservation land met the river. Fifteen kids, three cabins, a piano that needed tuning, and a counselor named Dave who played Joni Mitchell so well that even the boys shut up and listened.

Patty had arrived knowing no one. Thirteen years old, already tall, her wheat-colored hair hanging straight to her waist, smelling faintly of something the other kids couldn't name but she could. Home. She'd packed extra soap and a bottle of her mother's perfume, just in case.

She didn't need them.

Nick Johnson sat on the dock the first morning, bare feet in the water, playing an acoustic guitar like he was arguing with it. No finesse — his fingers hit the strings hard enough to make them buzz against the frets, and the sound that came out was raw and specific and entirely his. He played "Dream On" like a boy talking to someone who'd left the room and wouldn't come back.

Jackie Marsh sat at the camp piano in the music cabin, running scales that melted into something from Queen when he thought nobody was listening. The shift happened in his hands — rigid precision loosening into something riskier, wilder, alive. When he played what he wanted instead of what he'd been taught, his shoulders dropped three inches.

On the third day, Dave put all three of them together for a group exercise. "Find harmony," he said. "Not unison. Harmony. Three different notes that need each other."

Nick started singing "Your Song." Elton John — not his usual territory, but the melody was simple enough. His voice cracked on the high notes and he didn't hide it. Jackie came in underneath with piano chords that steadied the song without smothering it. And Patty — Patty found the space between them.

That was the only way she could describe it later, to anyone who asked, though nobody ever did. Nick's voice ran hot. Jackie's ran cool. And there was a temperature in the gap between them, a frequency where neither boy lived alone. She stepped into it with her voice and the gap closed.

Dave stopped the exercise. Just stood there with his hands at his sides and his mouth half-open, because what he was hearing shouldn't have come from three kids who'd met three days ago.

"Do that again," he said.

They did it all week. By the lake at sunset. In the music cabin during free period. Walking the trails between sessions, Patty humming the melody while Nick and Jackie found their parts without thinking, three voices braiding until you couldn't hear where one ended and the others began. The pine trees listened. The lake held still. And for five days, nothing in the world mattered except the sound they made when they stopped trying to make it.

That week she learned what harmony actually meant — not agreement, but three different truths finding the exact place where they didn't have to choose.

Now she sat by the milk coolers in a cafeteria that smelled like industrial ketchup and watched two boys who couldn't even look at each other, and the distance between what they'd been and what they'd become sat in her chest like a stone she'd swallowed years ago and never passed.

---

She brought it up at rehearsal.

St. Michael's Church on a Tuesday evening, the four of them sprawled across pews that smelled like old hymnals and lemon oil. Robin Bosshard sat cross-legged on the altar steps, bass across his lap, his faint Swiss accent making every sentence land a half-beat slower than the words around it. Kristy Wesslen picked at her acoustic, blonde hair curtaining her face. Steve Bankuti tapped a paradiddle on the back of a pew with his sticks — a habit so constant that Patty sometimes heard it in her sleep, ghost rhythms in the dark.

"Battle of the Bands," Patty said. "WBCN's sponsoring it. May fifteenth. Eight slots."

Robin looked up from his bass. "At Lincoln-Sudbury?"

"We'd have to register by Friday."

Kristy stopped picking. "That's the Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh show. The whole school's chosen sides."

"I know."

Steve's paradiddle stopped. In the sudden silence the church creaked around them — old wood settling, a radiator ticking somewhere in the walls.

"What are you saying?" Steve asked.

"I'm saying we enter."

Robin set his bass down. He did that when he wanted to think, as if the instrument's weight cluttered his head. "Why?"

Patty looked at the stained glass above the altar. St. Michael with his sword, standing over something beaten. Evening light through colored glass threw patches of red and blue across the wooden floor like bruises on the church's skin.

"Because somebody has to play music that isn't a weapon."

Kristy exchanged a look with Steve. "You want to walk into their war?"

"I want to play our songs. 'Dreams.' 'Landslide.' 'Silver Springs.' 'Go Your Own Way.' The way we play them — not louder, not angrier, not more perfect. Just honest."

"And what does that do?" Robin asked. Not skeptical — genuinely curious. Robin was always genuinely curious. It was his most useful quality and his most infuriating one.

"I don't know," Patty said. "Maybe nothing. Maybe you can't fix something by playing music at it. But I heard what those two sound like in the same room, and right now they've forgotten. Somebody has to remind them."

Robin picked his bass back up, turned it in his hands. "So we're entering a war to play peace songs?"

Patty almost smiled. "Something like that."

Steve resumed his paradiddle — faster now, a double stroke roll that rattled the hymnal rack. "Nick's band plays like they're kicking down a door. Jackie's band sounds like the Boston Pops with better taste in rock. We'll get swallowed."

"I don't care about winning."

"Then what's the point?"

She thought about the lake at Elbanobscot. Three voices and the pine trees and the last light on the water. The way Dave had stood there, frozen, because what he was hearing had no business coming from kids who barely knew each other's last names.

"The point is showing up. The point is playing music that remembers what music is for."

Kristy started picking — the opening notes of "Dreams," slow and sure. Robin found the bass line without being asked. Steve joined with brushes on the pew back, soft as rain on a window.

Nobody said yes. Nobody had to.

---

She walked home after rehearsal because the bus didn't run past seven and her mother was buried in feed invoices. Two miles through Southeast Sudbury in the April dark, past the Cavicchio property where the security light threw long shadows across empty pens, past the conservation land where mini-bike tracks scarred the trailheads like old wounds, past mailboxes that leaned toward the road like tired men waiting for news that never came.

The smell found her at the property line. Thick, animal, alive. Home.

Other girls at Lincoln-Sudbury fought for their freedom with clothes and attitudes and the careful deployment of cruelty. Patty fought for hers with feed buckets and guitar calluses and the stubborn refusal to be ashamed of where the wind blew from.

She wasn't the kid who got invited to parties on the west side of Hop Brook. She wasn't the kid whose mother drove a Volvo to PTA meetings or debated summer programs over white wine in kitchens with granite countertops. She was the kid who could tell the difference between a gilt and a sow by sound alone and didn't apologize for any of it. If that made her strange, fine. Strange was just another word for free, and freedom was the one thing she'd never let anyone take.

The house sat dark except for the kitchen light — a small colonial with vinyl siding that needed paint, a barn that leaned slightly east, and a yard where chickens had beaten the grass into mud. Her mother sat at the table, calculator and ledger open, reading glasses pushed up on her forehead. The radio played something country, turned low enough to be company without being conversation.

"Rehearsal okay?"

"Yeah. We're entering the Battle of the Bands."

Her mother looked up. "At the high school? The one with the radio station?"

"WBCN. Yeah."

A pause. Calculator set down. Glasses removed. The look a mother gives when she knows her daughter is about to walk into something complicated and has decided to let her do it anyway.

"That's the Johnson boy and the Marsh boy's thing. The whole town's been talking about it."

"I know."

"You sure you want to get in the middle of that?"

Patty opened the refrigerator, found last night's meatloaf, and ate it cold over the sink. The meat was dry. She didn't care.

"I'm not getting in the middle of anything. I'm just playing music."

Her mother watched her eat. The expression on her face said she'd raised a daughter who made her own decisions and she wasn't about to start second-guessing now — but she reserved the right to worry.

"Okay," she said. And went back to her ledger.

---

Friday. Last period. 2:45 PM.

The hallway outside Wentworth's room had cleared. Most kids were already gone — sprinting for buses, idling in parking lots, scattering into the warm afternoon like seeds from a dandelion head.

Patty walked into the music room alone. The fluorescent lights hummed their one flat note. Wentworth sat behind his desk grading papers with the patience of a man who'd traded symphony halls for steady paychecks twenty years ago and had mostly stopped regretting it.

The sign-up sheet for the Battle of the Bands sat at the corner of his desk. Seven names filled the seven lines above the last blank space.

"Mr. Wentworth."

He looked up. Pen still in hand.

"I'd like to register a band."

Something shifted in his face. Not surprise. Recognition. He'd heard Patty sing in music class — had stopped mid-sentence once, the way Dave had stopped at camp, because the voice coming out of this tall girl from the pig farms wasn't the voice of a girl who should have been sitting in a high school music class filling out worksheets about time signatures.

"Band name?"

"Rumours."

He wrote it down. Careful block letters.

"Members?"

"Robin Bosshard on bass and guitar. Kristy Wesslen, harmonies and acoustic. Steve Bankuti, drums. Me on lead vocals and guitar."

"Genre?"

"Fleetwood Mac."

Wentworth set his pen down. Leaned back. Studied her — this girl with damp-braided hair and fingers callused from steel strings and the faintest trace of something soap couldn't conquer. The pig farm kid who sat by the milk coolers and watched the cafeteria divide itself along lines she refused to recognize.

"You know what you're walking into," he said.

It wasn't a question.

"I know what I'm walking into."

He held her gaze for a moment longer, then slid the registration form across the desk. "Eight bands. You're the last slot."
