Patty signed her name. The pen scratched against the paper, loud in the empty room.

She took the carbon copy, folded it into her back pocket, and walked out into the hallway. The fluorescent lights buzzed overhead like trapped insects. The linoleum gleamed under her boots. Somewhere down the corridor, a locker slammed — the last student, the last echo, the building settling into the hollow quiet of a Friday afternoon.

Six weeks.

She hummed "Go Your Own Way" all the way to the bus stop. Not as a statement. Just something she did.

---

**END CHAPTER 23A**

---

CHAPTER 23B: PATTY'S PREPARATION

---

St. Michael's smelled like dust and devotion and whatever they put in the wood polish that made the pews glow amber under the overhead lights.

Patty had arranged the rehearsal for Tuesday — five o'clock, after the parish secretary locked up and left the key under the mat because Father DiNardo trusted musicians the way he trusted anyone who brought their own instrument to a house of God. The church was theirs from five to eight, and by five-fifteen they'd moved hymnals off the front pews, run an extension cord from the sacristy outlet, and turned the altar into a stage.

Steve set up his kit on the altar rug — snare, hi-hat, kick drum, a ride cymbal he'd bought at a yard sale in Maynard for three dollars. Robin plugged his bass into a small Fender amp wedged between the pulpit and the baptismal font. Kristy perched on the communion rail, acoustic guitar across her knees, tuning by ear because the electronic tuner Steve had borrowed from his older brother ate batteries faster than it found notes.

Patty stood at the center, where the priest stood on Sundays. Guitar strap over her shoulder. No setlist written down — she carried it in her head, each song in sequence, each transition mapped to the emotional architecture of what she wanted the audience to feel. Not think. Feel.

"Dreams" first. Open with longing. Let them settle.

"Landslide" second. Slow it down. Make them listen.

"Silver Springs" third. Turn the heat up. Let the ache build.

"Go Your Own Way" to close. Give them back their feet.

She'd chosen every song the way a surgeon chooses instruments — not for spectacle, but for function. Each one carried a specific message, and threaded together they formed an argument she couldn't make with words because words had a way of starting fights and she wasn't here to fight.

"Dreams" said: You don't own me, but I remember you.

"Landslide" said: Time passes. You can't stop it. What matters is what survives.

"Silver Springs" said: I know what we lost and I won't pretend it didn't matter.

"Go Your Own Way" said: You can leave. I'll still be here. And the door's still open.

Nick wouldn't hear it that way. Jackie wouldn't either. That was fine. Patty wasn't playing for their conscious minds. She was playing for the part of them that still remembered a lake, a sunset, three voices braiding in the pine-scented air before the world taught them to be afraid of harmony.

---

"From the top," she said. "Dreams."

Kristy started the intro — that floating C-major arpeggio that Lindsey Buckingham had made sound like falling water. Robin found the bass note underneath, a slow pulse. Steve came in with brushes, barely touching the snare, a whisper rhythm that said: listen.

Patty closed her eyes and sang.

"Now here you go again, you say you want your freedom..."

Her voice rose through the church. Past the pews and the hymnals and the offering boxes. Past the Stations of the Cross and the stained-glass St. Michael with his sword and the plaster saints in their wall niches who'd heard every kind of prayer but this. Up into the vaulted ceiling where the old beams held the weight of a hundred years of worship and the sound bounced back down warm and strange, the church itself becoming an instrument.

By the second verse, Robin had locked in. His bass hummed through the stone floor — you could feel it in your feet, a low vibration that connected the music to the bones of the building. Kristy leaned into the harmony on the chorus, her voice winding around Patty's like ivy around a trellis, different but inseparable. Steve's brushes hissed against the snare with the patience of someone who understood that rhythm wasn't about speed. It was about trust.

They ran "Dreams" three times. The first was loose — finding the tempo, remembering where the dynamics shifted. The second was tighter, the four of them listening to each other with the concentration of people defusing a bomb. The third was the one that mattered. On the third pass, the song stopped being four people playing and became one sound breathing.

"Landslide" came next. Patty alone at first — just voice and acoustic, the pick pattern Stevie Nicks had turned into a kind of lullaby for grown-ups. The church went still. Even Steve put his sticks down. There was no percussion in "Landslide," and Steve was smart enough to know that silence was a drummer's most powerful tool.

"I took my love, I took it down..."

Her voice cracked on the second line. Not a mistake — the crack was the song. Patty didn't smooth it over or pretend it hadn't happened. She leaned into it the way a good carpenter leans into the grain of the wood, letting the flaw become the feature. The congregation of empty pews received it. The saints in their niches received it. The dust motes floating in the evening light through stained glass received it and kept moving, indifferent and beautiful.

They worked for two hours. "Silver Springs" needed the most attention — the build from quiet verse to soaring chorus was the hardest transition, the place where a lesser band would either rush the crescendo or let the quiet parts die of neglect. Patty made them run the bridge eleven times until Robin's bass walk ascended in perfect parallel with her vocal line, two rivers meeting.

"Go Your Own Way" was the easiest. They'd played it a hundred times — at birthday parties, in Kristy's basement, at the Concord River cleanup where the only audience was a dozen volunteers in rubber boots. The song was in their muscles. All they had to do was let it out.

At eight o'clock, they packed up. Steve broke down his kit with the efficient motions of a kid who'd been setting up and tearing down drum sets since he was twelve. Robin coiled cables. Kristy stacked hymnals back into the pew racks.

"That felt different tonight," Robin said, zipping his bass into its case.

"Good different?" Patty asked.

Robin paused. Held the case in both hands. "Intentional different. Like you know something we don't."

Patty turned off the Fender amp. The hum died, and the church filled with the kind of silence that only exists in places built for it.

"I know what I want the music to do," she said. "That's all."

Robin looked at her for a long moment. Then he nodded, once, and walked out into the parking lot where his mother's Buick idled under the security light.

---

Wednesday. 11:15 AM. The hallway outside the biology lab.

The between-class crush at Lincoln-Sudbury moved like cattle through a chute — four hundred kids in six minutes, shoulder to shoulder, sneakers squeaking on linoleum, the air thick with body spray and bubble gum and the low roar of voices saying nothing important at maximum volume.

Patty pressed against the lockers to let a group of Rampers pass. She had biology in three minutes and the textbook weighed six pounds and her locker combination had been sticking since February.

Nick Johnson came from the other direction. Walking fast, guitar case slung over his shoulder like a rifleman carrying his weapon, head down, the set of his jaw saying don't talk to me and the tension in his shoulders saying I dare you to try. He'd lost weight since she'd last really looked at him — cheekbones sharper, eyes darker, the softness of the boy she'd known at Elbanobscot burned away by two years of grief and a house where the mortgage was always a month behind.

He passed within arm's reach.

Patty hummed. Not deliberately — it was something she did, the way other people cracked their knuckles or tapped their fingers. A melody lived in her throat at all times, and the one that surfaced now was "Go Your Own Way." Five notes. The opening phrase. A reflex, not a message.

Nick stopped.

One step past her. Frozen. His shoulders tightened, then loosened, then tightened again — the micro-movements of a body fighting a memory it didn't want to acknowledge. He turned his head, not all the way, just enough to show his profile against the fluorescent light. Something in the melody had caught him. A hook snagged on a memory buried so deep he couldn't name it — a lake, pine trees, a girl's voice finding the space between his and Jackie's, a week when music hadn't been a war.

He looked at Patty. She looked back.

For one second the hallway noise fell away and they stood in the gap between past and present, two people who knew what the other sounded like at thirteen, what their voice did when it wasn't trying to prove anything. The recognition was there — faint, underwater, a shape seen through clouded glass.

Then Nick blinked. Shook his head, a half-motion, like shaking off a chill. He walked on. Guitar case bouncing against his hip. Gone around the corner before Patty could exhale.

She watched him go. The hallway refilled with noise. Lockers slammed. Somebody laughed too loud about something that wasn't funny.

Patty leaned against the cool metal of her locker and let the melody die in her throat. Five notes. She hadn't planned them, hadn't aimed them. But they'd landed. Somewhere behind Nick's eyes, in the part of his brain that remembered things his body had decided to forget, those five notes had made contact.

Whether it mattered was another question. Whether anything she did in six weeks could undo four years of silence and bitterness — that was a question she didn't have the right to answer.

She hefted her biology textbook and walked to class.

---

Thursday. 4:30 PM. Wentworth's music room.

Patty had come back for the guitar picks she'd left in the instrument cupboard during last period. The hallway was empty — after-school clubs had migrated to the gym, and the music room existed in the deep quiet that settled over unused classrooms like dust on a shelf.

She opened the door and heard the piano.

Jackie Marsh sat at the upright in the corner. Not the Steinway in the band room — the scarred practice piano that Wentworth kept for students who needed to work through something the Steinway was too pristine to hold. Jackie's back was to the door. His blazer was draped over the piano bench beside him. His sleeves were rolled to his elbows, and his hands moved over the keys with the kind of precision that costs ten thousand dollars in lessons — but something else was happening in the music. Something the lessons hadn't taught.

He was playing "Somebody to Love."

Not performing it. Not rehearsing it. Playing it the way a person writes in a journal — private, unpolished, honest. The runs weren't clean. He let notes ring too long or cut them too short, feeling his way through the song instead of executing it. His left hand found the bass line by instinct, not by chart, and the chord voicings were his own — darker than the original, more complex, stacked with dissonance that resolved into something aching and true.

"Can anybody find me somebody to love?"

He sang it quiet. Barely above the piano's volume. Not Freddie Mercury's operatic howl — Jackie's voice, stripped of the performance polish he wore like armor, raw and reaching, the voice of a boy who meant every word and wished he didn't.

Patty stood in the doorway. She didn't move. Didn't breathe loud enough to break the room's agreement with Jackie's playing — the old walls and the fluorescent lights and the instrument lockers all holding still, offering the boy what the rest of his life refused to give him: a space where he didn't have to be perfect.

The song ended. The last chord hung in the room for three seconds, then two, then one, then gone.

Silence.

"That was real," Patty said.

Jackie spun on the bench. His hands came off the keys like they'd been burned. His eyes went wide — not fear, exactly, but the wild exposure of someone caught without their costume, standing in their own skin for the first time in front of someone who could see them.

"How long have you been standing there?"

"Long enough."

He stared at her. His mouth opened, closed, opened again. She watched the armor rebuilding in real time — shoulders squaring, chin lifting, the careful construction of a boy who'd been taught that vulnerability was a weakness and musicianship was measured in precision, not feeling.

But underneath it, in the second before the walls went up, she saw something she recognized. The same thing she'd seen at Elbanobscot when Jackie played Queen in the music cabin and thought nobody was listening. The same dropping of the shoulders. The same breath.

Freedom.

Patty turned to leave.

"Patty."

She stopped. Looked back over her shoulder.

Jackie sat on the piano bench with his hands in his lap. The armor was up, but it had a crack in it — a thin line running from his throat to his chest where the song still lived.

"What are you doing here?" he asked. The question meant more than the words.

"Getting my picks," she said. "From the cupboard."

He nodded. Not satisfied, but unable to ask what he really wanted to ask.

She crossed the room, opened the instrument cupboard, found her picks in the small cardboard box where she kept them — three medium tortoiseshell Fenders, worn smooth at the tips. She put them in her jacket pocket.

At the door, she stopped again. Not for drama. Because something in her wouldn't let her leave without saying it.

"You don't play like that when anyone's watching."

Jackie said nothing.

"You should."

She walked out. The door closed behind her with a soft click. In the hallway, her boots sounded like a slow heartbeat on the linoleum.

---

The night before the Battle.

St. Michael's Church. 10 PM. Everyone gone.

Patty sat barefoot on the altar rug. The wool was rough under her feet — decades of knees and spilled communion wine and the slow wear of people kneeling to ask for things they already had. Her guitar sat in her lap. A Martin D-28 that her father had bought used from a music shop in Framingham when she was fourteen, the spruce top darkened with age, the neck worn smooth where her thumb rode the back during chord changes.

The church was dark except for the votive candles flickering in their red glass holders near the side altar. Thirty small flames making thirty small shadows that moved on the walls like the ghosts of prayers that hadn't been answered yet.

She didn't play. Not at first. She sat with the guitar and listened to the church breathe — the radiators ticking their cooling rhythm, the old wood popping in its joints, the wind pressing against the stained glass from outside, St. Michael's sword catching the last light from the parking lot in a thin line of amber.

Tomorrow she'd stand on a stage in front of four hundred and forty-three people. Nick would be there with his grief and his duct-taped guitar and the fury that passed for music when you couldn't say what you actually felt. Jackie would be there with his precision and his blazer and the voice he only used when he thought no one could hear. Eight bands. A war dressed up as entertainment. A town watching its children fight each other because adults couldn't figure out how to stop drawing lines.

She couldn't save them. She knew that. She'd known it since camp, since the last morning at Elbanobscot when they drove away in different cars toward different lives and she stood on the gravel path with her duffel bag and understood — at thirteen, with the certainty that only children possess — that the world would not make room for what they'd found.

But she could play.

She could stand on that stage and play music that didn't ask for anything — not victory, not vindication, not proof that one kind of life was worth more than another. She could play "Dreams" and mean it. She could play "Landslide" and let her voice crack on the second line because the crack was real and real was the whole point. She could play "Silver Springs" and let the chorus swell until the walls pushed back. She could close with "Go Your Own Way" and let the room decide what that meant — separation or invitation, goodbye or open door.

She couldn't fix two boys who'd been broken by a town that taught them friendship was a luxury they couldn't afford.

But she could remind them what they sounded like before they learned to be afraid of each other.

She placed her fingers on the fretboard. C-major to G. The opening of "Go Your Own Way." The pick caught the low E string and the note filled the church, one clean tone expanding outward through the empty pews and the votive shadows and the stone walls that had heard a century of prayers and confessions and knew the difference between performance and truth.

Patty played it through. Once. The whole song. Alone in St. Michael's with her bare feet on the altar rug and the candles burning down and the wind outside pressing against the colored glass like it wanted to come in and listen.

When the last chord faded, she didn't move. The silence that followed wasn't empty. It was the silence of a room that had received something and was holding it, the way a church holds everything — the grief and the gratitude and the stubborn human insistence that some things are worth singing even when nobody's listening.

She wasn't nervous. She wasn't hopeful. She was ready.

Tomorrow, she'd walk onto a stage and into a war she hadn't started, carrying nothing but a guitar and four songs and the memory of three voices that once found each other by a lake in the summer when they were young enough to believe that finding was the same as keeping.

She packed her guitar. Slid on her boots. Blew out the votives one by one — thirty small flames dying between her fingers, thirty small trails of smoke rising toward the ceiling in the dark.

She locked the church door behind her and walked into the May night.

The stars were out. The air smelled like cut grass and lilac and, faintly, from the southeast, the honest smell of home.

She hummed "Go Your Own Way" all the way to her mother's car.

---

**END CHAPTER 23B**

---

CHAPTER 24: PATTY RETURNS

May 14th, 1979. St. Michael's, Sudbury, a little after nine. The last car door in the parking lot had been Steve's Chevette — the driver-side hinge whining past halfway — and Patty had counted it the way she counted beats, and then she'd been alone.

Rumours had packed up an hour ago: Kristy's amp, Robin's bass in its gig bag, Steve's kick pedal and snare in their padded case. They'd hugged her at the parish-hall door the way you hug somebody the night before surgery, a little too long and too careful, and now the sanctuary was what it always was on a Monday night, which was empty.

She had moved the guitar in here on purpose. The acoustic was a hand-me-down from her uncle in Hudson, a Yamaha FG-180 with a scratch along the lower bout where somebody at some wedding had set down a beer bottle. It lived in a soft case with a broken zipper she held shut with a shoelace.

She took it out and sat it across her thigh and tuned by ear. E. Flat. She bent the tuning key a hair. The string came up under her thumbnail with the small sweet creak of bronze against bone nut. A. Fine. D. Fine. G. Sharp; she gave it back. B — B was always the one — she tuned it twice. High E. Fine.

She was wearing jeans with barn mud dried on the left knee, a flannel over her Rumours T-shirt, her hair braided over the right shoulder. Her fingertips had the pad calluses guitar players get. The back of her left hand had a pink line from last Tuesday, a strand of barbed wire that had caught her when she went in after a sow. On her right forearm, the half-circle of a hoof step was going yellow at the edges. She smelled faintly of pig. She knew it. She was not going to apologize for it to a room full of saints.

She got up and walked to the rack of votives by the side altar — three rows of red glass jars on a black iron frame, most unlit on a Monday. She took a long match off the box, struck it, and the head caught with that small chemical pop. She held the flame to a wick in the second row. The wax made a tiny suck of air taking the fire, and the flame straightened into an orange flower the size of her thumbnail, and the red glass around it went alive.

She did not say who it was for. She never said. She blew the match out, set it in the sand tray with the other spent ones, and went back to the pew.

Above the main altar the big window was St. Michael with his foot on the dragon's neck and his sword down and through — the dragon bottle-green in the lead seams, the saint the color of a bruise healing. Sunday's incense still hung in the wood. Melted wax. Worn oak. The Stations of the Cross marched around the nave in plaster relief. Jesus falls. Jesus falls again. Jesus falls the third time.

The hymnal was red. She rolled it back and forth along her thigh, the pebbled leatherette making a soft dry sound on the denim.

She opened the camp notebook.

The letters were inside the back cover. Seven of them, folded small, paper gone soft at the creases. Four from Nick. Three from Jackie. She took Nick's top one and opened it across her knee. The handwriting was cramped and sideways-slanted like he was running out of room even though the page was half empty, and in the third line he had crossed out *my dad* and written *my father* above it and then crossed that out too and put *him*. Three tries on two letters. The pen had pressed so hard on the last one that the paper had dimpled through to the back.

She folded it. She slid it back.

Jackie's top letter was thinner paper, the kind with the faint blue lines. The handwriting went straight across the page like a prayer. Every *t* crossed. Every *i* dotted. At the bottom, where he had written *I am learning the C-minor prelude and I can almost make it breathe*, he had underlined *almost* once, careful, with a ruler. Her thumb rode the crease. She put it back.

Nick's cramped urgent fingers. Jackie's ruler.

She thought about Jackie at the café downtown last fall, lifting a sugar cube with the silver tongs and lowering it onto the spoon and into the tea to watch it dissolve from underneath, the whole small operation while he told her his father thought Queen was a phase. She thought about Nick at Friendly's in March, a tuna melt cooling because he was drumming a tempo on the Formica with a butter knife, the waitress calling him honey twice and him not hearing her either time.

She let the letters sit. She did not want to sort them anymore.

She pulled the guitar back onto her thigh and played a D. Then a G. She slid to a C, her ring finger missed the third fret and buzzed against the second, and she laughed — out loud, in the empty sanctuary, one small startled bark. St. Michael did not move. The dragon did not move. She played the C again and this time it rang.

Then, without meaning to, she was humming. It came up out of her the way weather came up out of a field. *Loving you / isn't the right thing to do.* She didn't sing the words. She hummed the line, just the shape of it, the small step-down at the end, her right hand picking a quiet bass note underneath because the song wanted that bass note and her hand knew.

Camp Elbanobscot, the summer she was fourteen, the lake going copper at the end of the day. Nick's guitar had been out of tune and neither of them had cared. Jackie had played a chord nobody had asked for, a suspended thing with a fourth hanging in it, and her voice had come in over the top without thinking, and for about six seconds the three of them had landed inside one sound — lake water on the dock pilings, the sap-hot smell of pine, three voices and two guitars and nobody keeping count. They had not been trying. That was why it worked. They had been breathing, and the breathing had happened to rhyme.

She hummed another bar. She was on the word the song wouldn't give her — the turn between *you* and *isn't* — when a car door went in the parking lot. Short, metal, far side.

Her thumb went still on the G string. The car started. The car pulled out. It wasn't for her.

Her hand went into her bag and found the little white Panasonic transistor her brother had given her two Christmases ago, antenna bent from too many glove compartments. She almost turned it on. WBCN would be where she'd left it; it was always where she'd left it. She took her hand out of the bag. Not tonight.

She tuned the B string one more time because the B string was always the one. She played the opening of the song she wasn't going to play tomorrow — the first four bars, for herself — and let it die against the stone. She put the guitar in the case and tied the shoelace.

The votive was still going. The red glass was warm when she passed a hand near it, not touching. She dipped two fingers in the holy-water stoup by the door and crossed herself the way her mother had taught her — quick, four points, sternum and shoulders — without closing her eyes.

She pushed the door open with her hip, the guitar case in her right hand and the notebook in her left. The night air came in smelling of cut grass and diesel off Route 20. Behind her the little orange flower kept burning inside its red glass, and St. Michael kept his sword down through the dragon, and the hymnal sat on the pew where she'd left it with the print of her thumb on the cover.

She let the door close on its own soft seal.

**END CHAPTER 24**


CHAPTER 25: EVE OF THE BATTLE

---

Dutton Road was quiet at a quarter to ten. Nick lay on top of his bedspread in his jeans and listened to the house tick the way houses did after everyone was supposed to be asleep. The refrigerator. A pipe in the wall. His mother's shoes coming off in the kitchen.

He got up.

The side door of the house opened onto the breezeway and the breezeway onto the garage, and in the garage the one bare bulb came on the way it always did — a half-flicker and then the yellow. The April 1975 Aerosmith calendar was still tacked over the bench where Dan had left it, the page turned to a month that would never get flipped again. The red Craftsman toolbox sat against the far wall, latches rusted shut. On the plywood lid, a Maxwell House can held a 9/16ths socket and a worn spark plug. Nick had not moved either thing in three years.

The $25 acoustic was on its stand by the door. He picked it up. He did not plug in. The Lincoln boys had gone home an hour ago — Stephen's Nova pulling away, Mark's laugh from the passenger window, David shouting something about tomorrow — and the garage still smelled of their sweat and his own, and of the motor oil that had soaked into the concrete long before any of them were born.

He sat on a milk crate in front of the toolbox.

He played "Dream On" in the dark, unplugged, the pick against the strings barely louder than the ticking house. He played it the way Dan had showed him one Sunday — thumb anchored, bend on the second fret, breathe on the pause. Halfway through the solo his left hand stopped where his father's hand had always stopped to nod, and he nodded at nobody, and he went on.

He finished. He set the guitar across his thighs. His left hand was cramping; he flexed it once on the fretboard and stopped.

He looked at the latches.

He laid a palm flat on the plywood lid, the way he had seen his mother lay a palm flat on the kitchen table when she was tired. The Maxwell House can did not move. The 9/16ths socket did not move.

He did not open the toolbox.

After a while he turned off the bulb and sat in the dark with the guitar warming his legs, listening to a truck downshift on Route 20 a half-mile away, and he understood that tomorrow he would play for his mother and for the ghost in the toolbox and for the truck driver and for nobody, and that the order of those things was the only thing he still got to decide.

In the kitchen, his mother's light went out at ten-thirty.

---

Two miles north, on Maple Street, Dr. Richard Marsh's house faced a row of hedges clipped to the same height every April by the same man. Jackie's bedroom was on the third floor, under the eaves. The Steinway was two floors below him and might as well have been in another country.

He sat on the wide sill of the dormer window with his knees drawn up and the curtain half-drawn behind him. The streetlight on the corner laid a pale strip across the rug. His mother had gone to bed. His father was in his study, reading. The house was the kind of quiet that measured itself.

On the desk behind him, the Juilliard envelope sat on top of a stack of recital programs. Acceptance or waitlist, he hadn't opened it. He had promised himself he would not open it until after tomorrow, for reasons he had not examined.

Face down on the blotter, in a drugstore frame, was a Polaroid from Camp Elbanobscot, 1975 — three kids at the end of a dock, a borrowed guitar across one lap, a girl between the two boys laughing at something the photograph had not caught. He had turned it face-down in March. He had not turned it back.

Under his pillow, the little transistor radio was already on.

WBCN, 104.1, Boston, playing what WBCN played at a quarter to eleven on a Monday in May — some Dire Straits track, "Sultans of Swing," the guitar clean as a knife through paper. The DJ's voice came on at the end. It was not Maxanne's. Maxanne had been gone two years by then, though the station still sounded like a room she had walked out of.

He pressed his ear closer to the pillow. He had done this since he was eight, since he had first understood that the house had listening ears and that the radio was where people talked about music like it was a thing you loved, not a thing you graded.

The DJ announced the next song. Jackie did not catch the name.

He thumbed the dial. The radio clicked off.

Two floors below him, the Steinway sat closed in the dark, polished, waiting. He put his forehead against the window glass and watched the streetlight flicker once on Maple Street at ten-thirty.

---

On the other side of Sudbury, past the orchard and the cornfields and the long dip where Landham Road crossed the brook, the Lally farm sat low against the pines. The hogs had been fed and watered. The barn lights were off. A sow was grunting somewhere in the back pen, a soft complaint at nothing, the way sows did when they were settling.

Patty's bedroom was at the back of the house, over the mudroom. Wide pine floors. A braided rag rug her grandmother had made. A lamp with a yellowed shade that cast the room in the color of old paper. A *Rumours* poster was tacked to the slant of the wall — the black-and-white one, Stevie leaning on Mick — the corners curled from the woodstove heat that rose through the register in winter.

She lay on top of the quilt in her jeans and a white t-shirt, not sleeping.

On the little desk by the window, she had a sheet of her mother's stationery and a Bic pen. She had written *Dear Nick* and crossed it out, written *Dear Jackie* and crossed it out, written *Dear both of you* and crossed that out too. The sheet was in the wastebasket now, balled up the way she balled up things she meant.

A headlight — a late school bus from the Route 20 yard going home empty — swept across the ceiling on Landham Road and was gone.

Her transistor was on the nightstand, low. A DJ was reading a community bulletin. She was not really listening.

She caught herself humming. She had been doing it all night, the way some people chewed the inside of their cheek. *You can go your own way, go your own way* — the bridge, not the chorus. She got two bars in and the hum caught in her throat the way a splinter catches in a finger, and she put her hand to her neck and stopped.

She was not going to fix them. She had told herself that at St. Michael's and she told herself that now. She only wanted to stand on the stage tomorrow and sing a song she loved, and let them hear what that sounded like.

Out in the barn, the sow sighed and was quiet.

The lamp went out at ten-thirty.

---

The Lincoln-Sudbury parking lot lay empty under its one sodium lamp, the stage door locked, the gym dark. A freight train pulled east toward Boston along the Route 20 cut, and the night closed behind it.

**END CHAPTER 25**


CHAPTER 26: THE BATTLE BEGINS

May 15, 1979. Seven o'clock on a Tuesday night. The Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School auditorium had been built in 1960 and smelled like it still — wood floor wax, the slow mineral breath of cast iron, four hundred and forty-three folding seats bolted into a concrete rake that groaned as kids sat down and groaned again when kids stood back up to wave at other kids. The fluorescent cove lights above the proscenium threw the whole room into the color of a grocery store. Behind the stage hung the painted backdrop every school play had used for a decade: a Concord Road maple tree in October, the red gone brown at the edges where the scrim had rolled. In the lobby the JV booster club had set up a popcorn machine and the smell of commercial butter and Aqua Velva and girls' perfume had come in with the crowd and was now layered, impossibly, on top of the baseline adolescent sweat of an April-warm room in May. The balcony was already full. Seniors.

Grace Johnson took a seat in the third row on the left. She had left Friendly's at four, changed at home into the good dress — the navy one with the buttons, the dress she'd worn to Dan's funeral and would not wear again after tonight — and brought her winter coat folded over one arm because she had nothing else to carry. The programs she'd picked up at the door were folded once in her lap. Her purse sat on top. She put her hands on top of the purse and then on top of the programs and then back on top of the purse.

Two seats to her right, Mary Murphy from the old Saint Michael's Tuesday group was settling in without looking at her. Mary had come. Mary did not say anything. That was enough.

Three rows behind Grace, Dr. Richard Marsh took his seat on the aisle. Gray suit, pressed shirt, no tie loosened. His hands rested flat on his knees, palms down, thumbs in line with the seam of his trousers. He sat the way he sat at department meetings.

On the stage, the judges' table was draped in a black cloth, four folding chairs behind it. Maxanne Sartori had flown up from the Elektra office at noon and had come to the high school straight from the Holiday Inn in Natick — leather jacket over a plain shirt, jeans, boots, a styrofoam cup of WBCN coffee in front of her with the red logo still on it. A clipboard. A pencil. The pint of Heaven Hill in her bag for later was nobody's business. Beside her Carter Alan was twenty-six and looked nineteen, earnest as a Boy Scout, taking his own notes. Bob Walker from Strawberries had thick forearms laid across a second clipboard as if selecting the draft. Chuck Morrison, WBCN morning MC, stood at the lip of the stage with the mic already hot.

He tapped it once. The tap came back through the house PA like a door closing.

"Good evening, Lincoln-Sudbury." A good-natured roar. "First ever WBCN Battle of the Bands, brought to you by the Rock of Boston and by Strawberries Records and Tapes, where for seven ninety-nine you can take home an album that will change your life or your sister's." He let the laugh build and cut it off. "Eight bands. Five hundred dollars. Recording time on Boylston Street. Let's go."

He introduced Maxanne and got the warmest hand of the night because half the room had heard her nights on 104.1 before she'd gone to Elektra. Carter got applause. Bob Walker got the loudest — studio time, album contract, the kind of future a Sudbury kid could carry out of a gymnasium. Chuck held up the lineup card.

"The Jesters. Detroit Rock City. The Dead Heads. Talking Heads Talking. Walking the Line. Crown Jewels. Rumours. The Regulators. In that order. God help us."

He read the first four again and waved the first four on.

The Jesters were Lincoln freshmen too young to be self-conscious. They opened with "Just What I Needed" and the bass player missed the turnaround twice and they played it sweet anyway; the room clapped because it was a start. Detroit Rock City came on in greasepaint and platform boots from Acton-Boxborough, a blood packet gone off early in the first verse, a fire-breather reduced to steam, and the kids in the first five rows loved them for trying. The Dead Heads — Wayland, patchouli, Birkenstocks — opened "Truckin'" warm and rolled it toward "Dark Star" and lost the back half of the balcony around minute seven; Chuck flicked the house lights twice and they landed it into applause that was kind and exhausted. Talking Heads Talking from Boston Latin came on in matching thrift-store suits and played "Psycho Killer" with such clinical tightness that the older brothers in the crowd recognized it before the younger kids did, and a whole row of Rats in the back threw their hands up, delighted.

By the end of the fourth band, kids had started to drift — to the lobby for popcorn, to the smoking corner outside the fire doors, to the bathroom in little groups so they could go together. The house mood wasn't electric. It was waiting.

Along the rake, the waiting arranged itself. The quieter kids held the middle. A cluster in leather and denim colonized the back rows with boots on the armrests. Boys with grease still under their nails leaned against the side wall near the shop-corridor door because sitting in the folding seats took the ache out of the wrong part of their backs. Up front, the study-hall kids had the first two rows in neat sections, pens out as if they would be quizzed. Nobody had planned it. It was just where bodies went.

---

Backstage stage-right, Nick Johnson stood at the gap in the heavy velvet curtain where the hem didn't quite reach the floor, and watched the house through the slit.

His guitar hung from the strap Dan had bought him at E.U. Wurlitzer in the fall of 1976, the leather gone dark and soft in the curve where his thumb rode. The strap was not the thing. The thing was the body of the guitar against the front of his ribs, warm from the stage lights even through the curtain, and the pick in the pocket of his jeans where he could feel it with two fingers without looking. His throat was dry the way it had gone dry the first time he'd stood up in English class and lost the shape of a sentence halfway through. He swallowed and it tasted of pennies. That was adrenaline. Stephen had told him that once. The body made a metal when it was afraid.

On the pad of his right thumb a blister had come up late the night before, a little puffed dome at the edge of the nail where the pick had ridden on the third hour of "Sweet Emotion," and he had drained it at midnight with a sewing needle and his mother's rubbing alcohol and wrapped it in a square of white first-aid tape and then peeled the tape off before he left the house because he could not feel the pick through the tape. The blister had opened again somewhere between the parking lot and here. He did not look at it.

Through the curtain gap he found his mother in the third row and then he made himself look away, because it hurt to look at her from behind and not be able to fix anything.

He slid his eye along the stage. At the lip of the grand piano on the far side of the wings, a boy in a white shirt was running his fingers over the keys without sound. Jackie. Nick saw the collar, the posture, the way the shoulder blades sat. He did not see the face. He did not need to. For perhaps a second the two of them looked up at the same time and their eyes caught through the velvet slit and through the length of the stage and the grip of lighting-kid's black tape along the boards, and then one of them — neither could have said which — looked away.

"Walking the Line." The stage manager's whisper, hot and close. "Three minutes."

Stephen Murphy was at Nick's shoulder. Mark had his bass already strapped. David was tightening the high hat for the third time in five minutes. Nick looked at them. They were kids from Lincoln. They were his kids for fifteen minutes.

"Yeah," Nick said. That was all he said.

He put his hand flat on the curtain like you put a hand on a horse's neck before you got up.

---

On the other side of the backstage, by the school's Steinway, Jackie Marsh took his hand off the keyboard and folded his fingers once in his lap to stop them from doing anything else.

Elizabeth was tuning the violin against a pitch pipe without needing to. Ace was up on a crate with a stick in each hand, walking paradiddles on his thigh. Max had squatted behind his bass amp and was watching a meter that didn't need watching. Four kids who had been practicing since they were small enough to need a phone book on the piano bench.

Footsteps on the wing stairs. A measured tread. Jackie knew the tread before he turned.

"Jackie."

Dr. Marsh stood in the curtain of the stage-left wing. He did not come all the way in. He stopped at the Steinway, and his right hand — without Jackie seeing — came down and rested for half a second on the polished wood of the fallboard over the keys, the wood warm from the lights, the hand wide and long-fingered and steady, the hand that had not played since before Jackie was born. He took it off again before he spoke.

"Fine?"

"Fine," Jackie said. "Just nervous."

"Good." Dr. Marsh let the silence do the next thing for him. Then he said, very quiet, "Play it the way you hear it," and he turned, and he went back down the stairs to the house.

Jackie stood where he was for a moment after the footsteps were gone. His father had never said that sentence before. His father did not know he had said it now. Jackie did not look at the fallboard; his father had not touched anything there.

He laid his own hand flat on the wood where the hand had been. It was warm. He took it away.

Out in the house, the Regulators from Framingham were three minutes into a version of "Whole Lotta Love" that had the balcony on its feet already, and the drummer hit the bottom of a fill and the room met him, and Chuck Morrison's voice rolled down from the PA: "Ladies and gentlemen. All the way from Sudbury, Massachusetts. Walking the Line."

---

In the third row, Grace Johnson's hand closed on the folded programs in her lap and did not let go.

Backstage, Nick looked once at Stephen and Mark and David, and then he walked through the gap in the curtain where his hand had been, and the stage lights hit him full in the face, and a stage monitor at the downstage-right edge crackled once, a small hot pop, and the crowd came up off its seats, and the strap took the weight of the guitar, and Nick's left hand found the neck.

**END CHAPTER 26**


CHAPTER 27: JACKIE'S PERFORMANCE

Backstage at 8:15, Jackie Marsh sat on a metal folding chair with the Martin D-28 across his thighs and tuned the low E by ear.

The guitar was there because it kept his hands busy. He had decided a week ago that he would not carry it out. The D-28 would stay backstage. He would walk on and go to the school's Yamaha, and the school's Yamaha would be the only instrument the four hundred and forty-three of them saw him touch. He had spent a month deciding this. His father had said nothing about it either way, which had been its own kind of pressure.

His fingers knew the D without looking. The A he tuned off the D. The E off the A. He went through the strings the way he had been taught at eight years old, one at a time, patient, the spring in the tuning peg resisting just so before it gave.

Elizabeth stood beside a road case playing G-major scales into her chest, the bow moving, her eyes on nothing, the small pink callus on the left of her neck above the cardigan collar. She played four octaves up and four octaves down and did not stop between. Max Wesslen sat on an Ampeg head with the Precision flat across his lap, plucked the open A, watched the tuner's needle, and moved the peg a sixteenth of a turn. Ace Callahan ran the tip of one stick around the rim of a practice pad he was holding in his left hand, tick tick tick tick, the dry small clock of a drummer's nerves.

From the stage side someone called three minutes and nobody said anything back.

Jackie set the Martin down on its stand. He flexed his left hand open and closed twice. His palms were dry. Cold air in through the nose, warm out through the mouth — his father's trick for the pre-concert body. He had been doing it for eleven years.

"Hey," he said.

The three of them looked up.

"We're playing Queen because we love Queen. That's it."

Ace said, "What if we don't win."

"Then we don't win."

Max said, "A-flat on the second."

"A-flat on the second."

Elizabeth closed the bow against her skirt, tightened the horsehair a quarter turn, and did not say anything.

Someone called one minute. Ace stood up and went through the blacks toward his kit. Max followed with the P-bass. Elizabeth followed with the violin at her side. Jackie did not look at his Martin again. He walked past it.

---

The house was bigger from the stage than it had looked from the audience floor. The lights cleaned his depth and left him a hot white wall — he could see the first three rows as faces, maybe four, and past that the dark went to a charcoal haze where the house rose. He walked to the Yamaha. The grand was half-open on the short stick. He knew this piano. He had played it for the spring recital in March and for the district assembly in April. It was the piano his father had helped the school buy in 1976. He had never thought of it as his before.

He sat. He touched middle C without playing it. The key was cool, and the key-dip was maybe two millimeters deeper than the Steinway upstairs at home, and the hammer action on the Yamaha leaned a hair to the treble side — felted badly, the school's maintenance man filing the hammers once a year with a piece of sandpaper whose grit nobody asked about. The asymmetry was a real thing he could feel with his knuckles before he played a note, and it steadied him because it was a fact, the way a bad floor under a bar stool is a fact, and you sit on it anyway.

He found the front row by its silhouettes. He could not read his father's face in the third row. The lights made Dr. Richard Marsh a shape in a good suit, dark on dark, a seated body under the spill. His hands on his knees or in his lap, impossible to tell. Jackie would not know anything about what his father's face did tonight. That would have to be all right.

Elizabeth raised the violin. Ace clicked the sticks four.

---

"Killer Queen" was a piece of furniture. Mercury had written it in an afternoon in the Trident Studios kitchen in 1974 — Jackie had read that in a Melody Maker interview six months ago and had held onto it — and it came out of the Yamaha clean, the sixteenths in the right hand, the music-box clockwork he had gotten clean in rehearsal twenty times. He let the Yamaha's treble lean work for him and kept the right-hand figures bright and closer to the fallboard where the hammers spoke easier. Max walked the roots under Ace's brushes. Elizabeth threaded the countermelody an octave above where the horn would have lived on the record.

The southwest-section kids made the sound a crowd makes when what it expected is being delivered. A small front-of-chest murmur. Assent. Jackie heard it and did not look up. He hit the last chord and let the pedal hang a breath longer than it needed, and the hall did what a hall does with a grand piano when the pedal hangs — it kept the note, and then it did not. The applause came in as the note went.

---

He did not stand up.

He gave Elizabeth the nod for A-flat.

Max found the root on the E string fifth fret and held it under his palm, not yet plucked. Ace brought the sticks down to brushes. Elizabeth tucked the violin closer. Jackie put his left hand on the A-flat in the second inversion and played the intro chord and sang the first line with his mouth close to the cardioid at the shell of the piano. His voice sat where it should. The floor monitor at his feet had been tilted too far back by somebody in the crew and he had to lean to catch his own return, and he leaned, and that was fine too.

"Somebody to Love" was not "Killer Queen." "Killer Queen" was jewelry. "Somebody to Love" was a church — Aretha's church, Mercury building a gospel piano song in A-flat because the black keys let the voice ride — and Jackie had been alone with it at the Steinway upstairs for three years, at one in the morning, the keyboard lid up and the pedal down and the house asleep. He played it now the way he had played it then. The left hand went into the big octave roll under the verse, the right hand answering itself up the stacked thirds. Ace's brushes turned to sticks at the pre-chorus. Max came in under with a walking line that climbed on the off-beats. Elizabeth did not double the melody. She had asked in rehearsal and he had said no. She played a long bowed descant on the second chorus, up in fifth position, a single line held across eight bars that narrowed the harmonic field to a spotlight and widened it again when she cut out — a piece of musicianship above his skill, and he felt the southwest section go quiet through it and did not understand why until he was most of the way through the bridge, when he realized they were listening.

The climb into the last chorus is the climb into the last chorus. It is the reason people cover this song and the reason most people covering it should not. The voice has to go up and it has to break a little or it is a lie.

He hit the note on *find me somebody to love* and his voice cracked on the second syllable.

He heard it crack.

His left hand did not adjust. The note held anyway.

He came down off the line and into the vamp. Max went up a fifth with him. Ace moved to the ride. Elizabeth's long bowed line came back in from under, and the four of them rode the last twelve bars the way they had ridden them in the basement on Tuesday. On the final cut Jackie lifted the sustain pedal on the downbeat and killed the Yamaha clean, and Ace caught the last hit on the crash and let it ring open, and Elizabeth pulled the bow off the string, and the cymbal decayed alone for four seconds before the room knew it could clap.

---

The hall did not come up all at once. A section near the back started it. Then the front. Then the middle filled in. Not thunder. A wide, uneven sound, a sound of four hundred and forty-three separate decisions. Jackie sat at the bench and his hands were shaking on his thighs and the sweat under his collar had come forward to his neck. His throat was hot from the high note. The A-flat key under his left thumb was still warm from his thumb.

He stood. Elizabeth was already walking off stage-right. Ace had lifted the snare and was carrying it. Max slung the Precision. Jackie followed them through the wing curtain and felt the change in the air where the stage light ended and the backstage light began, a few degrees cooler, and the Martin D-28 was where he had left it on its stand, and he walked past it without looking at it, because it had done its job, which was to be the thing he did not pick up.

**END CHAPTER 27**


CHAPTER 28: NICK'S PERFORMANCE

He was in the wings when Jackie's voice cracked.

Nick had been watching the gap between the curtain and the proscenium wall — a slice of stage, the back of Jackie's head at the piano — running the set in his head the way a guy runs a road before he drives it. Then Jackie hit the note in *Somebody to Love* and the note did not come out the way eleven years of lessons had built it to come out. It cracked. The auditorium did the thing four hundred and forty-three people do when they are holding one breath together — a half-second where nobody coughed.

Nick felt it in the knuckle of his left hand.

He had come up the side stairs planning to drop a hammer on Jackie — louder, faster, dirtier — and what had just happened out there was not something a hammer could answer. The set was the set. Four songs.

The Tele was Mr. Wentworth's. Late-sixties butterscotch blonde, clearcoat rubbed off where the teacher's forearm had lain for twenty years. Wentworth had met him at the music-room door at seven, handed it over in its gig bag without taking it out, and said *Back Friday*, and then, two steps away, over his shoulder, *The neck'll pull sharp under the lights — keep your ear on the B.* No speech. Nick had not trusted his voice to say thanks.

He wore his least-torn jeans. A black t-shirt with nothing on it. Dan's work boots, a size too big, two pairs of socks, the left heel lifting a quarter-inch when he walked.

Crown Jewels came off the way bands come off a stage they have just done the thing on. Jackie passed him six feet out, did not look at him. The bass player touched Nick's shoulder with two fingers going by. Nick did not try to say what that meant.

"Walking the Line," the stage manager said, not loud. "On deck."

He rolled his neck once. Closed his left hand, opened it. The tips of his fingers hit the calluses and stopped, and the calluses meant the hand was still his. He followed Stephen and David and Mark through the curtain-break.

The heat hit first. Four par-cans twelve feet up, putting the light down the way a kitchen window in August puts the sun down. His scalp started sweating before he reached the monitor. The stage smelled of somebody else's hairspray and the gaffer's tape on the setlist. The microphone smelled of Lysol. Below the lip of the stage the first three rows were pale ovals and the rest of the house was dark. He found his mother in the third row without trying. Purse on her lap, both hands on the clasp.

Stephen's Univox was humming, cord curled at his ankle the way Stephen kept it. David sat behind his uncle's Ludwig with the cracked crash riveted back together by a brass washer his father had drilled on the basement bench. Mark was at the borrowed Rhodes, the one that buzzed on the B below middle C. He had his thumb off that key already.

Nick plugged the Tele into the Fender Twin on loan from the band room. He hit the low E with the side of his thumb. The amp said yes. He ran the volume to seven, the tone to eight, and stepped to the microphone. He did not lean into it.

"Thanks," he said.

One word. He stepped back.

He did not look for anyone else. Not yet.

He did not look at the wings stage-right where Jackie had gone.

David counted it under his breath — *one-two-three-four* — and Nick dropped the opening riff of *Walk This Way* on the downbeat.

The riff was Joe Perry's thing — three fingers on the fretboard, the pick on the downstroke, the muted chug between the two accents, the accent on the two-and, and the auditorium got hit with it at once because that was how the song worked. You did not ease into *Walk This Way*. It came in at your waist and lifted. Stephen's bass got underneath him on the second bar. David's kick tucked in. Mark laid a Rhodes chord on top like a sheet going over a bed.

Nick did not sing the verses. Tyler's syllables — *backseat lover on the side of the road* — lived in a mouth nobody on this stage had. He carried the vocal line on the top two strings of the Tele, octaved, syllable-tight, and the chorus he hit with the open A and let the band find him. The northeast section came up out of their seats on the third bar. A kid in front yelled something that was not a word.

They ended on the tag. David hit the crash and the brass washer rattled against the bronze and the cymbal sounded like a trash-can lid. The house was loud.

Nick waited for it to come down. He did not speak. He turned the Tele's volume knob a half-notch and looked at Stephen.

Stephen started *Sweet Emotion*.

The descending figure Tom Hamilton had built the song on — low G, F-sharp, E, D. Stephen had practiced it in his bedroom in North Sudbury for a month and played it now like he had owned it his whole life. Nick let him walk alone for four bars. Mark laid a Rhodes pad under, barely there. The house got quiet because the song wanted the house quiet.

Then Nick brought the talk-box line in without a talk-box. You couldn't buy a Heil for love or money on Dutton Road. He had worked the part out with the tone knob pulled halfway off and a borrowed wah held at the sweep-middle under his boot. He fingered the line on the D and G strings and rocked the toe of the boot in a slow quarter-inch arc, and what came out of the Twin was not a talk-box; it was a nasal rub, a close-mouthed vowel, the shape of Joe Perry's trick without Joe Perry's gear. It worked because it had to work.

In the third row Grace Johnson did a thing Nick caught only in the corner of his eye — she let go of her purse clasp. Her hands lay flat in her lap.

He did not look again. The song demanded the fretboard.

They took it through the break and out the far end, and when David hit the final crash the feedback off the Tele caught the Twin and went to a squeal, high and clean, and Nick brought his right palm down on all six strings at the bridge and the squeal died mid-note. His palm stung. He did not look at it.

He nodded at Mark. Mark laid in the one-bar figure for the next one on the Rhodes. Nick hit the riff.

*Train Kept A-Rollin'*.

The Aerosmith version — heavier, meaner, the stutter on the downbeat Joe Perry had built off Jeff Beck and made his own. The stutter was in the pick hand. Nick muted with the heel of his right palm and the riff came out of the Twin like somebody hitting sheet metal with a tire iron in rhythm. Stephen locked on. David's kick took the stutter and doubled it. Mark kept off the B.

Halfway through the second verse he felt the tremor. It started in his left pinkie — the shake that had been showing up in rehearsals since Sunday, the one he had not told anybody about — and he fingered it out by tightening his grip a half-pound. The train kept rolling. The taste at the back of his throat was copper.

They took it out on the double-time and David hit the crash and the cracked cymbal rattled again, and Nick stood with the Tele against his hip and his chest heaving.

Three down. One left.

He looked at Mark.

Mark played the piano intro of *Dream On* on a Rhodes that buzzed on the B below middle C.

The Rhodes was not a piano. It had a bell in the top that a Steinway did not have, and it rolled in a way an upright did not roll, and when Mark laid the intro down it was not the Tyler piano part from the record — it was Mark's version of it, on a borrowed electric piano with a buzzing B, and it was the thing Nick had been hearing since the summer he was fourteen in the garage on a nicked-up copy of the first album.

Nick came in on the open A.

He did not sing. He played the vocal line on the Tele, single-note, clean tone, no wah, Wentworth's forearm-worn butterscotch handling the melody the way his mother handled a plate she didn't want to break. Stephen went to whole notes. David went to the ride. Mark stayed off the B.

He let his eye travel. He did not want to. He found her again — third row, aisle seat, the navy dress she had worn to the Curtis talent show in '75, to St. Elizabeth's on Sundays, and to the graveside at Beechwood on a Wednesday in November seventeen months ago when the ground had not yet frozen. Her purse was in her lap. Her hands were not on the clasp anymore. Her hands were flat on the dress the way they had been at the kitchen table the morning after, waiting for the next thing.

She was looking at him and she was not looking away. Across four hundred and forty-three people he held her there for the length of the solo's pickup bar. If he held her any longer he would not finish the song. He looked back down at the fretboard.

The solo came up. The Dan solo. The one Dan had bent over him in the garage one Sunday afternoon the summer he was fourteen and put the fingering into his left hand with a hand of his own — a hand that had hefted ninety-pound bags of Quikrete and turned a wrench on Fords since 1952. *Thumb anchored, Nicky. Bend on the second. Breathe on the pause.* Nick took it slow. He took it the way Dan had taken it. The bend on the second fret bit his fingertip because the pad there was not the pad he had rehearsed with — that was the acoustic's pad — and the Tele's strings were lighter and cut differently. He let the bite happen.

He came out of the solo and let the band ride the chord. Mark held the Rhodes. Stephen went long. David went to a wash on the crash.

Nick did not sing *Dream on, dream on*. There was no Steven Tyler on the Lincoln-Sudbury stage. He played the melody of the last chorus on the top string of the Tele, clean, and it cut the par-can haze and went out over the third row and the fourth and the back of the house, and he came down off the high note and back to the A, and Mark closed it on the Rhodes chord and David let the ride die on its own.

For a half-second the house was quiet the way Jackie had made it quiet.

Then it came.

The applause was not the *Walk This Way* applause. It was something else. Nick did not have a word for it. He stood with the Tele against his hip and did not lift his arm. He nodded once, not at anybody in particular.

He unplugged the Tele. The cord coiled at his feet the way Stephen's always coiled at Stephen's. He laid the Tele across the top of the Twin the way Mr. Wentworth had shown him — flat, strings up, not leaning. Stephen was saying something behind him that had the word *Nicky* in it and ended with a clap on his shoulder.

Nick walked off stage left.

In the wings the stage manager had a clipboard and a pencil and a headset on one ear. He did not look up.

"Ready Rumours," the stage manager said into the headset.

Nick walked past him. His left hand was shaking a small shake and he put both hands in the pockets of his jeans and kept walking.

**END CHAPTER 28**




CHAPTER 29: PATTY'S PERFORMANCE

Patty kicked her boots off stage-right and stepped onto the riser barefoot. The stage wood was warm where the lights had been cooking it for two hours. Nick's band had left a smell behind — sweat and amp dust and the faint burnt-resistor tang of a Marshall that had been run hard — and under that, because she carried it with her, there was the faint sour-sweet smell of pig on her flannel, which she knew, and was not going to apologize for to a room of four hundred and forty-three people.

She set the Yamaha on its strap. The scratch along the lower bout caught the gel light the blue of a bruise. The soft case lay open behind her amp, the shoelace she tied it shut with trailing out over the edge of the riser.

Robin came up on her left with the bass. Kristy took the Fender and the second mic. Steve Bankuti was already behind the kit, a kid from Sudbury Southeast whose father drove a milk route; he adjusted his snare stand one quarter-turn and then sat with his sticks crossed on his knee like a boy waiting to be told he was allowed to stand up at the dinner table.

Patty leaned into the mic. She did not say anything. She tuned her B string — B was always the one — and she nodded at Robin.

In the wings stage-left Nick was leaning on a flight case with a towel around his neck. In the wings stage-right, across the width of the proscenium, Jackie was standing the way Jackie stood when he was trying not to be seen, hands at his sides, the black piano jacket still buttoned. Patty saw both of them the way you see a thing you are not going to look at twice. She looked back at the neck of the guitar.

Steve's kick pedal squeaked on beat two. A small high squeak, a dry hinge, the way his father's truck door squeaked. Patty smiled — a small unmeant smile, because something had surprised her — and went into the opening figure of "Dreams."

*Now here you go again, you say you want your freedom.*

She held the word *go*. Not long, the way a singer shows off a hold, but long enough. Her C chord rang out clean under it, the one she had botched in the empty St. Michael's on Monday night and laughed at out loud — her ring finger sat in the third fret now and did not buzz. The auditorium came down off the high Aerosmith ride it had been on. A JV booster-club mother halfway down the aisle with a cardboard tray of Cokes stopped mid-stride between booths and did not put the tray down; she only stopped. In row H a man put his arm around his wife's shoulder and left it there. The held note came down onto the next line and Patty let it.

She did not talk between songs. She tuned the B again. Robin adjusted a tuning peg. Kristy sipped water. Steve wiped his palms on his jeans. The room waited the way a room waits when it has agreed, without anyone saying so, that the person on the stage gets to choose the pace.

She'd cut "Landslide" at breakfast. Her mother had set down the coffee pot and Patty had said it out loud to the kitchen — *not tonight* — and her mother had nodded and not asked why. Three songs now, not four. She'd told the band in the dressing room ten minutes ago and nobody had argued.

She counted them in for "Silver Springs."

This one she had not learned for Nick and Jackie. She had learned it last week in the fluorescent-lit basement of St. Michael's from a cassette Kristy had dubbed, and she had sung it out loud to four middle-aged parish women who came on Thursdays to arrange the altar flowers, and those four women had stood in the doorway of the basement with their hands in their cardigan pockets and listened. Her grandmother had sung in a kitchen in Sudbury Southeast to a radio on top of a Frigidaire in 1954. Her mother had sung in the same kitchen in 1966 with a baby on her hip. When Patty came into the long held second chorus — *you'll never get away from the sound of the woman that loves you* — she was not thinking about Nick. She was not thinking about Jackie. She was thinking about the four women at St. Michael's and the two women in her kitchen, and the private inheritance that lived in her hands was what went out into the microphone. The note bloomed. Kristy came in on the second *loves you* with a harmony a third below, and the two voices braided and held, and Steve's ride cymbal shimmered under it like water moving over flat stones.

The man in row H had his face in his hand. Patty could see him because the house lights were half up, the way Lincoln-Sudbury ran the Battle so the judges could take notes; she could see the whole first fifteen rows as clearly as she could see her own fingers. She let the chorus fall and stepped back off the mic and tuned the B one more time.

Robin looked at her. Patty nodded.

Steve's count-in on the sticks was a count-in she had been hearing in her head since sometime last November — four clean clicks of maple on maple — and then the bass came in on the Lindsey Buckingham pulse, eighth notes driving, and Kristy's rhythm guitar chunked down on the one, and Patty opened her mouth and sang the song that had been living under her breath since the jazz band room.

*Loving you / isn't the right thing to do.*

The hum had been in her for seven months. It had caught on her flute G key in Mr. Wentworth's room with Nick's amp plugged in and Jackie's hands on the keys. It had caught in her throat on the eve, a splinter in a finger, a sow sighing out in the dark barn. It had sat folded with the seven letters in the back of the camp notebook. Now it came all the way out. Not a fragment. Not a hum. The whole song, at full voice, in front of four hundred and forty-three people, her bare feet on a warm stage, the Yamaha FG-180 hanging off her shoulder by a hand-me-down strap.

The chorus came around the first time and the room found her. By the second chorus Kristy's harmony was over her like a second sky, and Robin's bass was walking the Buckingham line the way the record walked it, and Steve hit the snare crack on the two and the four the way a carpenter drives a nail — one hit, clean, no flourish. Patty saw Nick at the back of the stage-left wing for one beat. She saw Jackie at the back of the stage-right wing for one beat. They were not looking at each other. They were both looking at her. She did not hold either look. She went back to her fingers.

*You can go your own way. Go your own way.*

She sang the bridge and the last chorus and let the last chord ring, and she muted it with the flat of her right palm on the strings.

One beat of silence.

Then it came. Not polite. Rows standing up in a wave that started in the middle and went outward. The unstuck acoustic of the auditorium, four hundred and forty-three throats and twice that many hands. Some man near the back was shouting something Patty could not parse through the rest of it, a single syllable over and over, maybe her name, maybe just a sound. The JV booster mother was still standing in the aisle with the tray of Cokes and she was clapping with the tray balanced on her forearm.

Patty lifted the Yamaha off her shoulder. She knelt on the riser and laid it in the soft case and pulled the zipper as far as it would go and tied the shoelace through the broken pull tab, the knot she used at home to shut the barn gate. She picked up her boots by the laces in her left hand and the case in her right.

She walked off stage-left, past the monitor, past the flight case Nick had been leaning on — he had moved — and into the dim of the wing.

Kristy was there. Kristy put a hand on her shoulder and left it there for about two seconds and then took it off.

Steve was there. Steve did not know what to do with his face. He said, "Hi."

"Hi," Patty said.

She stood for a minute in the wing with her boots in her hand and listened to the applause do what applause did when it was not going to stop on its own, which was go on until somebody on the crew brought the house lights up the rest of the way.

**END CHAPTER 29**



CHAPTER 30: THE ANNOUNCEMENT

The judges' room smelled like burned coffee and indecision.

It was the guidance-office conference room off the main hall, requisitioned for the night — four metal folding chairs around a formica table, the fluorescent tube overhead hitting an uneven ballast, a low hum riding under the voices like a note somebody had left on. The coffee urn on the warmer by the door had been brewed at five and was now the color of wet tar. The cafeteria smell of sloppy joe leftovers came in every time somebody cracked the door. Nobody had cracked the door in twenty minutes.

Maxanne Sartori sat with her back to the door, her fingers wrapped around a Styrofoam cup she hadn't drunk from in twenty minutes. Across from her, Carter Alan leaned on his elbows, jaw working. Chuck Morrison had his legal pad covered in notes he kept crossing out, the yellow legal yellow against the gray formica. Bob Walker from Strawberries stood at the window watching the parking lot fill with headlights as families arrived for the announcement.

"We've been at this forty minutes," Carter said.

"I'm aware." Maxanne set the cup down. A ring bloomed on the table beneath it.

"Walking the Line played the tighter set," Chuck said. He tapped his pen against the pad. "Johnson's guitar work on *Dream On* — that wasn't a cover. That was a conversation with the original. Kid played like he had something to prove and nothing to lose."

"Crown Jewels had the superior arrangement," Bob said from the window, not turning. "The Marsh kid's piano on *Somebody to Love* — I've heard session players with less control. And the sister on violin. That wasn't rock. That was architecture."

Carter looked at Maxanne. They were all looking at Maxanne.

She'd been quiet through most of it. Listening. Running the same calculation she'd been running since both bands walked off — a calculation she already knew the answer to because she'd written the equation herself, two and a half years earlier, in a clause buried on page four of a contract no seventeen-year-old would read twice.

"How do you choose between authenticity and excellence?" she said.

The room went still.

Chuck stopped tapping. Bob turned from the window. Carter's jaw went slack for half a second before he caught it.

"Maybe you don't," Maxanne said.

She pulled the contract from her bag. Four pages, photocopied on the Sudbury High office machine, the toner already fading at the margins. She opened to page four and laid it flat, her index finger landing on Section 7, Subsection C.

"'In the event of a tied decision among the judging panel, prize money shall be divided equally between tied parties. Studio recording time, as specified in Section 3, shall be designated as shared time, to be used collaboratively or forfeited in full.'"

Carter read it twice. "You wrote this."

"I wrote the whole contract."

"When?"

"Fall of '76. In the pitch meeting with Tom Andrews. Before I left the station."

"Before you'd heard either band play."

Maxanne picked up her cup and drank the cold coffee. It tasted like pennies. "I heard them both play in '75. Curtis Middle School talent show. They did a mashup — Aerosmith and Queen. Two thirteen-year-olds who had no business sounding that good together. I wrote the tie clause because I thought there was a chance — a small one — that they'd grow up and both be too good to choose between."

"That's not judging," Chuck said. "That's engineering."

"Yes," Maxanne said. "It is."

Bob Walker sat down. The chair squeaked against the linoleum. "So we're calling it a tie."

"I'm not calling anything. I'm asking you three a question. Can you honestly pick one?"

Silence filled the room the way water fills a glass — completely, with nowhere for air to go.

Carter shook his head first. Then Chuck. Bob looked at his hands and said, "No."

"Then it's a tie."

---

The auditorium held four hundred and forty-three people and sounded like it held twice that. Nick Johnson stood stage left with Stephen Murphy, David Cassel, and Mark Pianka behind him. The Telecaster hung from his shoulder, the strap digging into a sunburn he'd gotten helping his mother reshingle the garage roof on Saturday. Jackie Marsh stood stage right, Elizabeth beside him with her violin case at her thigh. Ace and Max Wesslen flanked them.

Between the two bands: twelve feet of stage boards taped in black gaffer at four-foot intervals, and a single Shure SM58 on its chrome stand, the cable coiled at the base.

Maxanne walked to the microphone. The house noise dropped to a murmur, then to breathing.

"This has been one of the finest competitions I've witnessed in my career." The FM warmth that had introduced Aerosmith to Boston in '73 came out of her like she'd never left the booth. "Both bands performed at a level that would be remarkable for professionals. For high school musicians, it borders on the extraordinary."

She paused. Let the silence build. She knew how to use dead air the way a drummer knew how to use the space between beats.

"The judges' decision is unanimous. The 1979 WBCN Battle of the Bands ends in a tie."

The auditorium inhaled.

Then the noise came — not cheering, not booing, but the confused electric sound of four hundred and forty-three people trying to process something that wasn't in the script. Murmuring swelled, broke into scattered applause, died back to murmuring. A whistle from the back, high and piercing.

"Prize money of five hundred dollars will be split equally. Two hundred and fifty dollars to Walking the Line. Two hundred and fifty dollars to Crown Jewels."

She held up the contract. The paper caught the par-can light and glowed.

"One hundred hours of recording time at WBCN Studio C on Boylston Street has been awarded as shared time. Per the contract, that time must be used collaboratively by both bands — or it is forfeited entirely."

Now the murmuring stopped. Now the auditorium understood.

Nick's head turned toward Jackie. Jackie's head turned toward Nick. Twelve feet of stage boards between them. The microphone stand at knee height. The paper Maxanne was holding up thin enough that the light came through the margins.

Maxanne stepped back. "Gentlemen. Come forward and sign the award acknowledgment."

They walked from opposite directions and met at the microphone like two tides converging. Maxanne held the contract between them. Nick took the left edge. Jackie took the right. They stood shoulder to shoulder holding the same piece of paper, and neither one looked at the other.

Nick stared at the contract. The words swam. Two hundred and fifty dollars. His mother could use it. His mother could always use it. The studio — a hundred hours in a professional room on Boylston — came with a chain: shared, collaborative, with Jackie Marsh, who had not spoken to him since the funeral.

Jackie stared at the contract. His father would tell him to forfeit. Walk away clean. Take the money and the moral victory and pack for Juilliard.

They signed. Separate pens. Same line.

---

In the third row on the left, Grace Johnson stood.

She didn't applaud. She didn't shout. She stood the way she'd stood at Dan's funeral — straight-backed, dry-eyed, holding herself together with the same iron discipline that got her through double shifts and mortgage payments and PTA meetings. She'd left Friendly's at four and changed at home: the navy dress with the buttons, the dress she'd worn to Dan's funeral, the dress she'd worn to Curtis Middle School in '75 to watch her twelve-year-old play Aerosmith with the Marsh boy on piano. She would not wear it again after tonight. Her winter coat was folded over the seat behind her because she had nothing else to carry.

She watched her son on the stage holding a contract with the Marsh boy, and she thought about Dan. Dan who would have been in the front row. Dan who would have been shouting loud enough to embarrass Nick. Dan who would have grabbed both boys in a bear hug and said *that's how you do it* and meant every syllable.

Two hundred and fifty dollars.

*Take it*, Dan said in her head, as clear as if he were sitting one seat to her right. *We need it.*

Grace sat down. She folded her hands in her lap and waited.

---

Three rows back and across the aisle, Dr. Richard Marsh unclenched his hands.

He had not realized he had been gripping the armrests. The wooden fronts had left red grooves across his palms, two parallel tracks in each hand. He stared at the marks a moment, and then he looked up at his son.

Jackie stood in the stage lights with someone else's contract in his hand, and for one flashing unbearable second Dr. Marsh saw himself at twenty-two. A practice-room Steinway, the fallboard lowering into place, the sheet music going into a box he would seal before sundown. The rational choice. The correct choice.

The pinky of his left hand uncurled once, on its own, an octave-reach his thumb had not been asked to make since he was twenty-two.

He closed his hands again. Gently this time. The marks would fade.

---

In the back row, Patty Lally sat alone.

She hadn't won. She hadn't officially placed. Her name wasn't on the contract, wouldn't appear in the Sudbury Town Crier's write-up, wouldn't be mentioned when people talked about the Battle at the diner tomorrow morning.

But she'd played *Go Your Own Way* barefoot on that stage with her eyes closed and her voice wide open, and for four minutes and thirty-eight seconds, the auditorium had belonged to her.
