To a girl from the Sudbury Southeast pig farms who sang Fleetwood Mac because Stevie Nicks understood what it meant to love something that was already leaving.

She hummed it now. Quiet. Just for herself. *Go your own way.*

People were filing out. The parking lot would be chaos — Suburbans and pickups and Volvos trying to exit through the same two lanes, families clumping on the sidewalk, talking in that animated way that meant everyone had an opinion and no one was sure what had happened.

Patty watched them go. She didn't move. She'd wait until the auditorium was empty, and then she would walk. Three miles on Route 20 past the dairy farm and the reservoir, humming the whole way. 

---

Outside, the mid-May night was the temperature of a held breath. Sixty degrees. Damp. The maples along the high school drive were leafing out and the new leaves caught the sodium arc lamps in the parking lot and threw shadow patterns across the asphalt. From the Sudbury Dump a half-mile west, the spring peepers were at it — thin silver sound carrying on the low air the way only spring-peeper sound carries.

Nick and Jackie emerged from separate doors, the contract folded between them like a custody agreement for something that hadn't been born yet. Lights were on in kitchen windows down the hill. Television glow through curtains. The distant hiss of Route 20 traffic going east toward Wayland.

Somewhere in that town, two hundred and fifty dollars would pay an insurance bill. Somewhere else, two hundred and fifty dollars would sit in a savings account already thick with Juilliard money, barely noticeable.

A hundred hours of studio time hung in the air between them, unclaimed.

Grace Johnson came out the lobby door with her winter coat over her arm and walked to the wood-paneled '67 wagon in the second row of the lot. She opened the driver's door. The dome light came on briefly and then went off. The wagon started on the second turn of the key, the way it did in weather that wasn't cold, and she sat for a moment with her hands on the wheel before she put it in gear.

On Route 20 a quarter mile east, Patty Lally was already walking, barefoot boots in her left hand, the guitar case in her right, the hum in her throat.

**END CHAPTER 30**


CHAPTER 31: THE GARAGE, MIDNIGHT

Stephen Murphy's truck smelled like wet retriever and gear oil. Nick sat in the passenger seat with his window cracked two inches. May air, fifty-something, damp — the kind of late-spring New England night that put fog on the inside of the glass. Sodium lights made the wet asphalt of the Lincoln-Sudbury lot look like a record under a needle.

Grace had taken the wagon home twenty minutes ago. Navy dress, Friday double at Friendly's, exhausted. She'd said *come home when you come home* and touched his elbow and gone.

Stephen was inside helping David load the Ludwig. Nick had said he'd wait. He did not want to walk back through the auditorium. Did not want the handshakes, the *great job* from people who meant it and the *great job* from people who didn't.

A tie. He rolled the word against his teeth. Two hundred and fifty dollars. The '67 wagon's transmission had been slipping in second since March. The money would delay it, not fix it.

He clenched his jaw.

Knuckles on the passenger window.

Nick's hand went to the door lock before he saw who it was. Jackie Marsh stood outside the truck in his father's peacoat, collar turned up, breath ghosting. The coat Dr. Marsh had worn on a hundred Saturday mornings back when they were twelve and the world was simple.

Nick rolled the window down another three inches.

Jackie held up the contract. The paper was already softening at the folds. "We have to decide about the studio time."

"You take your half."

"Doesn't work that way." Jackie turned the contract toward the light. "Says *shared*. Together or forfeit."

"Then we forfeit."

"A hundred hours. Professional studio. Engineer."

"I know what it is."

"Do you know what it's worth?"

Nick looked at him through the gap. Jackie's face was half-lit, half-shadow, split down the middle.

"What do you want me to say?" Nick asked.

"I want you to get out of the truck."

Stephen came out of the side door carrying the kick drum by its lug.

"Stevie. Run us to my house?"

Stephen looked at Jackie, looked at Nick. He was a big kid who did not ask questions you didn't invite. "Yeah. Lemme get the floor tom."

---

Stephen drove them up Route 20 past the Store 24 with the Ludwig rattling under a moving blanket. Nobody spoke. The dash light made Jackie's cheekbone the yellow of an old instrument's varnish. At Horse Pond Road the Chevy with the bad belt was out again, and Stephen lifted two fingers off the wheel in recognition.

He turned up Dutton Road. The Johnson house sat where it always sat, white paint going gray on the north side, bay window dark, kitchen light on. Stephen put it in park.

"Call me tomorrow."

"Yeah."

Jackie got out first. Nick followed, carrying his guitar case by the handle Dan had re-riveted the summer he was fifteen. The pickup reversed out of the driveway and took its tail lights down Dutton toward Stephen's own kitchen and his own night.

---

Nick put his shoulder to the bay door.

The springs groaned at eleven forty-five the way they had groaned at eleven forty-five for twenty years, a two-tone complaint that belonged to the house the way a throat-clearing belongs to a man. The door rose. The smell hit them both.

Sawdust and motor oil. WD-40 and cold concrete. Dan Johnson's workshop, preserved past the point where anyone had decided to preserve it. The tools hung on pegboard in the Sharpie outlines Grace had drawn so Dan would put them back right — a system Dan had ignored and Grace had enforced, each outline a ghost of the tool it held.

The bare sixty-watt bulb on exposed Romex. The 1975 April Aerosmith calendar above the workbench, frozen on Joe Perry in a white scarf. On the Maxwell House lid beside it, Dan's 9/16ths socket, set there the week after the funeral and not moved since.

The red toolbox sat under the calendar. Latch shut. Paint chipped at the corners. Nick did not look at it.

Jackie stopped in the doorway.

He hadn't been here since the summer before eighth grade. The Brubeck summer. Three weeks on "Take Five" because Jackie said the time signature was impossible and Nick said impossible was just a word for things that hadn't been done yet. Dan had brought them lemonade in Mason jars with too much sugar and sat on the workbench with his boots swinging, listening like a man watching a magic trick he could not explain.

"It's the same," Jackie said.

"Yeah." Nick plugged in the amplifier. The tubes warmed to a low hum that settled in under the bulb's own hum a whole tone lower. "Sit down or go home."

Jackie sat. He pulled a folding chair to the middle of the floor and looked around for a keyboard, and his eyes went — once, and then away — to the red toolbox under the calendar, and then back.

"Behind the salt," Nick said.

Leaning against the workbench, behind a bag of Morton's road salt Dan had never put out, was the Wurlitzer 200A Jackie had left there the spring of '76 — spring of the mashup — its power cord coiled on top like a sleeping snake. Jackie pulled it out. Three keys stuck. Middle C had a hairline crack that bent the timbre toward a small broken bell. He pressed it twice, listening.

Nick strapped on the Stratocaster. '72, tobacco sunburst, paper-route money and a loan from a dead man he was still paying back into silence. He hit an E. The walls shivered.

"What do you want to play?" Jackie said.

"'Walk This Way.'"

"Fine."

Nick counted off — one, two, three, four — too fast. Anger tempo. His right hand attacked the riff, all downstrokes, the way Joe Perry never played it, because Joe Perry had groove and Nick had fury. The garage filled with sound. Too much. The notes piled up like cars in a chain collision.

Jackie came in on the Wurlitzer too controlled. He flattened the melody into something clean and mathematical, every note accountable, no room for accident or breath.

They crashed into each other at the chorus. Nick pushed faster. Jackie pulled tighter.

Nick stopped. The last chord rang out and died against the concrete.

"That was garbage."

"Your tempo —"

"My tempo was fine. You played it like sheet music."

"You played it like a bar fight."

Six feet of oil-stained concrete between them. The amplifier hummed.

"'Bohemian Rhapsody,'" Jackie said.

"No."

"You picked first."

Nick exhaled through his teeth. "Fine."

Jackie counted it off. Proper tempo. The opening piano chords, his hands moving without instruction, the way a tongue knows its own mouth. He played it too respectfully. Like something he was afraid of breaking.

Nick came in on guitar for the operatic section and overplayed. The notes came out strained and sharp.

It fell apart at the hard rock section. Nick heavy. Jackie theatrical. Two different bands in the same room.

Silence.

"We can't do this," Jackie said.

"No."

"We sound like strangers."

"We *are* strangers."

The amplifier hummed. A car passed on Dutton Road, headlights sweeping across the small window above the workbench and vanishing. Somewhere in the house a faucet ran and stopped.

The side door opened.

Grace stood in the frame, backlit by the kitchen. She had changed out of the dress into the Friendly's sweatshirt she wore in the house, and the white Bradlees sneakers with the new insoles. Her hair was down. Her face was tired the way it was always tired now, a tiredness that went past sleep into something structural.

She looked at Nick. She looked at Jackie. She looked at the Wurlitzer plugged in on the folding chair.

"Your father would've liked that," she said. "Both of you winning."

She went back inside. The door closed with a click.

---

The silence after was different from the silences before. Those had been walls. This one was a window.

Nick sat on the edge of the workbench. He put the guitar in his lap and muted the strings with the heel of his hand. Jackie stared at the closed door where Grace had been.

"Don't," Nick said.

"He used to bring us lemonade."

"I said —"

"In Mason jars. With too much sugar. He'd sit right where you're sitting."

Nick's throat closed. He swallowed against it. The bench was solid under him, real — Dan had built it from reclaimed oak, sanded it smooth enough to run a palm across. He could feel the grain through his jeans.

"Remember the Brubeck?" Jackie said.

Nick's hands loosened on the strings. A harmonic escaped — thin, bright, gone.

"Yeah."

"'Take Five.' You said the time signature was impossible."

"You said that."

"Did I?"

"You sat right there and said *five-four is impossible, nobody plays in five-four*, and I said *Brubeck does*, and you said *Brubeck is an alien.*"

Something moved in Jackie's face. Not a smile — too much scar tissue for that — but the muscles around his eyes loosened and for a second he was the kid with lemonade in a Mason jar who believed music was the only honest language.

"Want to try?" Jackie said.

"Try what?"

"You know what."

Nick did know. Summer of '76. "Sweet Emotion" into "Bohemian Rhapsody." Three afternoons bridging them — the Aerosmith riff transposed into D minor because D minor was the key center of the Queen ballad section. It should not have worked. It worked.

Nick counted off slower this time. Not angry. Not controlled. Just honest.

His guitar found the "Sweet Emotion" bass line clean, no distortion, letting the notes breathe the way Dan used to say you let wood breathe before you sealed it. Once. Twice. Finding the groove.

Jackie played a chord. Just one. D minor. He let it hang. Then he played it again and added a ninth, and the chord opened up like a door into a room that had been locked for two years.

Nick heard it. His hands responded before his brain could object. Same pattern, different color. The Aerosmith groove sat inside the Queen harmony and the two songs began to breathe the same air.

Jackie added the melody. Tentative. *Is this the real life* over the bass groove of "Sweet Emotion." Nick looked at him once — Jackie had his eyes closed, his right foot tapping quarter notes on the concrete, the heel of his loafer catching grit — and looked back at the fretboard.

The Stratocaster started to sing the way it only sang when Nick forgot to be angry, when his hands stopped fighting the instrument and started talking to it. Fragments of "Walk This Way" crept in as texture, as color, not a separate song but a thread in the weave.

The tools vibrated on the pegboard. A screwdriver walked to the edge of its hook and hung there, buzzing. A Phillips-head followed, chattering against the wood. The 9/16ths socket rolled a quarter-inch on the Maxwell House lid and stopped. The garage breathed — old wood expanding at a frequency neither instrument was playing, concrete humming under their feet, the bare joists overhead picking up a sympathetic drone that was not in either instrument but was somehow in both of them, the air itself thick with overtones no single string and no single key had produced alone. The red toolbox did not move. Jackie's eyes went to it once, the paint chipped at the corners, the latch shut, and then back to the broken middle C.

They played for eleven minutes. No structure. No arrangement. Two musicians remembering what their hands had always known and their lives had made them forget.

When they stopped, the silence rang.

Nick's fingers trembled on the fretboard. Adrenaline. Or something older.

"That's what we could have been," he said.

Jackie looked at him. Two years of silence. Two years of choosing sides and crossing streets. "We could still do it."

The phone rang.

Nick stared at the side door. Kitchen phone, audible through the frame Grace had left cracked. Four rings. Five. Grace's footsteps on the linoleum. Her voice, muffled by the wall, a long pause, then again.

The side door opened. Grace, one hand on the frame, the receiver stretched to the end of its curled cord.

"It's for both of you," she said. "The woman from the radio station."

Nick took the phone. Maxanne's voice came through clear and close, the particular urgency of someone who had been listening to something she wasn't supposed to hear.

"Get down to the station. Now."

"It's midnight."

"I know what time it is. Bring whatever you just played."

She hung up. Nick held the dead receiver and looked at Jackie.

"WBCN," he said. "She wants us there now."

Jackie looked at the Wurlitzer. At the tools on the pegboard, still faintly trembling. At the red toolbox that had not moved.

"Okay."

---

Nick pulled the bay door down to the concrete with both hands, slow, easing the springs the last six inches so the counterweight did not slam, the way Dan had taught him. He did not look at the toolbox on the way out.

Grace drove. She did not ask questions. She had put a windbreaker on over the sweatshirt and she had taken her wallet because she was Grace Johnson and she did not leave the house at one in the morning without her wallet. The '67 wagon pulled out of Dutton Road onto Route 20 east and found the center line and stayed there. The speedometer sat at forty-five. The dash light was the color of old butter. Jackie had the Wurlitzer on his lap in the back seat with the cord wrapped around his wrist.

Nobody spoke past the Wayside Inn. The wagon's timing was a half-beat off in third and Grace did not mention it, because mentioning a thing and fixing a thing were in different budgets and always had been.

They came into Boston at twelve fifty-eight AM, the skyline low and lit, the downtown skyline where Dan had stopped being alive, thirty-eight stories at the foot of the harbor. Nick looked once at its blinking red beacon and then away. Grace did not look at it at all.

**END CHAPTER 31**


CHAPTER 32: STUDIO C

The hallway smelled like tape oxide and old cigarettes. Maxanne walked ahead of them, boot heels cracking on linoleum, not looking back. She'd been in the lobby when Grace's wagon turned off Boylston — standing under the WBCN sign with her arms crossed and her leather jacket zipped to her throat, looking less like a radio legend than a woman who had just bet something she couldn't afford to lose.

Grace stayed in the car. She'd said Go the way she said everything — once, without elaboration — and Nick had climbed out and found Jackie already on the sidewalk, hands in his peacoat pockets, breath clouding, waiting.

It was 2:14 AM.

Studio C was smaller than Nick expected. He'd imagined something palatial — walls of equipment, a mixing board the size of a car, the kind of room Aerosmith or the Cars laid down the tracks Maxanne spun into gold. Instead: a room the size of his mother's living room. Acoustic panels on the walls like quilted armor. Two boom mics facing each other four feet apart. A window into the control room where a kid in a Red Sox cap sat behind a board that was, in fact, the size of a small car.

"Mike," Maxanne said, knocking on the glass. "They're here."

The kid looked up. Twenty-five, maybe. Wire-rims. The kind of enthusiasm that came off him like heat off an engine. He gave a thumbs-up through the glass and started flipping switches. The lights on the board shifted — red, green, amber — and the room hummed with the particular frequency of electronics waking up.

"Two mics," Nick said. "Where's the amp?"

"DI box." Maxanne pointed at a small metal rectangle near the right mic, a cable snaking from it to the wall. "Plug in. Mike takes the tone off the board."

"Never played through a DI."

"Tonight you have."

Jackie was turning slow in the middle of the room. An upright sat against the far wall — not the Wurlitzer with its cracked keys, but a Yamaha U3, black lacquer reflecting the overheads in long bright streaks. He lifted the fallboard. The keys were perfect. Weighted. He pressed middle C and the note bloomed through the room with a clarity that made the garage version sound like it had been played through a blanket.

"Sit," Maxanne said, pointing Jackie to the bench and Nick to a stool near the DI. "Tune up. Mike needs five minutes for levels."

She stepped into the control room and closed the door. Through the glass Nick watched her lean down to Mike's ear and say something. Mike nodded. His hands moved across the board.

Nick plugged in. Without an amp the Strat sounded thin through the monitors — all string and no body, a voice without a chest. He played a chord. The monitors shaped it, added warmth, gave it room. Mike's hands on the board. The kid knew what he was doing.

Across the four feet of studio air, Jackie ran scales. His fingers moved on the Yamaha the way a swimmer moves in open water — long strokes, effortless, technique so internalized it had gone invisible. The piano responded like it had been waiting for him.

Maxanne's voice came through the overheads, studio-clear. "Here's the deal. One take. No overdubs. No punch-ins. What you played in that garage — play it here."

Nick found Jackie through the space between the microphones. "She was listening?"

Jackie's hands stopped on the keys. "How?"

Maxanne: "I was parked at the end of your road. You were audible from the brook. I drove to the Store 24 on Twenty and called the house."

Nick thought about the kitchen window, cracked two inches because the radiator made the room too hot in May. The sound of them playing carrying through the gap, across the yard, down Dutton Road. A frequency looking for a receiver.

"One take," Jackie said.

"One take," Maxanne said.

Mike's voice, younger and less steady: "Levels are good. Whenever."

Nick and Jackie looked at each other. Four feet of studio air. Two mics. Two instruments.

"You start," Nick said.

Jackie put his hands on the keys. He closed his eyes. For three seconds, nothing. Then — D minor. The chord that had opened the door in the garage. On the Yamaha, in the acoustic treatment of Studio C, the chord did not open a door. It knocked down a wall. The overtones spiraled into the room and hung there, suspended, ringing.

Nick's hands found the bass line. The Aerosmith riff he'd played a thousand times — in the garage, at rehearsals, alone at three in the morning when sleep wouldn't come. He played it different now. The DI stripped away the distortion, the amp growl, the feedback that masked imperfection. What was left was the melody underneath. Clean. Exposed. The bones of the song without the muscle.

Jackie's left hand picked up the riff and doubled it an octave higher, and for four bars they played the same line in unison — guitar and piano, the same phrase, the same breath. Then Jackie's right hand drifted upward, and the Queen melody entered. Not sung. Played. The piano carried the vocal line the way a river carries a leaf.

The songs merged — not gently. They merged the way tributaries merge, each losing its name but not its current, the combined flow stronger than either stream alone. The Aerosmith groove became the heartbeat. The Queen melody became the breath. In the space between, something new existed, something that belonged to two boys at 2 AM who had found in the overlap of their separate obsessions a sound that was theirs.

Nick's eyes were closed. He didn't need to see the fretboard. The riff evolved — a phrase of "Walk This Way" crept in, Tyler's strut translated into six strings, and then the fragments wove together, trading, answering, carrying on the conversation they had started in a garage when they were thirteen and didn't know enough to be afraid.

The Yamaha opened up — bass to treble, thunder to glass. The operatic architecture arrived. Nick's guitar sang the counter-melody Brian May had written for three overdubbed guitars, except Nick played it on one, and the single line cut through the piano chords like light through a prism.

The music stumbled once — Nick's fingers slipped at the twelfth fret, caught themselves, turned the slip into a bend Jackie matched with a crushed note on the piano — and the stumble became part of the song, a crack that let the light in, proof that this was live and would never happen again in exactly this way.

The hard rock section hit. Nick dug in. Jackie hammered the keys. For thirty seconds they played loud enough to rattle the acoustic panels, the monitors feeding back in a loop that built and built until it crested and broke, and then —

Silence.

Two beats.

Jackie played the final chord. D minor again, where they had started, but this time with the sustain pedal down, the note ringing into the room, fading like the last light of a day neither of them wanted to end.

Nick lifted his hands from the strings. The monitors hummed. The tape rolled.

Four minutes and forty-three seconds.

---

Through the glass, Mike sat with his hands flat on the board and his mouth open. Maxanne stood behind him, one hand on the back of his chair, the other pressed flat against the glass.

She thumbed the talkback. "Stay there."

She came into the studio, crossed to the tape machine, and rewound. The reels spun backward with a whirring that filled the room like a held breath being taken back.

"Listen," she said, and pressed play.

Their own playing came back through the monitors, familiar and strange the way a recording always is, because it captured not just the music but the room, the air, the moment. Nick heard his guitar and didn't recognize it. Without distortion, without the amp, he sounded like himself. Not like Joe Perry. Not like Jimmy Page. Like Nick Johnson, seventeen, playing in the middle of the night because the music asked him to.

Jackie heard the Yamaha and his breath went short. The tape had caught everything — the classical training, the rock instinct, the war between them, and the place in the middle where the war stopped and the two traditions started listening to each other. He sounded like no one he had been trained to sound like. He sounded like what he had been afraid to become.

Jackie pressed his fingers to his eyes. His shoulders drew up. When he dropped his hands, his face was wet.

"This is what we could have been," he said.

Nick looked at him. The wetness on Jackie's face caught the studio lights.

"We could still be this," Nick said.

Maxanne leaned against the tape machine and crossed her arms. She looked at both of them the way a woman looks who has been in rooms where music happened for thirty years and knows exactly what she has just witnessed and exactly what it costs.

"You could walk away from everything," she said. "Juilliard. The Army. Forget the plan. Choose this."

The hum of the monitors was the only sound. It sounded like a question that had been asked in a hundred thousand rooms where young musicians sat across from the choice between what they were and what they were supposed to become.

Nick looked at the clock on the wall. Digital. Red numbers. 3:17 AM.

He thought about Grace.

She was in the parking lot. Right now. In a wood-paneled wagon with a hundred-and-eighty thousand miles on it, wearing a Friendly's uniform she had been in since six that morning. Twenty-one hours. She had driven him here without asking why, without complaining, without mentioning she had to be back for Sunday brunch in five hours because Sundays paid time-and-a-half.

He couldn't walk out to that parking lot and lean into the wagon's window and tell her he was throwing the Army over for a sound in a studio at three in the morning. He couldn't watch her face do the thing it did when she was terrified and wouldn't show it.

He couldn't. Not because he didn't want to. Because she had already given everything, and asking for more was a kind of theft he wasn't capable of.

"I can't," Nick said.

---

Jackie sat at the piano bench. His hands rested on his thighs, away from the keys, as if touching them might make the decision for him.

He thought about his father.

Fourteen years of lessons. Every Saturday, the drive from Sudbury to Boston — forty minutes each way on Route 9, Dr. Marsh's Buick smelling like leather and the Bach cantatas he played on the tape deck because Bach was the foundation of everything. Fourteen years of recitals, competitions, auditions. The Juilliard application his father had helped him write — three drafts, each one more careful than the last, each one a collaboration between a son who played and a father who had stopped playing and never said why.

Dr. Marsh had saved for Juilliard since Jackie was five. A separate account. Monthly deposits. Jackie had found the bankbook once, in the desk drawer in his father's study, and the column of numbers had stretched across three pages, twelve years of deposits, the date and the amount and nothing else.

His father had closed the lid on a practice-room Steinway in 1960 and had not opened a piano for himself since — a career, a stability, a rational path. Every day since, he had been pouring whatever he sacrificed into his son — not the same dream exactly, a translation of it, the version his father believed was the safe version of the dangerous thing he had wanted.

He was wrong. Jackie knew that. His father believed in the wrong version of him — the recital version, the competition version, the version that played Chopin with impeccable technique and empty hands. But the belief itself was real. The fourteen years were real. The drives, the deposits, the three drafts of the application. His father had built a bridge from his own abandoned dream to his son's future, and Jackie couldn't burn it. Not even for this.

Jackie closed his eyes. "Me either."

The words fell into the room like stones into water.

"It's not giving up," Nick said. His voice was rough. He cleared his throat and it didn't help. "It's choosing the people who already chose us."

Maxanne pushed off the tape machine. She walked to the window that looked into the control room, where Mike sat with his headphones around his neck, watching them through the glass with the expression of a man who understood he was witnessing something he would spend years trying to describe.

"That's what I told myself in 1977," Maxanne said. Her back was to them. Her reflection floated in the glass, translucent, superimposed over the meters and the blinking lights of the equipment that recorded and preserved other people's music into the dark.

Jackie stood. He looked at Nick. Nick looked back.

"If things were different," Jackie said.

"Yeah," Nick said.

Jackie extended his hand. Nick took it. They shook — one firm grip, held for two seconds, released. Not a hug. A hug would have been too much.

Nick went out through the studio door. Jackie went out through the control room. Separate exits. The hallway split and they walked in opposite directions, toward opposite ends of the building, toward the separate lives that were waiting for them the way debts wait — patient, certain, compounding.

---

Maxanne stood alone in Studio C. The acoustic panels absorbed her reflection. The boom mics angled toward empty chairs.

Mike appeared in the control room window and pressed the talkback.

"What do we do with this?" He held up the reel — seven inches of magnetic tape, four minutes and forty-three seconds, everything.

Maxanne looked at the reel in his hand. You couldn't break a band that had already broken itself.

"We preserve it," she said. "Because somebody needs to remember this was possible."

Mike passed it through the window. She took it in both hands. Light. Lighter than it should have been for what it carried.

She slid the reel into a cardboard sleeve and tucked it under her arm. She turned off the studio lights. She walked out of WBCN into the three-AM dark on Boylston, where the parking lot held one car — Grace Johnson's wagon, engine idling, headlights off — and two empty spaces where two boys had already gone.

**END CHAPTER 32**


CHAPTER 33: THE SEPARATIONS

I.
NICK
September 1979 — Fort Benning, Georgia

The barracks smelled like floor wax and sweat and Pine-Sol, and underneath all of it, red clay. Georgia clay got into everything — boots, seams, nostrils, the spaces between teeth. By the second week of basic training, Nick had stopped noticing the taste. By the fourth week, he'd stopped noticing most things.

The body adapted. That was the trick the Army knew and didn't advertise: exhaust the mind and the body takes over, and the body is obedient in ways the mind never was. Run. March. Crawl. Repeat. His legs learned distances his brain refused to calculate. His hands learned the M16 the way they'd once learned the Stratocaster — by repetition so thorough it became reflex, muscle replacing thought, fingers finding positions without being told.

The guitar lived in his footlocker. A case inside a case — he'd wrapped the Stratocaster in a bedsheet before sliding it into the battered hardshell, because the lock was broken and the latches were loose and the instrument was the one thing he owned that couldn't be replaced. The footlocker sat at the end of his bunk, regulation distance from the wall, combination lock threaded through the hasp. Thirty-eight men in the barracks. He trusted none of them with this.

After lights out, when the breathing settled into the rhythm of collective sleep — thirty-eight men inhaling and exhaling in almost-unison, the human equivalent of a room full of machines powering down — Nick opened the footlocker. The combination was his mother's birthday. He'd told himself that was for security. He knew it was for something else.

He didn't plug in. The barracks had no amplifier, and even if it had, the sound would have brought the drill sergeant and the drill sergeant would have brought consequences Nick couldn't afford. He played unplugged. The Stratocaster barely made a sound. Structure only. No voice. The notes existed in the space between his fingers and the fretboard, audible only to him and to anyone lying awake within three feet.

He played "Sweet Emotion." The bass riff, slow, the way he'd played it in Studio C. Without the amp, the melody was skeletal — the architecture of the song stripped to its beams, a blueprint you could hold in your hands. He played it every night for a week. Then he played "Take Five" in five-four time. Then he played nothing he could name, just sounds, his fingers moving on the strings the way a man paces in a small room, not going anywhere, just proving he still can.

Specialist Haywood, two bunks down, listened for a month before he spoke.

"You any good?" Haywood asked one night. Whispering. The barracks dark.

"I was."

"What happened?"

Nick played a D minor chord, so quiet it sounded like paper tearing. "Joined the Army."

The drill sergeant found him on a Tuesday. Week seven. Nick had his back against the footlocker and the guitar in his lap and his eyes closed, and he didn't hear Sergeant Decker's boots on the waxed floor because Decker walked soft when he wanted to — a skill he'd learned in two tours that he never discussed.

Nick opened his eyes. Decker stood over him. The sergeant's face was unreadable in the dark — a topography of shadows and discipline. He looked at the guitar. He looked at Nick. He said nothing.

He walked away. His boots made no sound. Nick sat in the dark and waited for the consequence that never came.

Grace wrote every week. Her letters arrived on Tuesdays in envelopes she'd bought in bulk at Woolworth's — white, standard, her handwriting tight and efficient, the same handwriting that filled out inventory forms and shift schedules and bank deposit slips. She wrote about the restaurant. The new assistant manager was stealing from the register and Grace was building a case. The wagon's timing belt finally snapped; she'd gotten a ride from Helen Gorski to the mechanic and the repair had cost four hundred dollars, which was a hundred and fifty more than the estimate but what are you going to do. The leaves were turning. Mrs. Abernathy's dog had dug up the Hendersons' tomato garden again.

She never asked about the guitar. She never asked if he was happy. She asked if he was eating and if he'd gotten the package she sent (oatmeal cookies, wrapped in wax paper, half-crushed by the postal service) and if he needed new socks.

Nick wrote back on lined paper torn from a government-issue notepad. He wrote about training — the obstacle course, the rifle range, the ten-mile march in full pack that left blisters on his heels the size of quarters. He wrote about the food, which was bad, and the weather, which was worse. He wrote about Haywood, who was from Detroit and could bench-press two-forty and sang Motown in the shower with a voice that belonged on a stage.

He didn't mention the guitar. He didn't mention the tape. He didn't mention the nights when he lay in his bunk after playing and stared at the ceiling and thought about a studio at 3 AM and a chord that sounded like the rest of his life.

The $250 had gone to Grace. She'd protested — "That's your money, your prize" — and he'd left the check on the kitchen table next to the salt shaker and said, "Cash it." She had. She'd cashed it the way she cashed everything — without ceremony, without sentiment, because money was a tool and tools were for using and sentimentality was a luxury she'd been stripped of the day they lowered Dan into the ground.

Nick lay in his bunk. Fort Benning breathed around him — thirty-eight men, red clay, wax and Pine-Sol, the distant sound of someone's radio playing country through a single earphone. He was trading his body for education. The American working-class bargain: three years of service for tuition, room, board, and the chance to become something his father never had the chance to become.

Dan would have understood. Dan had made his own bargain — his body for a paycheck, construction sites for a mortgage, physical labor for a house on Dutton Road where his son could play guitar in a garage that smelled like sawdust.

The bargain killed him.

Nick closed the footlocker. He set the combination. He lay down and stared at the ceiling until the staring became sleep.

---

II.
JACKIE
August 1979 — Manhattan

The room was nine feet by eleven. Jackie knew because he'd measured it. A twin bed, a desk, a dresser, a window that looked onto West 66th Street where taxis moved in yellow procession and the sound never stopped. His bedroom in Sudbury had been fourteen by sixteen with a view of the oak tree his mother had planted the year he was born.

The piano was better. A Steinway Model B, seven feet, in the practice room on the fourth floor. Jackie touched the keys the first time and understood what serious meant. This instrument didn't tolerate hesitation. It responded to intention the way a blade responds to pressure — immediately, precisely, without forgiveness.

He practiced six hours a day. Chopin etudes, Bach inventions, Debussy preludes. His professors praised his precision. His left hand was called exemplary. His sight-reading was called exceptional.

His roommate, Paul Nakamura from Portland, Oregon, played cello and listened to jazz and kept a turntable in the corner that he fed a steady diet of Miles Davis and Thelonious Monk.

"What kind of music do you listen to?" Paul asked, their second night.

"Classical," Jackie said.

He didn't mention Queen. He didn't mention the Crown Jewels, or the Battle of the Bands, or the Wurlitzer with the cracked middle C, or the garage on Dutton Road, or the studio at 3 AM. He said "classical" the way you close a door — firmly, with your back against it.

October. His first recital. Chopin's Ballade No. 1 in G minor. He'd been playing it since he was fifteen, and by now his fingers held every phrase with the certainty of long acquaintance. The auditorium in the Juilliard building was small — a hundred and twenty seats — but it resonated like a space designed by people who understood that sound was architecture.

He played it perfectly. Every note in its place. Every dynamic marking observed. The rubato was tasteful, the pedaling was clean, the climax built and released with the structural integrity of a well-made bridge.

Dr. Marsh attended. He flew from Boston to LaGuardia and took a taxi to Lincoln Center and sat in the third row in a suit he'd pressed himself because Jackie's mother was visiting Elizabeth at UMass that weekend. He sat with his hands on his knees and his eyes on his son's hands and his face carried an expression that Jackie recognized from fourteen years of recitals: pride. The specific, complicated pride of a man watching his son do the thing he'd been unable to do himself — not the same thing, not exactly, but close enough to feel like redemption.

After the recital, Dr. Marsh said, "That was beautiful." Two words that cost him more than Jackie would understand for another twenty years.

Jackie said, "Thank you." Two words that cost him just as much.

In November, Jackie took the train to Boston. WBCN's studio looked different in daylight — smaller, shabbier, the hallway scuffed and the carpet stained with coffee the color of old pennies. Maxanne was in Studio B, setting up for the evening show. The leather jacket was slung over the back of her chair. A cassette sat on the console — unlabeled, a new band's demo, the tape leader pulled out a quarter inch the way she pulled it when she was about to cue. The room smelled like coffee and tape oxide and the forty years of cigarettes nobody had shampooed out of the walls.

"I wanted to say goodbye," Jackie said.

Maxanne leaned against the console. "You came all the way from New York to say goodbye?"

"I was in town. Thanksgiving."

"How's Juilliard?"

"Fine. Different. We don't talk."

Maxanne's eyes narrowed. "We?"

"Nick and me. Different worlds."

A silence that tasted like tape oxide.

"You heard from him?" she asked.

"No."

She didn't press. She looked at him for a long moment and then at the cassette on the console, and then back at him, and she nodded the way people nod when they've decided not to say the thing they wanted to say.

"The music you made together—" she started.

"Was a long time ago," Jackie said.

Eight months. It had been eight months. But Jackie said it the way people say "a long time ago" when they mean "a distance I've put between myself and something I can't afford to look at directly."

His eyes moved past her to the wall behind the console, to the metal shelving where seven-inch reels in cardboard sleeves were racked spine-out, dates in black marker running down their edges. He looked at the shelf for a second longer than a glance. He looked away.

"I've got a train to catch," he said.

He took the train back to New York. He practiced Debussy. He did not play "Bohemian Rhapsody." He did not play anything in D minor. He slept in a room that was nine by eleven and dreamed in dimensions he couldn't measure.

**END CHAPTER 33**


CHAPTER 33A: THE STATION'S FINAL COMPROMISE

August 11, 2009. Seven-fourteen PM. I pull into the WBCN parking lot and kill the engine and sit.

The building looks the same from outside. Squat brick, the kind of architecture that says *functional*, a place where things get made, not displayed. The antenna rises off the roof like a finger pointing at something nobody wants to look at. The logo on the side is still backlit. Someone has already taken the promotional banner down from the front entrance. The glass doors throw the lot back at me — my car, the August sky, the heat-shimmer coming off the asphalt in slow animal waves.

I check my purse. It's there. It's always there.

The cassette sits in the zippered pocket next to a pack of tissues, a lipstick I haven't worn in months, a parking stub from a restaurant I don't remember. Thirty years it's traveled with me. Brookline to New York to LA to Nashville to wherever the corporate music machine needed a woman who could identify talent and keep her mouth shut about what talent was actually worth.

I take it out. Hold it against the windshield. The label has gone the color of weak tea. My block letters blurred by three decades of body heat and pocket friction but still legible. *Demo — Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979*. A hairline crack along one edge I noticed in '93 and Scotch-taped and have checked every few months since, the way a nurse checks vitals on a patient who isn't changing.

Under my arm, in a padded mailer I have not opened since I drove out of my own driveway in Cambridge this afternoon, is the reel. Seven-inch, cardboard sleeve, the master Mike cut in Studio C the night Nick Johnson and Jackie Marsh stopped being enemies for four minutes and forty-three seconds. The cassette is the talisman. The reel is the weapon. I have carried one for thirty years and the other for thirty years and tonight is the first night they have been in the same room since 1979.

I get out of the car.

---

The lobby is a labyrinth of cardboard.

I stop inside the entrance and let my eyes adjust. The reception desk — where for forty-one years some kid buzzed visitors through, took delivery of promo records, fielded calls from fans and artists and people who thought they were both — is stripped to its laminate shell. Computer gone. Phone gone. The chewed-pen-on-a-string gone. A box labeled RECEPTION — MISC sits with its flaps open, a tangle of phone cords and a chipped WBCN — THE ROCK OF BOSTON mug the movers couldn't be bothered with.

I pick up the mug. The ceramic is cold. I put it back.

The hallway past reception is stacked with boxes two and three high, labeled in black marker in the clinical shorthand of institutional death. STUDIO A — EQUIPMENT. ARCHIVES 1971-1980. PROMO — UNSORTED. AWARDS / PLAQUES. The Gold Records are gone from the walls. They left rectangular ghosts where the frames blocked forty years of nicotine and grime, clean patches on dirty plaster the exact shape of things that used to matter.

My shoes click on the linoleum. The building is quiet in a way I have never heard it. Not the productive quiet of between-shifts, not the focused quiet of a DJ deep in a set, but the quiet of a body that has stopped breathing. The hum is wrong. For forty-one years WBCN had a hum you could feel in the floor, electricity running through the board and signal bleeding up the cable into the tower, a heartbeat you could lean your hand on. I felt it in '73 when I was twenty-seven and running late for a shift in new boots that gave me blisters I didn't notice until April. I felt it in '76 when I pressed my hand to the corridor plaster during a Springsteen broadcast and the wall seemed to move under my fingers. That hum is gone.

Tonight's hum is mechanical. A thin synthesized wash bleeding through the closed door of what used to be Promotions. I stop. Listen. Christian contemporary test tones. Someone is in there running the automation playlist that will take this frequency at 12:01 AM. A synthesized choir rehearsing salvation to an empty room, the new tenant moving in before the old one's body is cold.

I keep walking.

---

The station manager's office is open. Lights on, nobody home.

I shouldn't go in. I've spent thirty years not doing things I should have done, so the small reverse crime of doing something I shouldn't seems about right.

The desk is cleared to three items. A telephone. A desk lamp. A document in a clear plastic sleeve, the kind you use for things that need protecting from coffee rings and casual handling. I read the header through the plastic without picking it up.

ASSET PURCHASE AGREEMENT

Between: WBCN Radio, Inc. (Seller)

And: Educational Media Foundation (Buyer)

I don't need to read further. Everybody knows the terms. The Globe reported them, the Herald reported them, the trades covered it the way medical journals cover a terminal prognosis. WBCN sold to EMF. The operators of K-LOVE and Air1. Effective August 12, 2009, 12:01 AM, the frequency that carried Aerosmith and the Cars and Boston and J. Geils and Springsteen becomes Christian contemporary.

Below the header, in a line I have not seen before, somebody — the station manager, or a lawyer with a human moment — has written in blue ballpoint:

*41 years. Thank you.*

I stand there looking at it longer than I should. Four words, and whoever wrote them didn't even bother with complete sentences. *As if the building could hear.* I almost say it out loud. As if the frequency would remember.

The phone rings. I watch it ring three times, four, five. Nobody here to answer. The phone quits and the office settles back into its quiet, except for the Christian contemporary bleeding down the corridor, the test tones louder now, confident, a synthesized choir finding its pitch.

I leave the document where it is. I leave the mug where it is. I leave the lamp on.

---

Studio B.

I push through the door and here it is. My coffin, my cathedral, my confessional. The room where I played *Dream On* for the first time. The room where Springsteen walked in wet from a Storrow Drive rainstorm and leaned into the mic and said HI MOM.

The equipment is still here. The Wheatstone board glows its familiar amber, more solder than original circuitry now. The turntables sit dusty on their platforms, needles pulled and probably packed in one of the boxes in the hall. The cart machines are empty except for one slot. Someone has left the final song.

I walk to the cart machine. A single cartridge, strip of masking tape across the top, Sharpie in someone's tidy block letters:

SHINE ON YOU CRAZY DIAMOND — PINK FLOYD — FINAL TRACK — DO NOT REMOVE

I pick it up.

The cart is warm from the machine's standby heat. I turn it over in my hand. Twenty-six minutes of Pink Floyd. The whole nine-part suite. *Wish You Were Here*, 1975. I remember the album came in to the station in a shrink wrap that smelled like the pressing plant, and I played "Shine On" in full on a Sunday night in September at 11 PM and got six phone calls during the fade and one of them was a man crying and he could not say why.

The song is for Syd Barrett. Everyone who knows Pink Floyd knows that. The founder. The crazy diamond. The kid from Cambridge with the kaleidoscope eyes who built the band and then burned out inside it, acid and schizophrenia and whatever the exact mixture was, and the remaining four wrote an entire album trying to explain to themselves why he wasn't in the room anymore. Syd died in 2006. Three years ago. In Cambridge, in a modest house, painting and gardening and not speaking to the music press. The tape in my purse is older than his entire time in Pink Floyd. He was in the band for three years. I have carried this cassette for thirty.

Someone in a conference room in North Carolina opened a brochure of Pink Floyd tracks and picked the one that's about Syd Barrett. They had no idea what they were doing. The corporate machine, accidentally honest for the first time in thirty years.

It's the right song. That's the thing I am standing here trying to swallow. It's the right goddamn song.

It's an elegy for the one who couldn't survive the industry. It's an elegy for the one who went first. It's an elegy for Syd, and for Nick — Nick who played Aerosmith covers in a garage full of a dead father's tools and then chose his mother and put the guitar in a case and went to build things with his hands — and for Jackie, who did survive, who went to Juilliard and came out whole-looking, which is a different kind of burning out that doesn't get a suite written for it. And for me. The one who left and called it ambition.

I set the cart down on the console. Gentle, the way you put down a thing you respect.

---

The engineering booth door opens and the kid comes in. Mid-twenties, Bruins hoodie, phone in hand before the door is closed. Same kid from my last shift here. They drew him short straw twice.

"Ms. Sartori. Kevin. I'll be your engineer tonight."

"Kevin. You got any bourbon?"

He blinks. "I've got Red Bull."

"That'll have to do."

He retreats to his booth. Through the glass I watch him settle in and immediately check his phone. Born in '84. He has never lived in a country where a DJ chose the music. He has never felt the electricity of a booth when someone drops a needle on a track nobody has heard and a city leans in.

John Lennon said it late in 1980, a few months before he went. About Bruce. *God help Bruce Springsteen when they decide he's no longer God.* I have watched that prophecy come true in slow motion for thirty years. Not just Bruce. All of them. The machinery that lifts kids up is the same machinery that grinds them down, and the hand at the lever does not change. Tonight the hand at the lever is a kid from North Carolina with a Pink Floyd brochure.

I reach into my bag. There is a flask in there too. Old habit. Tonight it's Heaven Hill — I switched off Pappy a long time ago, once I started paying for my own bottles and not letting the labels do it. Heaven Hill is cheaper and more honest and more me. Pappy was for rooms with A&R guys in them. Heaven Hill is for a Tuesday at the kitchen table.

I find the paper cups in the second drawer where they have always been. Pour two fingers.

I lift the cup a half inch in the direction of the cart machine. *For Syd.* I drink it.

I pour another. *For Dan Johnson.* For Grace's husband who died on a crane cable in 1977 and started all of this. Drink it.

I pour another. I hold it over the console and I don't drink it for a second, because Jackie Marsh is not dead. Jackie is at Berklee teaching music theory. Which is a different kind of not-alive but not one that takes a toast. I drink it anyway. For the Jackie who isn't teaching. For the Jackie who would have played Carnegie and didn't.

I don't pour one for Nick. Nick is in Sudbury and alive and I will not pretend otherwise tonight. I am tired of pretending.

---

I take the demo cassette out of my purse. Set it on the console, to the right of the Wheatstone, about four inches from the Pink Floyd cart. Not a plan. A memorial arrangement. The cart and the cassette side by side, the way you'd set two photographs on a mantel, the way Grace probably set Dan's watch and his wedding ring on the nightstand the first night.

Tonight it's Pink Floyd.

I can say that to myself and mean it. The corporate overlords accidentally wrote the right song. "Shine On You Crazy Diamond" is a real elegy — twenty-six minutes of Gilmour's guitar reaching for a man who wasn't coming back, saxophone in the middle like a funeral procession, Gilmour's voice finally breaking on *you reached for the secret too soon, you cried for the moon*. That's what you play when a rock and roll station dies. Not a demo tape with a hairline crack down the shell. Not two kids from Sudbury nobody knows. Not a private grudge against a labyrinth I helped build.

You play Pink Floyd. You let Gilmour take them out. You go home.

The cassette sits there next to the cart. My talisman. Thirty years of pocket heat, warm through the plastic shell. I run my thumb along the taped crack. I do not pick it up.

---

The ON AIR sign is dark. It lights at eight.

I sit down in the broadcast chair. The leather creaks the same way it creaked in 1974. I put the MDR-7506s around my neck, the old Sonys with the three black rings of electrical tape where the headband cracked in 1998, the only piece of equipment in this room older than my regrets.

Through the glass Kevin's phone lights his face pale blue.

Down the hall the Christian contemporary swells into some kind of chorus. Synthesized voices on a synthesized rising third, hopeful, ascending, praise music designed to lift you without asking you to lift yourself. The future of 104.1, rehearsing five hours early. I press my palm flat against the wall beside the console and feel the faint foreign vibration, the frequency of a building learning to speak a language it was never built for.

The padded mailer is on the console beside the cassette. I haven't opened it. I won't yet.

Forty-six minutes until I open the mic.

I crack my knuckles. One hand, then the other. I lean back in the chair. The console glows its amber the way it has glowed for thirty-five years, little lights going about their small electrical business, waiting for somebody to tell them what to do.

I wait.

**END CHAPTER 33A**


CHAPTER 34: THE LAST VOICE OF BOSTON ROCK — PART 2

Eleven-thirty. Half an hour left.

I've been talking for four hours. The battle. The tie. The 3 AM in Studio C. Dan's crane cable in '77. Grace in the third row in her good navy dress. The tie-clause I wrote in the fall of '76 in a pitch meeting before I ever left this building. I have given WBCN the longest uninterrupted monologue in its forty-one years on the rock dial and WBCN has not interrupted me, because WBCN is already gone. Only the signal is left, and the signal is waiting.

The phones have been ringing all night. I have let them ring. The board lights up in constellations, dies, lights up again. Somewhere down the hall a program director has given up and gone home. The kid through the glass — Kevin, Bruins hoodie, twenty-five, raised on Spotify — has put his phone down. He's watching me the way you watch a fire nobody lit on purpose.

Two objects on the console.

On the left, a cart. Brown plastic shell, cream label, Sharpie in somebody else's hand: SHINE ON YOU CRAZY DIAMOND — PINK FLOYD — FINAL TRACK — DO NOT REMOVE. Someone pulled it off the LP sleeve of *Wish You Were Here* this afternoon and loaded it into the NAB machine for me. Corporate's picked ending. Cued. Ready. All I have to do is punch it.

On the right, a seven-inch open-reel in a cardboard sleeve. Mike's handwriting, faded to the sepia of a shoebox Polaroid: *Nick/Jackie — un-Authorized — May 1979*. The sleeve is soft at the edges from thirty years of hands. The reel's been in my Cambridge study in a locked drawer the first of every month for three decades. I check it. I don't play it. I know what's on it. I carry the cassette of it in my purse the way a soldier carries a bullet that missed him — not as a souvenir, as proof.

The cassette sits on the console between the cart and the reel. Talisman. Witness. Unused tonight.

The ON AIR sign holds its red. Twenty-eight minutes until it doesn't.

WBCN signed on in 1968. I was twenty-four when I got here, in 1970. I'm sixty-three now. The subtraction is not simple.

---

I lean into the pop filter.

"I want to talk to you about Pink Floyd," I say. "Specifically about 'Shine On You Crazy Diamond,' which is on the cart machine in front of me, which somebody in corporate in North Carolina decided three weeks ago was how this station should end. I want to tell you why they're right."

The phones go off again. I don't look.

"The song is from *Wish You Were Here*. 1975. Gilmour wrote the opening four-note guitar phrase and when Roger Waters heard it he stopped and said, this is him. He meant Syd Barrett. Syd was Pink Floyd's founder. He named the band — took two old Carolina bluesmen, Pink Anderson and Floyd Council, and made them one word. Everything Pink Floyd became was built on top of Syd, and by the time they sat down to make *Wish You Were Here* he was already gone. Not dead. Gone. Acid, mostly. A head that couldn't come back from where he'd taken it. He was twenty-four."

I pick up the cart. Weigh it in my palm. The plastic is warm from the lamp.

"They recorded the record and one afternoon a fat bald man in a raincoat wandered into the control room at Abbey Road, and for ten minutes the band didn't recognize him. It was Syd. He listened to the song they'd written about him and he smiled politely and he went home. He died in 2006. Three years ago, July. He was sixty."

My voice is bourbon-low. Heaven Hill paper cup on the console, gone soft at the rim.

"'Shine On You Crazy Diamond' is about burning so bright at the beginning that the rest of your life is a long dim afterward. It's about the one who couldn't make the compromise and didn't survive not making it. It's about a band that built a cathedral on the bones of a friend they couldn't save. It's a nine-part suite. It opens a record and it closes a record. You can't put it on in the car. You have to sit down for it."

Kevin through the glass has his hand on the fader. Waiting for my cue.

"Corporate picked this song because it sounds like grief without committing to any. Because it's long enough to run out the clock. Because the Christian broadcasting people taking this frequency at one minute past midnight needed something that would end the old story quietly so the new story could begin. And I will say this, because I have to say it on the air before I do anything else tonight — corporate got it right. Accidentally. For the first time in thirty years of corporate getting everything wrong, somebody in North Carolina picked the correct song for a rock station going dark. Syd Barrett deserves it. Pink Floyd deserves it. The people who spent four decades listening to this frequency deserve it. 'Shine On' is the elegy. I mean that. I'm ready to play it."

I am. I mean it all the way down to the hand on the cart.

The VU meters idle. The reel to my right waits in its sleeve. The ON AIR holds red.

---

And then — nothing dramatic, just a small thing in the chest — I look at the reel.

Syd Barrett has Pink Floyd. Syd Barrett has a cathedral and a nine-part suite and a record that sold six million copies and a fat bald afternoon at Abbey Road that's been told so many times it's its own myth. Syd Barrett is fine. Syd Barrett does not need my airtime.

Nick Johnson has four minutes and forty-three seconds in a drawer in my house.

I have carried this tape thirty years and called it a lot of things in my own head. Unfinished. Unauthorized. Unsaid. Tonight, for the first time, I let myself call it the thing it always was, the thing I would never have said out loud in any room where anyone could hear me say it: the best band that never was.

"Sorry, Syd," I say into the mic.

I set the cart down. I pick up the reel.

My hands know this motion. They've known it since I was twenty-four. The reel fits the spindle like a key in a lock that was cut for it. I thread the tape across the capstan, past the heads, onto the take-up. The leader is the color of old butter.

---

"Okay," I say. "New plan. I'll get in trouble for this. It doesn't matter who with.

"May 15, 1979. Lincoln-Sudbury Regional High School auditorium. Battle of the Bands, WBCN-sponsored, four hundred and forty-three seats, every seat full. Two bands. The Crown Jewels — a kid named Jackie Marsh on the school's Yamaha grand, and three others. Walking the Line — a kid named Nick Johnson on a borrowed Telecaster, and three others. These two boys had grown up on the same brook, played on the same mini-bike trails, traded baseball cards in the same middle school cafeteria, and by the spring of '79 they weren't speaking. The town had done its work. Class did its work. A crane cable in November of '77 did its work. They tied. I made sure they could. I wrote the tie-breaker clause in the fall of '76 in a pitch meeting at this station with Tom Andrews, and I left the trap baited for three years before it closed.

"They played that tie for the room. Then at 2 AM Grace Johnson drove her son, and somebody's mother or father drove Jackie, to this building. I'd pulled a session engineer out of bed. Studio C. Two microphones four feet apart. No amp. Nick through a DI box, Jackie on a Yamaha upright. One take. No overdubs. 'Sweet Emotion' folded into 'Bohemian Rhapsody,' Aerosmith bleeding into Queen, a bar of 'Walk This Way' strutting through the middle just to see if it could. Four minutes and forty-three seconds. A stumble at the twelfth fret that Jackie caught on a crushed piano note and turned into the best sound in the recording.

"Then they shook hands in the hallway and went home to the people who'd already chosen them. Nick to the Army. Jackie to Juilliard. Me back to the label. I put the reel in a drawer. I've checked it the first of every month since Reagan. I have never played it. I am going to play it now. For you. Full volume. The way I used to, before I learned to ask permission."

The kid behind the glass has taken his hand off the fader. Something has happened to his face I will think about later.

"This is how rock was supposed to crash the silence," I say. "One time only. Stay with me."

I press play.

---

The VU meters slam red. My signature. The engineers used to hate it. The listeners never did.

D minor. Jackie's Yamaha. The chord fills the booth, fills the building, fills 104.1 FM from the Cape to the New Hampshire border — fifty thousand watts, for the last time tonight. The chord hangs in the air like a held breath the room has been waiting for since 1968.

Then Nick's guitar enters. Sweet Emotion stripped down to its bones. Clean, exposed, a boy playing at 2 AM in a room where anger did not help and he'd finally found that out. I've heard this tape two hundred and forty times in my head. I have never heard it out loud. The speakers give it dimensions my memory couldn't — the hum of the Studio C walls, the breath before a phrase, the slight hiss of Mylar wound on a plastic spool that cost forty cents wholesale in '79.

The two songs find each other. Jackie's left hand doubles Nick's riff an octave high, then lets go, and the Queen melody rises up out of the piano the way a river carries a leaf. The stumble at the twelfth fret. The crushed note. The crack that lets the light in. The hard rock section — both of them digging in, thirty seconds of controlled fury, the kind of fury that is actually tenderness with nowhere else to go. Two beats of silence.

The final chord. D minor. Sustain pedal down. The note rings and rings and fades.

Four minutes and forty-three seconds. The reel turns on into leader. I stop the machine. The spindle clicks.

The booth is quiet in a way no booth has ever been quiet in this building.

---

I lean into the mic one more time.

"This is Maxanne Sartori, signing off."

A beat. The clock on the wall reads 11:59.

"The music was real. Goodnight, Boston."

The ON AIR sign dies at midnight. Not a second off. Forty-one years of red light and then no red light. The booth drops to emergency fluorescent, the color of old newsprint, and the absence of the red is louder than anything I ever played through this microphone.

Transmitter silence. Not quiet. Empty. The space where something used to be.

Twelve-oh-one. Static, then smooth. A man's voice mixed too hot, processed through reverb, the audio equivalent of a smile with nothing behind it.

*You're listening to JOY FM, bringing you the good news in music and ministry.*

A synthesizer choir fills the booth where Studio B used to live. I laugh once, sharp, half a sob, and the acoustic foam eats it.

---

Kevin opens the booth door. He stands in it. He does not speak. I understand we are not going to speak.

I leave the reel on the playback machine. Someone from JOY FM can inherit it with the rest of the ghosts. I pick up the cassette off the console and put it back in my purse where it has lived. I leave the Pink Floyd cart where it sits, honor guard for a suite that did not get its airtime.

The hallway's moving boxes have not moved. The lobby is darker than when I came in. Outside, August in Boston, asphalt and harbor, the antenna tower dark against the sky for the first time in forty-one years.

My Volvo wagon is the only car in the lot. Twelve years old. I get in and close the door and the silence wraps around me the way Hop Brook wraps around a stone.

My phone buzzes.

A text from Sophie. My niece. Nineteen. Emerson. Thinks radio is what old people did before podcasts.

*Aunt Max did you play that song?? Someone recorded it someone put it on YouTube.*

A second text, already landing.

*It is BLOWING UP.*

I open the laptop on the passenger seat. The WBCN WiFi still works because nobody's thought to kill it yet. YouTube loads in the blue glow.

TWO GUYS WHO SHOULD HAVE BEEN FAMOUS — WBCN FINAL BROADCAST. Posted fourteen minutes ago by BostonRockRIP. Thumbnail of the antenna tower in Impact font.

50,000 views.

I refresh. 78,000.

The comments scroll past the way the request line used to scroll at eleven on a Friday night.

*1979??? This sounds like it could be today.*

*Who are these kids and how do I find them.*

*Sitting in my car in the parking lot of a CVS in Quincy crying.*

*Aerosmith plus Queen = this. How is this not a record.*

*Whoever played this on her last broadcast is a legend.*

*The Neighborhoods opened for the Cars at the Rat in '79 and these guys never even made the card. This is criminal.*

*RIP WBCN. Thank you Maxanne.*

I refresh. 140,000. 212,000. Twelve-thirty, three hundred thousand. The number climbs like something alive.

My phone rings. 212. New York. I don't answer. It rings again. 617. I don't answer. 310. Los Angeles. The calls stack up the way planes stack over Logan in weather. A voicemail from a producer at NPR. A voicemail from a writer at Pitchfork. A text from Carter Alan: *Maxanne what did you do.* A text from a number I don't know: *do you know where they are.*

One million views at 1:14 AM.

I am crying. Not sadness, not happiness. Some older thing my face knew before I did.

I close the laptop. The car goes black. I sit there in the Volvo in the empty lot with my hands on the wheel and I let myself think what I have not let myself think for thirty years.

---

Somewhere in a practice room on Mass Ave, on a Wednesday afternoon a month from now, a man in his late forties will be sitting at a Steinway Model B. Not his own Steinway. His office feels wrong today and he has been drifting through practice rooms since noon, the way he has been drifting through them more and more often this year without quite admitting to himself that he is. The teaching day is mostly over. His last two students canceled — one by email, one by not showing up. The room is provisionally his, the way an academic room is provisionally anyone's: forty minutes, then somebody else's. The lid is open on the full stick. He is not playing. He has not played seriously for himself in years. He plays *for* students now — demonstrates voice leading at the keyboard, voices a chord to make a point, closes the lid at the hour.

He is sitting with his hands resting on his thighs. His back is straight. His feet are flat under the pedals. It is the shape his body settled into one November afternoon in a radio studio on Boylston Street thirty years ago, when he had stood in a doorway and looked at a wall of seven-inch reels and said he had a train to catch — and it is a shape his body has been returning to more and more these last years, Wednesdays like this one, hands quiet on the wool of his slacks, the piano warm and unplayed in front of him. He has not yet noticed. He will notice, someday.

Someone will come to the door with a laptop. A colleague, maybe, or a kid from the theory class — somebody young enough to have found it first. They will say, you have to hear this. He will not move at first. He will listen all the way through, four minutes and forty-three seconds, with his hands in his lap and his eyes closed. And when it ends his right hand will lift from his thigh. It will not lift because he lifts it. It will lift the way a hand lifts from a table when someone calls you from the next room and you are already rising before you have decided. It will move across the air over the keys and come down on the D above middle C without pressing — the pad of the finger resting on the ivory, the key not moving, the string not sounding. A beat. Two beats. Then his left hand will come up to meet it. The weight will come down. The D minor chord will sound, just the chord, just once — and he will nod, very slightly, the way people nod when they finally hear somebody say out loud the thing they had decided they were alone with.

I don't know him. I have never met him. I am making him up in a Volvo in an empty parking lot at one-twenty in the morning because I need to. He might be out there. He might not. The imagining is what I have.

I turn the key. The Volvo catches on the second try. I drive out onto the Pike heading west, toward Cambridge, toward home, and I leave the radio off.

**END CHAPTER 34**
